The Asymptotic Analysis of Some PDE and Steklov Eigenvalue Problems with Partially Reactive Patches in 3-D
Denis S. Grebenkov, Michael J. Ward

Abstract
We consider steady-state diffusion in a three-dimensional bounded domain with a smooth reflecting boundary that is partially covered by small partially reactive patches. By using the method of matched asymptotic expansions, we investigate the competition of these patches for a diffusing particle and the crucial role of surface reactions on these targets. After a brief overview of former contributions to this field, we first illustrate our approach by considering the classical problems of the mean first-reaction time (MFRT) and the splitting probability for partially reactive patches characterized by a Robin boundary condition. For a spherical domain, we derive a three-term asymptotic expansion for the MFRT and splitting probabilities in the small-patch limit. This expansion is valid for arbitrary reactivities, and also accounts for the effect of the spatial configuration of patches on the boundary. Secondly, we consider more intricate surface reactions modeled by mixed Steklov-Neumann or Steklov-Neumann-Dirichlet problems. We provide the first derivation of the asymptotic behavior of the eigenvalues and eigenfunctions for these spectral problems in the small-patch limit for a spherical domain. Extensions of these asymptotic results to arbitrary domains and their physical applications are discussed.



1
Introduction

Many vital processes in microbiology rely on diffusive search
for small targets, such as proteins searching for their partners or
specific sites on a DNA chain, ions searching for channels on the
plasma membrane of the cell, viruses searching for nuclear pores, etc.

[
1
,
81
,
18
,
71
,
60
]
. In
heterogeneous catalysis, reactants move toward active sites on a
solid catalytic surface to be chemically transformed

[
73
,
86
,
64
]
. In nuclear magnetic resonance
experiments, spin-bearing molecules diffuse in tissues or mineral
samples and may relax their magnetization on magnetic impurities that
are located on confining walls

[
21
,
46
,
77
]
. Various search problems on a
macroscopic scale such as animal or human behavior are inspired by
ecology
[
79
,
60
]
. These and many other natural
phenomena are often modeled by reflected Brownian motion that is
confined inside a bounded domain by an impenetrable boundary. This
stochastic process is stopped (or killed) under certain conditions
that represent interactions of the diffusing particle with prescribed
regions (called “targets”) that are located either inside the domain
or on its boundary. Depending on the context and application, such
interactions may represent a chemical reaction, a binding, a
conformational change to a different state, a relaxation of
magnetization or fluorescence, an escape from the domain, etc. One of
the simplest and most studied stopping condition is the first arrival
onto the targets. In this scenario, the efficiency of the diffusive
search is usually characterized by the distribution of the
first-passage time (FPT) to single or multiple targets

[
102
,
108
,
94
,
92
,
37
,
60
]
.

From a mathematical viewpoint, many former works have been dedicated
to the analysis of the
mean
first-passage time (MFPT) and its
dependence on the shape, size, and spatial arrangement of the targets
in the confining domain. If
{\bf X}
denotes the starting point of
reflected Brownian motion in a bounded domain
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d}

with a smooth boundary
{\partial\Omega}
, the MFPT
T({\bf X})
to a target region

{\partial\Omega}_{a}\subset{\partial\Omega}
satisfies the Poisson equation with mixed
Dirichlet-Neumann boundary condition:

\displaystyle-D\Delta T({\bf X})

\displaystyle=1\,,\quad{\bf X}\in\Omega\,,

(1.1a)

\displaystyle T({\bf X})

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf X}\in{\partial\Omega}_{a}\,,

(1.1b)

\displaystyle\partial_{n}T({\bf X})

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf X}\in{\partial\Omega}_{r}={\partial\Omega}\backslash{\partial\Omega}_{a}\,,

(1.1c)

where
D>0
is a constant diffusion coefficient,
\Delta
is the
Laplace operator, and
\partial_{n}
is the outward normal derivative.
The Dirichlet condition on
{\partial\Omega}_{a}
characterizes the first-arrival
stopping condition (if the particle starts from
{\partial\Omega}_{a}
, the process
is immediately stopped, yielding
T=0
), whereas the Neumann
condition on
{\partial\Omega}_{r}
ensures zero flux across the reflecting part of
the boundary. The mixed boundary condition presents the major
challenge in solving this boundary value problem (BVP) in general
domains, in which a trivial separation of variables, such as in
rectangles or concentric spheres, is not applicable. Despite the
existence of advanced techniques such as dual integral or series
equations
[
115
]
, or the generalized method of separation of
variables
[
62
,
49
]
, the disk with an
arc-shaped target is presumably the only nontrivial example for which
an exact explicit solution of (
1.1
) has been found

[
111
,
107
,
47
,
91
]
.

For this reason, various approximate, numerical and asymptotic
techniques have been developed over the past two decades. When the
target
{\partial\Omega}_{a}
is small, determining the asymptotic behavior of the
MFPT is usually referred to as
the narrow escape problem
(see an
overview in
[
72
]
). The presence of a small Dirichlet
patch
{\partial\Omega}_{a}
on an otherwise reflecting boundary is a
singular

perturbation
[
97
,
93
,
119
]
. In fact, if
{\partial\Omega}_{a}
was
absent, the MFPT would be infinite. As a consequence, the solution of
(
1.1
) diverges in the small-patch limit
{\partial\Omega}_{a}\to\emptyset
. The very first asymptotic result for the MFPT to a
small circular patch of radius
\epsilon
on a spherical boundary of
radius
R
dates back to Lord Rayleigh, who found (in the context of
acoustics) that, to leading-order in
\epsilon
,
T\sim|\Omega|/(4D\epsilon)
, where
|\Omega|=4\pi R^{3}/3
is the volume of
the spherical domain
[
3
]
. Even though the particle
escapes through the two-dimensional boundary patch, the MFPT scales
inversely with the radius of the patch (
\sim\epsilon
) and not with
its area (
\sim\epsilon^{2}
). This is a characteristic feature of a
perfectly reactive circular patch with Dirichlet condition, also known
as the diffusion-limited regime in diffusion-controlled reactions

[
96
,
121
,
20
,
10
,
106
,
64
]
,
and this behavior is typical for Dirichlet patches of arbitrary
shape. However, as we will discuss below, an additional scaling regime
emerges for more sophisticated reaction mechanisms. Rayleigh’s
leading-order result has been generalized to other domains and patch
shapes, both in two and three dimensions, and the asymptotic results
have been applied to many specific problems in microbiology (see

[
69
,
114
,
111
,
112
,
109
,
70
,
113
]
and the overviews in
[
71
,
72
]
).

However, to obtain more refined asymptotic results, the method of
matched asymptotic expansions

[
80
]
, as tailored in

[
119
]
and
[
118
]
to analyze PDE problems with strong
localized perturbations, has been implemented to systematically
calculate higher-order asymptotic approximations of the MFPT, the
splitting probability, and related first passage quantities in both
two- and three-dimensional settings with either boundary patches or
interior targets
(
[
35
,
30
,
99
,
29
,
26
,
31
,
38
,
13
,
14
,
74
]
).
Some asymptotic results for related problems on unbounded domains are
given in
[
87
,
89
,
83
]
(see also the references
therein). An alternative approach to the method of matched asymptotics
was described in
[
28
]
. Even though our discussion is
focused on ordinary diffusion, extensions to more sophisticated
diffusion processes, and to problems with stochastic resetting, have
been explored (see

[
34
,
5
,
17
,
66
,
12
]
and the
references therein). We also emphasize that the knowledge of the
mean
FPT does not fully characterize the distribution of this
random variable
[
44
,
58
]
. Analytical and numerical
studies for obtaining the full probability distribution include

[
107
,
59
,
105
,
88
,
27
]
(see the
references therein).

The above stopping condition corresponds to the simplest reaction
kinetics when the reaction event occurs certainly and instantly upon
the first arrival onto the target. This perfect reaction scenario
assumes that the diffusive search is the only limiting factor and thus
oversimplifies reaction kinetics that is relevant in many applications

[
98
,
64
]
. Indeed, a particle that arrives onto
the target may not react instantly due to various reasons: (i) a
reaction event often requires overcoming an activation energy barrier

[
120
,
67
]
; (ii) an escape event may involve overcoming
an entropic barrier
[
123
,
103
,
25
]
; (iii) a
macromolecule may need to be in the proper conformational state to
bind its partner
[
36
,
42
,
90
]
; (iv) the target
may switch between active and passive states (e.g., an ion channel can
be open or closed)
[
4
,
104
,
82
]
; (v) the
target may be microscopically inhomogeneous so that the arrival point
may be inert

[
9
,
7
,
8
,
95
,
87
,
11
,
101
]
.
Whatever the microscopic origin is, such
imperfect
targets,
generally referred to as
partially reactive
, are often modeled
by a Robin boundary condition
[
33
]
. For instance, the BVP
(
1.1
) is replaced by

\displaystyle-D\Delta{\mathcal{T}}({\bf X})

\displaystyle=1\,,\quad{\bf X}\in\Omega\,,

(1.2a)

\displaystyle D\partial_{n}{\mathcal{T}}+{\mathcal{K}}{\mathcal{T}}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf X}\in{\partial\Omega}_{a}\,,

(1.2b)

\displaystyle\partial_{n}{\mathcal{T}}({\bf X})

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf X}\in{\partial\Omega}_{r}={\partial\Omega}\backslash{\partial\Omega}_{a}\,,

(1.2c)

with the mixed Robin-Neumann boundary condition. The reactivity
{\mathcal{K}}
,
which has units of length per time, characterizes the facility of the
reaction event, by ranging from
0
(inert passive target, no
reaction) to
+\infty
(perfect reaction upon the first arrival). If
the particle that arrives onto the target fails to react, it is
reflected from the target and resumes its diffusion in the domain
until the next arrival, and so on. As a consequence, the successful
reaction event is generally preceded by a sequence of diffusive
excursions in the bulk after each failed reaction attempt, and the
mean
first-reaction time
(MFRT),
{\mathcal{T}}({\bf X})
, satisfying
(
1.2
), can significantly exceed the MFPT
T({\bf X})
satisfying
(
1.1
). The probabilistic interpretation of the Robin
boundary condition and the relation of the reactivity
{\mathcal{K}}
to the
probability of the reaction event upon each attempt were provided in

[
51
]
.

The effect of partial reactivity on the efficiency of the diffusive
search, which was ignored in most former studies, has recently
attracted considerable attention. For instance, the small-patch
asymptotic behavior of the MFRT was deduced in

[
61
]
by using a constant-flux approximation. In
particular, for a circular patch on a spherical boundary, the MFRT was
shown to behave as
|\Omega|/({\mathcal{K}}|{\partial\Omega}_{a}|)
in the leading order in

\epsilon
, where
|{\partial\Omega}_{a}|=\pi\epsilon^{2}
is the surface area of
the patch. The faster divergence
{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-2})
is reminiscent
to the reaction-limited rate, which becomes dominant in the
small-patch limit for a finite reactivity
{\mathcal{K}}
. The change of scaling
between diffusion-limited and reaction-limited regimes suggests a
nontrivial dependence on the reactivity, especially in the limit

{\mathcal{K}}\to\infty
. In fact, the limits
{\mathcal{K}}\to\infty
and

\epsilon\to 0
are not interchangeable. This point was further
investigated in
[
65
,
22
,
56
]
, where the
behavior of the MFRT on a small circular patch was inspected for
small, intermediate, and large reactivities by different methods. The
effect of spatially heterogeneous reactivity was analyzed via a
spectral approach
[
48
]
, whereas the role of target
anisotropy onto the MFRT was studied in
[
23
]
. The
trapping rate of a reflecting plane covered by partially reactive
circular patches was estimated by means of boundary homogenization
technique
[
100
]
.

Despite this recent progress, many open questions about partially
reactive patches in 3-D remain unsolved. Some key open questions
include the following:

(i) Most aforementioned works have dealt with a single patch. Can we
develop an asymptotic theory that incorporates the effect of multiple
patches that compete with each other for capturing diffusing particles
(the so-called diffusional screening
[
40
]
or diffusive
interactions
[
116
,
117
,
6
]
)?

(ii) Many former works focused on the leading-order term, which scales
as
\epsilon^{-2}
. However, the expected “correction” terms

{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-1})
,
{\mathcal{O}}(\log\epsilon)
and
{\mathcal{O}}(1)
may provide
significant contributions, and their knowledge is required for an
accurate estimation of the MFRT in applications, especially if

\epsilon
is not too small. Can we develop a systematic approach to
capture these higher-order terms?

(iii) To our knowledge, all previous analyses on partially reactive
patches have assumed their
circular
shape. How does the shape
of the patch affect the asymptotic behavior of the MFRT?

(iv) What is the impact of the spatial arrangement of patches? This
question was addressed for perfectly reactive patches but remains open
for partially reactive ones.

(v) Even though the use of the MFRT is a common way to characterize
the efficiency of the diffusive search, other quantities may be needed
to reveal versatile facets of this phenomenon. For instance, one
often employs the splitting probabilities to describe the efficiency
of individual patches in their competition for capturing the diffusing
particles. What is the asymptotic behavior of the splitting
probabilities of partially reactive patches?

(vi) Finally, the Robin boundary condition (
1.2b
)
implements the simplest model of a constant reactivity on the patch.
The encounter-based approach
[
51
]
allows one to
introduce a much more general class of surface reactions that
describe, e.g., progressive activation or de-activation of the patch
by its interaction with diffusing particles, non-Markovian binding,
surface adsorption,
etc.
[
53
,
15
,
16
]
. In probabilistic
terms, one imposes a more general stopping condition that involves the
boundary local time, i.e., a proxy of the number of encounters of the
particle with the patch
[
50
,
52
]
. In turn, the
PDE formulation of this framework substitutes the Robin boundary
condition by an integral equation on the patch. A natural framework
for solving and analyzing such PDEs relies on the Steklov-Neumann
problem
[
56
]
or the Steklov-Dirichlet-Neumann problem

[
54
]
(see §
2
for their formulation).
These are basic extensions of the conventional Steklov spectral
problem that has been thoroughly studied in spectral geometry

[
84
,
43
,
32
]
. To characterize the
efficiency of multiple small patches with more sophisticated reaction
mechanisms, can we analyze the asymptotic behavior of the eigenvalues
and eigenfunctions of the related Steklov problems in the small-patch
limit? To our knowledge, this asymptotic problem was not addressed in
the past (except for
[
56
]
that focused on a single
circular patch).

In this paper, we aim at answering all these questions. For this
purpose, we combine the method of matched asymptotic expansions, based
on strong localized perturbation theory
[
119
]
, with spectral
expansions based on the local exterior Steklov problem on each patch.
The use of geodesic normal coordinates is another key tool to access
higher-order terms in the asymptotic expansions. In the next section,
we formulate four asymptotic problems and summarize our main results.

2
Summary of main results

We consider reflected Brownian motion in a three-dimensional bounded
domain
\Omega
, with a smooth boundary
{\partial\Omega}
, which consists of the
union
\partial\Omega_{a}=\cup_{i=1}^{N}\partial\Omega_{i}
of
N

reactive patches
\partial\Omega_{i}
and the remaining reflecting
(inert) boundary
\partial\Omega_{r}
. Each reactive boundary
patch
\partial\Omega_{i}
of diameter
2L_{i}
is assumed to be
simply-connected with a smooth boundary, but with an otherwise
arbitrary shape. Our asymptotic analysis will exploit an assumed
length-scale separation
{L/R}\ll 1
, where
L\equiv\max\limits_{i}\{L_{i}\}
, and
2R
is the diameter of the confining domain

\Omega
. The patches are assumed to be well-separated in the sense
that

\mathrm{dist}\{{\partial\Omega}_{i},{\partial\Omega}_{j}\}\gg L
for all
i\neq j
. We
will study four different problems that can be analyzed with a common
theoretical framework. For this reason, we will employ the same
notations, e.g.,
U({\bf X})
, for formulating and studying these
problems.

(I) The mean first-reaction time,
{\mathcal{T}}({\bf X})=U({\bf X})
, on the union

{\partial\Omega}_{a}
of partially reactive patches

\partial\Omega_{1},\ldots,\partial\Omega_{N}
with finite reactivities

{\mathcal{K}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{K}}_{N}
, satisfies a Poisson equation with mixed
Neumann-Robin boundary conditions, re-formulated from (
1.2
)
as

\displaystyle\Delta U

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{D}\,,\quad{\bf X}\in\Omega\,,

(2.3a)

\displaystyle D\partial_{n}U+{\mathcal{K}}_{i}U

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf X}\in\partial\Omega_{i}\,,\quad i=1,\ldots,N\,,

(2.3b)

\displaystyle\partial_{n}U

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf X}\in\partial\Omega_{r}\,.

(2.3c)

We also consider the volume-averaged MFRT,

\overline{U}\equiv\frac{1}{|\Omega|}\int_{\Omega}U({\bf X})\,d{\bf X}\,,

(2.4)

which corresponds to the average with respect to a uniform
distribution of initial points
{\bf X}\in\Omega
, where
|\Omega|

denotes the volume of
\Omega
.

(II) The splitting probability,
U({\bf X})
, to react on the first patch

\partial\Omega_{1}
, before reacting on the other patches, satisfies

\displaystyle\Delta U

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf X}\in\Omega\,,

(2.5a)

\displaystyle D\partial_{n}U+{\mathcal{K}}_{i}U

\displaystyle=\delta_{i1}{\mathcal{K}}_{i}\,,\quad{\bf X}\in\partial\Omega_{i}\,,\quad i=1,\ldots,N\,,

(2.5b)

\displaystyle\partial_{n}U

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf X}\in\partial\Omega_{r}\,,

(2.5c)

where
\delta_{11}=1
and
\delta_{i1}=0
for
i=2,\ldots,N
.
The volume-averaged splitting probability is also given by
(
2.4
).

(III) More sophisticated surface reactions can be formulated in terms
of various Steklov spectral problems
(cf.
[
51
,
52
,
53
,
54
,
55
,
56
]
).
One such problem consists of finding the eigenpairs
\{\Sigma,U\}

of the mixed Steklov-Dirichlet-Neumann (SDN) problem formulated as

\displaystyle\Delta U

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf X}\in\Omega\,,

(2.6a)

\displaystyle\partial_{n}U

\displaystyle=\Sigma U\,,\quad{\bf X}\in\partial\Omega_{1}\,,

(2.6b)

\displaystyle U

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf X}\in\partial\Omega_{i}\,,\quad i=2,\ldots,N\,,

(2.6c)

\displaystyle\partial_{n}U

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf X}\in\partial\Omega_{r}.

(2.6d)

These eigenpairs allow one to solve the escape problem when the
diffusing particle has to react on the patch
\partial\Omega_{1}

before escaping from the domain
\Omega
through any Dirichlet patch

\partial\Omega_{j}
, for
j=2,\ldots,N
, which may represent holes or
channels on the domain boundary
[
54
]
.

(IV) Finally, we will consider the mixed Steklov-Neumann (SN) problem
that consists of finding the eigenpairs
\{\Sigma,U\}
satisfying

\displaystyle\Delta U

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf X}\in\Omega\,,

(2.7a)

\displaystyle\partial_{n}U

\displaystyle=\Sigma U\,,\quad{\bf X}\in\partial\Omega_{i}\,,\quad i=1,\ldots,N\,,

(2.7b)

\displaystyle\partial_{n}U

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf X}\in\partial\Omega_{r}\,.

(2.7c)

These eigenpairs can be used to investigate sophisticated
surface reactions on multiple patches and their competition. The
general spectral properties of mixed Steklov-Neumann problems,
known as sloshing or ice-fishing problems in hydrodynamics, were
studied previously in
[
68
,
41
,
78
,
85
]
(see
also the references therein).

For each of these four problems we focus on the spherical domain

\Omega=\{{\bf X}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\,|\,|{\bf X}|\leq R\}
in order to derive
the three-term asymptotic behavior in the limit
{\varepsilon}={L/R}\ll 1
. We
emphasize that the leading-order terms in our derived asymptotic
results do not depend on the geometry of the confining domain and are
thus valid for an arbitrary domain with a smooth boundary. However,
our emphasis is on calculating the higher-order terms in the
asymptotic expansions, which are often relevant for applications, and
are needed for determining the effect of the location of the patches
on the surface and for deriving a homogenization result for the MFRT.
Although the methodology for deriving the three-term expansions can
potentially be extended to arbitrary 3-D domains, we will restrict our
analysis to the sphere, where the surface Neumann Green’s function is
available analytically.

For our analysis, it is convenient to reformulate the four problems in
terms of dimensionless variables defined by

{\bf{x}}=\frac{{\bf X}}{R}\,,\quad L=\max\limits_{i}L_{i}\,,\quad{\varepsilon}=\frac{L}{R}\,,\quad a_{i}=\frac{L_{i}}{L}\,,\quad\kappa_{i}=\frac{L{\mathcal{K}}_{i}}{D}\,,\quad\sigma=\Sigma L\,.

(2.8)

Moreover, the dimensionless MFRT
u({\bf{x}})
will be expressed as

u({\bf{x}})=\frac{D}{R^{2}}U({\bf{x}}R)\,.

(2.9)

Such a rescaling of

U
is not needed for the splitting probability (which is already
dimensionless) nor for the Steklov eigenfunctions, which are
defined up to a suitable normalization.

In terms of the new variables (
2.8
) and
(
2.9
), the dimensionless MFRT
u({\bf{x}})
in the unit
sphere
\Omega
with partially reactive patches and dimensionless
reactivities
\kappa_{i}
satisfies

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\bf{x}}}u

\displaystyle=-1\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\Omega\,,

(2.10a)

\displaystyle{\varepsilon}\partial_{n}u+\kappa_{i}u

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\partial\Omega^{{\varepsilon}}_{i}\,,\quad i=1,\ldots,N\,,

(2.10b)

\displaystyle\partial_{n}u

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\partial\Omega_{r}=\partial\Omega\backslash\partial\Omega_{a}\,,

(2.10c)

where
\Delta_{{\bf{x}}}
is the Laplacian in
{\bf{x}}
, and
\partial_{n}
is
again the outward normal derivative to
\partial\Omega
. Each
reactive boundary patch
\partial\Omega^{{\varepsilon}}_{i}
, of small diameter

{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon})
, is assumed to be simply-connected with a smooth
boundary, but with an otherwise arbitrary shape, and satisfies

\partial\Omega^{{\varepsilon}}_{i}\to{\bf{x}}_{i}\in\partial\Omega
as
{\varepsilon}\to 0
.
The patches are assumed to be well-separated in the sense that

\left|{\bf{x}}_{i}-{\bf{x}}_{j}\right|={\mathcal{O}}(1)
for all
i\neq j
. With
respect to a uniform distribution of initial points
{\bf{x}}\in\Omega
for
the reflected Brownian motion, the dimensionless volume-averaged MFRT
is

\overline{u}\equiv\frac{1}{|\Omega|}\int_{\Omega}u({\bf{x}})\,d{\bf{x}}\,,

(2.11)

where
|\Omega|={4\pi/3}
is the volume of
\Omega
. The geometry of a
confining sphere with reactive patches on its boundary is depicted in
Fig.
1(a)
.

(a)

Brownian trajectory in unit sphere

(b)

Geodesic normal coordinates

Figure 1
:

(a): Sketch of a Brownian trajectory in the unit sphere in
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}

with partially reactive patches
{\partial\Omega}^{{\varepsilon}}_{1},\ldots,{\partial\Omega}^{{\varepsilon}}_{N}

on the boundary. (b): Geodesic normal coordinates

(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})^{T}
centered at
{\bf{x}}_{i}\in\partial\Omega
, with the
geodesics (orange and blue curves) indicated.

In the limit
{\varepsilon}\to 0
of small patches, in §
4

we will derive an asymptotic expansion for
u({\bf{x}})
and
\overline{u}

for arbitrary
\kappa_{i}>0
. The main result is summarized in Proposition

1
of §
4
. For the special
case where
\kappa_{i}=\infty
and when the patches are disks, such an
analysis has been performed in
[
30
]
by expanding
spherical coordinates near each patch. In §
4
we
will use a different and simpler approach than in
[
30
]

that relies on geodesic normal coordinates as introduced in §
3
, which allows us to more readily consider the
case of finite
\kappa_{i}
and arbitrary patch shapes. More explicit
results will be obtained when the patches are locally circular with
radii
{\varepsilon}a_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,N
. Further preliminary results that
are the central building blocks for our analysis of the MFRT and for
the other three problems are summarized in §
3
. As a byproduct of this analysis, we also
derive in §
4.4
a three-term expansion for the
principal (lowest) eigenvalue of the Laplace operator with mixed
Neumann-Robin boundary conditions.

In a similar way, in terms of (
2.8
), the splitting
probability
u({\bf{x}})
for a Brownian particle in the unit sphere

\Omega
to react on a specific target patch on the domain boundary,
labeled below by
\partial\Omega_{1}^{{\varepsilon}}
, before reacting on any of
the remaining
N-1
, with
N\geq 2
, other boundary patches satisfies

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\bf{x}}}u

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\Omega\,,

(2.12a)

\displaystyle{\varepsilon}\partial_{n}u+\kappa_{i}u

\displaystyle=\delta_{i1}\kappa_{i}\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\partial\Omega_{i}^{{\varepsilon}}\,,\quad i=1,\ldots,N\,,

(2.12b)

\displaystyle\partial_{n}u

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\partial\Omega_{r}=\partial\Omega\backslash\partial\Omega_{a}\,.

(2.12c)

Here
\delta_{11}=1
and
\delta_{i1}=0
for
i=2,\ldots,N
and
we have used the same notation and assumptions on the patches as for
the MFRT problem (
2.10
). The asymptotic analysis of
(
2.12
) is done in §
5
,
with our main result being summarized in Proposition

2
.

In terms of (
2.8
) the dimensionless SDN problem in
the unit sphere
\Omega
consists of finding the eigenpairs

\{\sigma,u\}
satisfying

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\bf{x}}}u

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\Omega\,,

(2.13a)

\displaystyle{\varepsilon}\partial_{n}u

\displaystyle=\sigma u\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\partial\Omega_{1}^{{\varepsilon}}\,,

(2.13b)

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\partial\Omega_{i}^{{\varepsilon}}\,,\quad i=2,\ldots,N\,,

(2.13c)

\displaystyle\partial_{n}u

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\partial\Omega_{r}.

(2.13d)

In the limit
{\varepsilon}\to 0
, this problem is analyzed in §
6
, with the main result summarized in Proposition

3
.

In turn, the dimensionless SN problem for (
2.7
)
consists of finding the eigenpairs
\{\sigma,u\}
satisfying

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\bf{x}}}u

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\Omega\,,

(2.14a)

\displaystyle{\varepsilon}\partial_{n}u

\displaystyle=\sigma u\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\partial\Omega_{i}^{{\varepsilon}}\,,\quad i=1,\ldots,N\,,

(2.14b)

\displaystyle\partial_{n}u

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\partial\Omega_{r}\,.

(2.14c)

This problem is studied in §
7
, with the main result
given in Proposition
4
. For a single circular patch,
the leading-order asymptotic behavior of the SN problem was thoroughly
analyzed in
[
56
]
. We will extend this previous analysis
for the Steklov eigenvalues by determining a three-term asymptotic
result that pertains to multiple well-separated, but arbitrary-shaped,
patches. Finally, in §
8
we discuss a few open
problems.

3
Preliminaries

We first derive some preliminary results that are central for our
asymptotic analysis as
{\varepsilon}\to 0
of the MFRT, the splitting
probability, and the Steklov eigenvalue problems. Our framework will
use strong localized perturbation theory
[
119
]
based on the
method of matched asymptotic expansions. To construct the local
expansion near each patch on
\partial\Omega
it is convenient to
introduce geodesic normal coordinates

{\bf{\xi}}=(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})^{T}\in\left({-\pi/2},{\pi/2}\right)\times\left(-\pi,\pi\right)\times[0,1]
in
\Omega\cup\partial\Omega
so
that
{\bf{\xi}}=0
corresponds to
{\bf{x}}_{i}\in\partial\Omega
, with
\xi_{3}>0

corresponding to the interior of
\Omega
. Here
\xi_{2}
can be viewed
as the polar angle of a spherical coordinate system centered at
{\bf{x}}_{i}

on the sphere, but defined on the range

\xi_{2}\in\left({-\pi/2},{\pi/2}\right)
that avoids the usual
coordinate singularity of spherical coordinates at the north pole.
The curves obtained by setting
\xi_{3}=0
and fixing either
\xi_{1}=0

or
\xi_{2}=0
are geodesics on
\partial\Omega
(see
Fig.
1(b)
).

In terms of the global transformation
{\bf{x}}={\bf{x}}({\bf{\xi}})
between cartesian
and geodesic coordinates, in (
A.249
) of Appendix

A
we derive an exact expression for the Laplacian of a
generic function
{\mathcal{V}}({\bf{\xi}})\equiv u\left({\bf{x}}({\bf{\xi}})\right)
. Then, by
introducing the inner, or local variables,
{\bf{y}}=(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3})^{T}
,
defined by

\xi_{1}={\varepsilon}y_{1}\,,\qquad\xi_{2}={\varepsilon}y_{2}\,,\qquad\xi_{3}={\varepsilon}y_{3}\,,

(3.15)

we derive in Appendix
A
that for
{\varepsilon}\to 0
, and with

V({\bf{y}})={\mathcal{V}}({\varepsilon}{\bf{y}})
and

\Delta_{{\bf{y}}}V\equiv V_{y_{1}y_{2}}+V_{y_{2}y_{2}}+V_{y_{3}y_{3}}
, we have

\Delta_{{\bf{x}}}u={\varepsilon}^{-2}\Delta_{{\bf{y}}}V+{\varepsilon}^{-1}\left[2y_{3}\left(V_{y_{1}y_{1}}+V_{y_{2}y_{2}}\right)-2V_{y_{3}}\right]+{\mathcal{O}}(1)\,.

(3.16)

This two-term inner expansion will be central in our asymptotic analysis.

The leading-order term in our local or inner expansion near
{\bf{x}}={\bf{x}}_{i}

relies on the canonical solution
w_{i}=w_{i}({\bf{y}};\kappa_{i})
satisfying

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\bf{y}}}w_{i}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf{y}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{3}\,,

(3.17a)

\displaystyle-\partial_{y_{3}}w_{i}+\kappa_{i}w_{i}

\displaystyle=\kappa_{i}\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\in{\Gamma}_{i}\,,

(3.17b)

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{3}}w_{i}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\notin{\Gamma}_{i}\,,

(3.17c)

\displaystyle w_{i}

\displaystyle\sim\frac{C_{i}(\kappa_{i})}{|{\bf{y}}|}+{\frac{{\bf p}_{i}(\kappa_{i}){\bf\cdot}{\bf{y}}}{|{\bf{y}}|^{3}}}+\cdots\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty\,,

(3.17d)

where the neglected term in (
3.17d
) is a
quadrupole. Here

{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{3}\equiv\{{{\bf{y}}=(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3})\,|\,\,y_{3}>0\,,\,-\infty<y_{1},y_{2}<\infty\}}

is the upper half-space, and
{\Gamma}_{i}\asymp{\varepsilon}^{-1}\partial\Omega^{{\varepsilon}}_{i}
is the compact flat Robin
patch on the horizontal plane
y_{3}=0
, obtained by
rescaling and flattening the small patch
{\partial\Omega}_{i}^{{\varepsilon}}
on the
spherical boundary. In (
3.17d
), the dipole vector

{\bf p}_{i}={\bf p}_{i}(\kappa_{i})
has the form
{\bf p}_{i}=(p_{1i},p_{2i},0)^{T}
to
ensure that the far-field behavior (
3.17d
) satisfies
(
3.17c
). When
{\Gamma}_{i}
is symmetric in
y_{1}
and
y_{2}
,
such as when
{\Gamma}_{i}
is a disk, we must have
p_{i1}=p_{i2}=0
by
symmetry, so that the dipole term in the far-field (
3.17d
)
vanishes identically.

3.1
Reactive capacitance

By using the divergence theorem over a large hemisphere, we readily
obtain the following identity for
C_{i}(\kappa_{i})
:

C_{i}(\kappa_{i})=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{{\Gamma}_{i}}q_{i}(y_{1},y_{2};\kappa_{i})\,dy_{1}dy_{2}\,,\quad\mbox{where}\quad q_{i}(y_{1},y_{2};\kappa_{i})\equiv-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{y_{3}}w_{i}|_{y_{3}=0}\,.

(3.18)

In analogy to electrostatics,
C_{i}(\kappa_{i})
can be interpreted as a

capacitance
of the partially reactive patch
{\Gamma}_{i}
with
reactivity
\kappa_{i}
, which we will refer to as the
reactive
capacitance
. In turn, we will refer to
q_{i}
as the
charge
density
. Although there is no explicit analytical solution to
(
3.17
) for arbitrary
\kappa_{i}
, in Appendix
D
we
establish a spectral representation (
D.302
) of
w_{i}
in
terms of eigenpairs of a suitable exterior
local
Steklov problem
(
D.294
), from which we deduce

C_{i}(\kappa_{i})=\frac{\kappa_{i}}{2\pi}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\mu_{ki}d_{ki}^{2}}{\mu_{ki}+\kappa_{i}}\,.

(3.19)

In (
3.19
),
\mu_{ki}>0
are the Steklov eigenvalues
that correspond to nontrivial spectral weights
d_{ki}\neq 0
defined
in (
D.301
). Both
\mu_{ki}
and
d_{ki}
depend on the shape of
the patch
{\Gamma}_{i}
. Although their numerical computation is required
for a given patch shape, the functional form of
C_{i}(\kappa_{i})
and
its dependence on reactivity
\kappa_{i}
is universal. Moreover, in
the important special case where all the patches are of the same shape
(but of variable size), such as a collection of disks, the rescaling
relations (
D.307
) imply that

C_{i}(\kappa_{i})=a_{i}{\mathcal{C}}(\kappa_{i}a_{i}),\qquad i=1,\ldots,N,

(3.20)

where
{\mathcal{C}}(\mu)
is the reactive capacitance of the rescaled patches

{\Gamma}_{i}/a_{i}
, which needs to be computed only once for a given patch
shape.

For an arbitrary patch shape, we readily calculate from (
3.19
)
that the derivative,

C_{i}^{\prime}(\kappa_{i})\equiv\frac{dC_{i}(\kappa_{i})}{d\kappa_{i}}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\mu_{ki}^{2}d_{ki}^{2}}{(\mu_{ki}+\kappa_{i})^{2}}>0\,,

(3.21)

is strictly positive for all
\kappa_{i}
(except for the simple
poles
\{-\mu_{ki}\}
), so that
C_{i}(\kappa_{i})
increases
monotonically between consecutive poles,
and on the positive semi-axis
\kappa_{i}>0
. Moreover, in the
small-reactivity limit
\kappa_{i}\to 0
, one can employ the
Taylor expansion

C_{i}(\kappa_{i})=-a_{i}\sum\limits_{n=1}^{\infty}c_{ni}\,(-\kappa_{i}a_{i})^{n}\,,

(3.22a)

where the coefficients

c_{ni}=\frac{1}{2\pi a_{i}^{n+1}}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{d_{ki}^{2}}{\mu_{ki}^{n-1}}

(3.22b)

are defined to be invariant under dilations of the patch. In
Appendix
D
, we show that

c_{1i}=\frac{|{\Gamma}_{i}|}{2\pi a_{i}^{2}}\,,\qquad c_{2i}=\frac{1}{2\pi a_{i}^{3}}\int\limits_{{\Gamma}_{i}}\omega_{i}({\bf{y}})\,d{\bf{y}}\,,\qquad c_{3i}=\frac{1}{2\pi a_{i}^{4}}\int\limits_{{\Gamma}_{i}}\omega_{i}^{2}({\bf{y}})\,d{\bf{y}}\,,

(3.23)

where
\omega_{i}({\bf{y}})
is defined by

\omega_{i}({\bf{y}})=\int\limits_{{\Gamma}_{i}}\frac{d{\bf{y}}^{\prime}}{2\pi|{\bf{y}}-{\bf{y}}^{\prime}|}\,,\qquad\mbox{for}\quad{\bf{y}}\in{\Gamma}_{i}\,.

(3.24)

To leading order the expansion (
3.22a
) yields

C_{i}(\kappa_{i})\sim\kappa_{i}\frac{|{\Gamma}_{i}|}{2\pi}\,,\qquad\mbox{as}\quad\kappa_{i}\to 0\,.

(3.25)

In the opposite high-reactivity limit,
C_{i}(\kappa_{i})
approaches the
capacitance
C_{i}(\infty)
of the patch
{\Gamma}_{i}
. After inspecting
the spectral representation (
3.19
), we propose the
following heuristic approximation over the entire range of
reactivities:

C_{i}(\kappa_{i})\approx C_{i}^{\rm app}(\kappa_{i})=\frac{\kappa_{i}C_{i}(\infty)}{\kappa_{i}+2\pi C_{i}(\infty)/|{\Gamma}_{i}|}\,,\qquad\mbox{for}\quad\kappa_{i}>0.

(3.26)

This sigmoidal approximation gives the correct limit as
\kappa_{i}\to\infty
and agrees with the leading-order term in the small-reactivity
limit
\kappa_{i}\to 0
(it is also close to the lower bound
(
D.339
) derived in Appendix
D.4
). However,
this approximation fails to recover the higher-order terms for

\kappa_{i}\ll 1
, and does not correctly reproduce the asymptotic
approach to
C_{i}(\infty)
(see (
3.33a
)). We remark
that a similar sigmoidal formula was developed for approximating the
principal eigenvalue of the Laplace operator
[
23
]
and
for studying the boundary local time distribution on small targets

[
52
]
.

Circular patch

When
{\Gamma}_{i}
is a disk of radius
a_{i}
, the limiting problem with

\kappa_{i}=\infty
in (
3.17
), for which
w_{i}=1
on
{\Gamma}_{i}
,
is the classical problem for the capacitance of a flat disk
(cf.
[
75
]
), whose solution labeled by
w_{i}({\bf{y}};\infty)
, is
(see page 38 of
[
39
]
)

w_{i}({\bf{y}};\infty)=\frac{2}{\pi}\sin^{-1}\left(\frac{a_{i}}{B(y_{3},\rho_{0})}\right)\,,

(3.27a)

where
\rho_{0}\equiv(y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2})^{1/2}
and

B(y_{3},\rho_{0})\equiv\frac{1}{2}\left(\left[(\rho_{0}+a_{i})^{2}+y_{3}^{2}\right]^{1/2}+\left[(\rho_{0}-a_{i})^{2}+y_{3}^{2}\right]^{1/2}\right)\,.

(3.27b)

From this solution, we obtain the far-field behavior

w_{i}({\bf{y}};\infty)\sim C_{i}(\infty)\left(\frac{1}{|{\bf{y}}|}+\frac{a_{i}^{2}}{6|{\bf{y}}|^{5}}\left(y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2}-2y_{3}^{2}\right)+\cdots\right)\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty\,,

(3.27c)

where
C_{i}(\infty)={2a_{i}/\pi}
is the electrostatic capacitance of the
circular disk of radius
a_{i}
(cf.
[
75
]
). Owing to the
symmetry of the disk, (
3.27c
) confirms that there is no
dipole term in the far-field. In addition, from (
3.27a
)
and the radial symmetry, we calculate

q_{i}(y_{1},y_{2};\infty)=q_{i}(\rho_{0};\infty)\equiv-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{y_{3}}w_{i}({\bf{y}};\infty)|_{y_{3}=0}=\frac{1}{\pi\sqrt{a_{i}^{2}-\rho_{0}^{2}}}\,,\quad 0\leq\rho_{0}\leq a_{i}\,,

(3.28)

which is needed in our analysis below. We conclude that, in the
large-reactivity limit, one has

C_{i}(\kappa_{i})\to C_{i}(\infty)=\frac{2a_{i}}{\pi}\,,\quad\textrm{as}\quad\kappa_{i}\to\infty\,.

(3.29)

However, owing to the edge singularity in (
3.28
) at

\rho_{0}=a_{i}
, the difference
C_{i}(\kappa_{i})-C_{i}(\infty)
is not
analytic for
\kappa_{i}\gg 1
. This difference has been estimated
analytically from an integral equation formulation in
[
65
]
,
and in our notation is given explicitly in (
D.322
) of
Appendix
D.3
(see also Fig.
10
and
the order estimate in (
3.33a
)).

Figure 2
:

The reactive capacitance
C_{i}(\kappa_{i})
for a circular patch
{\Gamma}_{i}

of unit radius (
a_{i}=1
), as computed from (
3.19
).
Filled circles presents the poles
\{-\mu_{ki}\}
, all located on the
negative axis, at which
C_{i}(\kappa_{i})
diverges. The dash-dotted
horizontal line indicates the asymptotic limit
C_{i}(\infty)=2/\pi
.

For a circular patch, one can use the oblate spheroidal
coordinates to efficiently solve the exterior local Steklov problem as
in
[
55
]
to compute
\mu_{ki}
and
d_{ki}
. Some of
these values are reported in Table
3
of Appendix

D
, whereas the function
C_{i}(\kappa_{i})
is shown in
Fig.
2
. As expected, this function increases
monotonically from
0
at
\kappa_{i}=0
to its limit

C_{i}(\infty)=2a_{i}/\pi
as
\kappa_{i}\to\infty
.
The asymptotic behavior of
C_{i}(\kappa_{i})
for
\kappa_{i}\ll 1

is given by (
3.22a
), in which the exact values of the
first three coefficients
c_{ni}
are determined in Appendix

B
(see also Appendix
D
) as

c_{1i}=\frac{1}{2}\,,\quad c_{2i}=\frac{4}{3\pi}\approx 0.4244\,,\quad c_{3i}=\frac{4}{\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}r\left[E(r)\right]^{2}\,dr\approx 0.3651\,,

(3.30)

where
E(r)
is the complete elliptic integral of the second kind. In
(
D.318
) of Appendix
D
we give a fully
explicit accurate approximation for all coefficients
c_{ni}
with
n\geq 2
.

(a)

Small
\kappa_{i}
comparison

(b)

Sigmoidal approximation

Figure 3
:

The reactive capacitance
C_{i}(\kappa_{i})
for the circular patch

{\Gamma}_{i}
of unit radius (
a_{i}=1
).
(a):
A comparison of

C_{i}(\kappa_{i})
numerically computed from (
3.19
), with
the one-, two-, and three-term approximations obtained from
(
3.22a
) and (
3.30
), valid for
\kappa_{i}\ll 1
.
(b):
The sigmoidal approximation (
3.31
)
provides a decent approximation of the numerical result for

C_{i}(\kappa_{i})
on the full range
\kappa_{i}>0
.

Figure
3(a)
shows that a three-term
small-reactivity series expansion of
C_{i}(\kappa_{i})
in
(
3.22a
), with the coefficients from (
3.30
),
provides a very close approximation for
C_{i}(\kappa_{i})
on
the range
0<\kappa_{i}a_{i}<0.45
. Finally, the heuristic
formula (
3.26
), when applied to a disk-shaped patch of
radius
a_{i}
, reads

C_{i}(\kappa_{i})\approx C_{i}^{\rm app}(\kappa_{i})=a_{i}{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm app}(\kappa_{i}a_{i})\,,\quad\mbox{where}\quad{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm app}(\mu)=\frac{2\mu}{\pi\mu+4}\,.

(3.31)

We verified numerically that (
3.31
) provides a good
approximation (see Fig.
3(b)
), with a
maximal relative error of
4\%
, over the entire range of

\kappa_{i}>0
. We summarize the asymptotic results above as
follows:

Lemma 1
.

When
{\Gamma}_{i}
is the disk
y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2}\leq a_{i}^{2}
, its
reactive capacitance is determined by (
3.19
), as well as
by

C_{i}(\kappa_{i})=2\int_{0}^{a_{i}}q_{i}(\rho_{0};\kappa_{i})\rho_{0}\,d\rho_{0}\,,\qquad q_{i}(\rho_{0};\kappa_{i})=-\frac{1}{2}w_{i,y_{3}}|_{y_{3}=0}\,,

(3.32)

where
w_{i}
is the solution to (
3.17
). It has the
asymptotics

\displaystyle C_{i}(\kappa_{i})

\displaystyle\sim C_{i}(\infty)+{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{\log\kappa_{i}}{\kappa_{i}}\right)\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad\kappa_{i}\to\infty\,,\quad\mbox{with}\quad C_{i}(\infty)=\frac{2a_{i}}{\pi}\,,

(3.33a)

\displaystyle C_{i}(\kappa_{i})

\displaystyle\sim a_{i}\biggl[c_{1i}\kappa_{i}a_{i}-c_{2i}(\kappa_{i}a_{i})^{2}+c_{3i}(\kappa_{i}a_{i})^{3}+{\mathcal{O}}((\kappa_{i}a_{i})^{4})\biggr]\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad\kappa_{i}\to 0\,,

(3.33b)

with the coefficients
c_{ni}
for
n=1,2,3
given by
(
3.30
). The error estimate in (
3.33a
)
follows from (
D.322
) of Appendix
D.3
.

3.2
Monopole from a Higher-Order Inner Solution

Our higher-order asymptotic analysis of each of our four problems
(
2.10
)–(
2.14
) also involves the monopole
coefficient
E_{i}=E_{i}(\kappa_{i})
, which is defined by the solution to
the following inhomogeneous inner problem (see Appendix

C
):

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\bf{y}}}\Phi_{2hi}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf{y}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{3}\,,

(3.34a)

\displaystyle-\partial_{y_{3}}\Phi_{2hi}+\kappa_{i}\Phi_{2hi}

\displaystyle=-\kappa_{i}{\mathcal{F}}_{i}\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\in{\Gamma}_{i}\,,

(3.34b)

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{3}}\Phi_{2hi}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\notin{\Gamma}_{i}\,,

(3.34c)

\displaystyle\Phi_{2hi}

\displaystyle\sim\frac{E_{i}}{|{\bf{y}}|}\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty\,,

(3.34d)

where
{\mathcal{F}}_{i}={\mathcal{F}}_{i}(y_{1},y_{2};\kappa_{i})
is the unique
solution to

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}_{i,y_{1}y_{1}}+{\mathcal{F}}_{i,y_{2}y_{2}}=q_{i}(y_{1},y_{2};\kappa_{i})I_{{\Gamma}_{i}}\,,\quad I_{{\Gamma}_{i}}\equiv\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1\,,&(y_{1},y_{2})\in{\Gamma}_{i}\\
0\,,&(y_{1},y_{2})\notin{\Gamma}_{i}\end{array}\right.\,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}_{i}\sim\frac{C_{i}}{2}\log\rho_{0}+o(1)\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad\rho_{0}\equiv(y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2})^{1/2}\to\infty\,.

(3.35c)

Here
C_{i}=C_{i}(\kappa_{i})
while the charge density

q_{i}(y_{1},y_{2};\kappa_{i})
is given in (
3.18
).

For an arbitrary patch shape, in Appendix
C
we show
that
E_{i}(\kappa_{i})
is determined by

E_{i}(\kappa_{i})=-\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}\int\limits_{{\Gamma}_{i}}\int\limits_{{\Gamma}_{i}}q_{i}({\bf{y}};\kappa_{i})\,q_{i}({\bf{y}}^{\prime};\kappa_{i})\,\log|{\bf{y}}-{\bf{y}}^{\prime}|\,d{\bf{y}}\,d{\bf{y}}^{\prime}\,.

(3.36)

In addition, in the limit
\kappa_{i}\to 0
, we derive in Appendix

C
that to leading order

E_{i}(\kappa_{i})\sim e_{i}C_{i}^{2}(\kappa_{i})\,,\quad\mbox{with}\quad e_{i}\equiv-\frac{1}{2|{\Gamma}_{i}|^{2}}\int\limits_{{\Gamma}_{i}}\int\limits_{{\Gamma}_{i}}\log|{\bf{y}}-{\bf{y}}^{\prime}|\,d{\bf{y}}\,d{\bf{y}}^{\prime}\,,

(3.37)

where
C_{i}(\kappa_{i})\sim{\kappa_{i}|{\Gamma}_{i}|/(2\pi)}
for
\kappa_{i}\ll 1
.

The next result, also proved in Appendix
C
,
characterizes
E_{i}
when
{\Gamma}_{i}
is a disk:

Lemma 2
.

When
{\Gamma}_{i}
is the disk
y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2}\leq a_{i}^{2}
, we have

E_{i}=E_{i}(\kappa_{i})=-\frac{\log{a_{i}}}{2}[C_{i}(\kappa_{i})]^{2}+a_{i}^{2}\,{\mathcal{E}}_{i}(\kappa_{i}a_{i})\,,

(3.38)

where
C_{i}(\kappa_{i})
is given by (
3.19
), and

{\mathcal{E}}_{i}(\mu)=2\int_{0}^{1}\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\left(\int_{0}^{\rho_{0}}a_{i}q_{i}(\eta a_{i};\mu/a_{i})\,\eta\,d\eta\right)^{2}\,d\rho_{0}

(3.39)

corresponds to the unit disk, with
q_{i}(\rho_{0};\kappa_{i})

as given in (
3.32
). The asymptotic behavior of
E_{i}(\kappa_{i})
is

\displaystyle E_{i}

\displaystyle\sim E_{i}(\infty)\equiv-\frac{2a_{i}^{2}}{\pi^{2}}\left(\log{a_{i}}+\log{4}-\frac{3}{2}\right)\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad\kappa_{i}\to\infty\,,

(3.40a)

\displaystyle E_{i}

\displaystyle\sim\frac{\kappa_{i}^{2}a_{i}^{4}}{8}\left(\frac{1}{4}-\log{a_{i}}\right)\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad\kappa_{i}\to 0\,.

(3.40b)

Although there is no explicit formula for
E_{i}(\kappa_{i})
for
arbitrary
\kappa_{i}>0
even when
{\Gamma}_{i}
is a disk, in Appendix

E
we show how it can be numerically computed to high
precision by expanding the charge density
q_{i}
in terms of Steklov
eigenfunctions. Moreover, when
{\Gamma}_{i}
is a disk, we provide the
heuristic approximation (see Appendix
E
)

E_{i}(\kappa_{i})\approx E_{i}^{\rm app}(\kappa_{i})=-\frac{a_{i}^{2}\log{a_{i}}}{2}\left[{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm app}(\kappa_{i}a_{i})\right]^{2}+a_{i}^{2}{\mathcal{E}}^{\rm app}(\kappa_{i}a_{i})\,,\qquad\kappa_{i}>0\,,

(3.41a)

where

{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm app}(\mu)
is given by the sigmoidal approximation
(
3.31
), and we define (see (
E.358
) of
Appendix
E
)

{\mathcal{E}}^{\rm app}(\mu)=\left[{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm app}(\mu)\right]^{2}\biggl(\frac{3}{4}-\log{2}+\frac{1}{\frac{1}{\log{2}-5/8}+5.17\,\mu^{0.81}}\biggr)\,.

(3.41b)

In Fig.
4
we show that, over the full range

\kappa_{i}>0
, (
3.41a
) agrees remarkably well,
with only a maximal relative error of
0.7\%
, with corresponding
numerical results as computed using the methodology described in
Appendix
E
. This heuristic approximation also ensures that
the required limits from (
3.40
) as
\kappa_{i}\to 0

and
\kappa_{i}\to\infty
hold.

Figure 4
:

For a circular patch of radius
a_{i}=1
, the heuristic
approximation
{\mathcal{E}}^{\rm app}(\kappa_{i})
(solid line) from
(
3.41b
) is compared with
E_{i}(\kappa_{i})={\mathcal{E}}_{i}(\kappa_{i})
(filled circles) given by (
3.38
) and
computed via the numerical approach in Appendix
E
. The
dashed horizontal line is the asymptotic limiting value
(3-4\log 2)/\pi^{2}
consistent with (
3.40a
).

3.3
The Surface Neumann Green’s Function

The asymptotic solutions at each order in the
outer region, defined at
{\mathcal{O}}(1)
distances from the surface
patches, is represented in terms of the surface Neumann Green’s
function
G_{s}({\bf{x}};{\bf{x}}_{i})
for the unit sphere
\Omega
, which is the
unique solution to

\Delta_{{\bf{x}}}G_{s}=\frac{1}{|\Omega|}\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\Omega\,;\quad\partial_{n}G_{s}=\delta({\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i})\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\partial\Omega\,;\quad\int_{\Omega}G_{s}\,d{\bf{x}}=0\,,

(3.42)

with
|{\bf{x}}_{i}|=1
. The exact solution to (
3.42
), as derived
in Appendix A of
[
30
]
, is

G_{s}({\bf{x}};{\bf{x}}_{i})=\frac{1}{2\pi\left|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i}\right|}+\frac{|{\bf{x}}|^{2}+1}{8\pi}+\frac{1}{4\pi}\log\left(\frac{2}{1-{\bf{x}}{\bf\cdot}{\bf{x}}_{i}+\left|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i}\right|}\right)-\frac{7}{10\pi}\,.

(3.43)

The following result characterizes the local behavior of
G_{s}
as

{\bf{x}}\to{\bf{x}}_{i}
in terms of the local geodesic coordinates
{\bf{y}}
defined in
(
3.15
).

Lemma 3
.

As
{\bf{x}}\to{\bf{x}}_{i}
with
|{\bf{x}}_{i}|=1
, we have

G_{s}({\bf{x}};{\bf{x}}_{i})\sim\frac{1}{2\pi|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i}|}-\frac{1}{4\pi}\log\bigl(|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i}|+1-|{\bf{x}}|\bigr)+\frac{\log{2}}{4\pi}-\frac{9}{20\pi}+o(1)\,.

(3.44)

In terms of the local geodesic coordinates

{\bf{y}}={\varepsilon}^{-1}{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}^{T}({\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i})
, where
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}
is the
orthogonal matrix from (
A.255
) of Appendix
A
, we
have that

G_{s}\sim\frac{1}{2\pi{\varepsilon}|{\bf{y}}|}-\frac{1}{4\pi}\log\left(\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\right)+\frac{y_{3}(y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2})}{4\pi|{\bf{y}}|^{3}}-\frac{1}{4\pi}\log(|{\bf{y}}|+y_{3})-\frac{9}{20\pi}+o(1)\,.

(3.45)

Proof.

We use the law of cosines with
|{\bf{x}}_{i}|=1
to get
2{\bf{x}}{\bf\cdot}{\bf{x}}_{i}=|{\bf{x}}|^{2}+1-|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i}|^{2}
, so that (
3.43
) becomes

G_{s}({\bf{x}};{\bf{x}}_{i})=\frac{1}{2\pi\left|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i}\right|}+\frac{|{\bf{x}}|^{2}+1}{8\pi}+\frac{1}{4\pi}\log\left(\frac{4}{\left(|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i}|+1\right)^{2}-|{\bf{x}}|^{2}}\right)-\frac{7}{10\pi}\,.

(3.46)

Upon using
a^{2}-b^{2}=(a-b)(a+b)
, we let
{\bf{x}}\to{\bf{x}}_{i}
with
|{\bf{x}}_{i}|=1
to
obtain that (
3.46
) reduces to (
3.44
).
Finally, as
{\bf{x}}\to{\bf{x}}_{i}
we use
1-|{\bf{x}}|\sim{\varepsilon}y_{3}
in (
3.44
),
together with (
A.254
) of Appendix
A
, to reduce
(
3.44
) to (
3.45
).

4
The Mean First-Reaction Time

In this section, we investigate the MFRT in the small-patch
limit and derive its three-term expansion, which is valid for
arbitrary reactivities. We also discuss its asymptotic behavior for
fixed reactivities, as well as the homogenization of the spherical
boundary. The asymptotic tools developed in this section will be
applied for solving the three other problems in the sections below.

We use the method of matched asymptotic expansions to construct
solutions to (
2.10
) in the limit
{\varepsilon}\to 0
. In the outer
region away from the Robin patches we expand the outer solution as

u\sim{\varepsilon}^{-1}U_{0}+U_{1}+{\varepsilon}\log\left(\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\right)U_{2}+{\varepsilon}U_{3}+\cdots\,,

(4.47)

where
U_{0}
is a constant to be determined and where
U_{k}
for
k\geq 1

satisfies

\Delta_{{\bf{x}}}U_{k}=-\delta_{k1}\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\Omega\,;\quad\partial_{n}U_{k}=0\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\partial\Omega\backslash\{{{\bf{x}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{x}}_{N}\}}\,.

(4.48)

Here
\delta_{k1}=1
if
k=1
and
\delta_{k1}=0
for
k>1
. Our
asymptotic analysis below provides singularity behaviors for each

U_{k}
as
{\bf{x}}\to{\bf{x}}_{i}
, for
i=1,\ldots,N
. The non-analytic term in

{\varepsilon}
in (
4.47
) arises from the subdominant logarithmic
term in the local behavior (
3.45
) of the surface Neumann
Green’s function.

In the inner region near the
i
-th Robin patch we introduce the
local geodesic coordinates (
3.15
) and expand each
inner solution as

u\sim{\varepsilon}^{-1}V_{0i}+\log\left(\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\right)V_{1i}+V_{2i}+\ldots\,.

(4.49)

Upon substituting (
4.49
) into (
3.16
), we obtain
that
V_{ki}
for
k=0,1,2
satisfies

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\bf{y}}}V_{ki}

\displaystyle=\delta_{k2}\left(2y_{3}V_{0i,y_{3}y_{3}}+2V_{0i,y_{3}}\right)\,,\quad{\bf{y}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{3}\,,

(4.50a)

\displaystyle-\partial_{y_{3}}V_{ki}+\kappa_{i}V_{ki}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\in{\Gamma}_{i}\,,

(4.50b)

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{3}}V_{ki}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\notin{\Gamma}_{i}\,,

(4.50c)

where
\delta_{22}=1
and
\delta_{k2}=0
if
k=0,1
.

Since the leading-order matching condition is
V_{0i}\sim U_{0}
as

|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty
, we have

V_{0i}=U_{0}\left(1-w_{i}\right)\,,

(4.51)

where
w_{i}
is the solution to (
3.17
), defined on the
tangent plane to the sphere at
{\bf{x}}={\bf{x}}_{i}
, which has the far-field
behavior (
3.17d
) in terms of
C_{i}
and
{\bf p}_{i}
. The matching
condition is that the local behavior of the outer expansion
(
4.47
) as
{\bf{x}}\to{\bf{x}}_{i}
must agree with the far-field
behavior of the inner expansion (
4.49
), so that

\begin{split}\frac{U_{0}}{{\varepsilon}}+U_{1}+{\varepsilon}\log\left(\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\right)U_{2}+&{\varepsilon}U_{3}+\ldots\\
\sim\frac{U_{0}}{{\varepsilon}}&\left(1-\frac{C_{i}}{|{\bf{y}}|}-\frac{{\bf p}_{i}{\bf\cdot}{\bf{y}}}{|{\bf{y}}|^{3}}\right)+\log\left(\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\right)V_{1i}+V_{2i}+\ldots\,.\end{split}

(4.52)

Since
|{\bf{y}}|\sim{\varepsilon}^{-1}|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i}|
from (
A.255
) of
Appendix
A
, it follows that the outer correction
U_{1}

must satisfy (
4.48
) with the singular behavior
U_{1}\sim-{U_{0}C_{i}/|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i}|}
as
{\bf{x}}\to{\bf{x}}_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,N
. In this
way,
U_{1}
satisfies

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\bf{x}}}U_{1}=-1\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\Omega\,;\quad\partial_{n}U_{1}=0\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\partial\Omega\backslash\{{{\bf{x}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{x}}_{N}\}}\,,

(4.53a)

\displaystyle U_{1}\sim-\frac{U_{0}C_{i}}{|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i}|}\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad{\bf{x}}\to{\bf{x}}_{i}\in\partial\Omega\,,\quad i=1,\ldots,N\,.

(4.53b)

From the divergence theorem, the solvability condition for
(
4.53
) is that
|\Omega|=2\pi U_{0}\sum_{i=1}^{N}C_{i}
,
which determines
U_{0}
as

U_{0}=\frac{|\Omega|}{2\pi\overline{C}}\,,\quad\mbox{where}\quad\overline{C}\equiv\sum_{j=1}^{N}C_{j}\,.

(4.54)

The solution to (
4.53
) is represented in terms of the
surface Neumann Green’s function of (
3.43
) as

U_{1}=\overline{U}_{1}-2\pi U_{0}\sum_{j=1}^{N}C_{j}G_{s}({\bf{x}};{\bf{x}}_{j})\,,\quad\mbox{where}\quad\overline{U}_{1}\equiv|\Omega|^{-1}\int_{\Omega}U_{1}\,d{\bf{x}}\,.

(4.55)

We recall that the coefficients
C_{i}=C_{i}(\kappa_{i})
, defined by
(
3.17d
) and (
3.18
), have the asymptotic
behavior for both small and large
\kappa_{i}
given in Lemma

1
for circular Robin patches. As in

[
30
]
, we need to expand the unknown constant

\overline{U}_{1}
in (
4.55
) as

\overline{U}_{1}=\overline{U}_{10}\log\left(\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\right)+\overline{U}_{11}\,,

(4.56)

where
\overline{U}_{10}
and
\overline{U}_{11}
are constants
independent of
{\varepsilon}
, which we determine below. We remark that the

\overline{U}_{10}\log\left({{\varepsilon}/2}\right)
term in
(
4.56
) is a “switchback term”
[
80
]

and effectively corresponds to inserting a constant term between

{U_{0}/{\varepsilon}}
and
U_{1}
in the outer expansion (
4.47
).

To proceed to higher order, we expand
U_{1}
as
{\bf{x}}\to{\bf{x}}_{i}
by using
the local behavior (
3.45
) of
G_{s}
near the
i
-th
patch. The matching condition (
4.52
) becomes

\frac{U_{0}}{{\varepsilon}}\left(1-\frac{C_{i}}{|{\bf{y}}|}\right)+\left(\frac{U_{0}C_{i}}{2}+\overline{U}_{10}\right)\log\left(\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\right)+\frac{U_{0}C_{i}}{2}\left(\log(y_{3}+|{\bf{y}}|)-\frac{y_{3}(y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2})}{|{\bf{y}}|^{3}}\right)\\
+U_{0}\beta_{i}+\overline{U}_{11}+{\varepsilon}\log\left(\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\right)U_{2}+{\varepsilon}U_{3}+\ldots\\
\sim\frac{U_{0}}{{\varepsilon}}\left(1-\frac{C_{i}}{|{\bf{y}}|}-\frac{{\bf p}_{i}{\bf\cdot}{\bf{y}}}{|{\bf{y}}|^{3}}\right)+\log\left(\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\right)V_{1i}+V_{2i}+\ldots\,.

(4.57)

Here the constant
\beta_{i}
is defined by the
i
-th component of the
matrix-vector product

\beta_{i}=-2\pi\left({\mathcal{G}}_{s}{\bf{C}}\right)_{i}\,,

(4.58)

where
{\bf{C}}\equiv(C_{1},\ldots,C_{N})^{T}
and
{\mathcal{G}}_{s}
is the
symmetric Green’s matrix defined by

{\mathcal{G}}_{s}\equiv\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}R_{s}&G_{12}&\cdots&G_{1N}\\
G_{21}&R_{s}&\cdots&G_{2N}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
G_{N1}&\cdots&G_{N,N-1}&R_{s}\end{array}\right)\,,\quad R_{s}\equiv-\frac{9}{20\pi}\,,\quad G_{ij}\equiv G_{s}({\bf{x}}_{i};{\bf{x}}_{j})\,.

(4.59)

The matrix entries above can be calculated from (
3.43
).

Upon comparing the

{\mathcal{O}}\left(\log\left({\varepsilon}/2\right)\right)
terms on both
sides of (
4.57
) we conclude that we must have

V_{1i}\sim\overline{U}_{10}+{U_{0}C_{i}/2}
as
|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty
,
where
V_{1i}
satisfies the inner problem (
4.50
) with

k=1
. This solution is determined in terms of
w_{i}
of
(
3.17
) by

V_{1i}=\left(\frac{U_{0}C_{i}}{2}+\overline{U}_{10}\right)\left(1-w_{i}\right)\,.

(4.60)

The far-field behavior (
3.17d
) for
w_{i}
yields for

\rho=|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty
that

V_{1i}\sim\left(\frac{U_{0}C_{i}}{2}+\overline{U}_{10}\right)\left(1-\frac{C_{i}}{|{\bf{y}}|}+\cdots\right)\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty\,.

(4.61)

Next, we substitute (
4.61
) into the matching condition
(
4.57
). We conclude that the solution
U_{2}
to
(
4.48
) has the singular behavior

U_{2}\sim-\left(\tfrac{1}{2}U_{0}C_{i}+\overline{U}_{10}\right){C_{i}/|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i}|}
as
{\bf{x}}\to{\bf{x}}_{i}
. Therefore,
U_{2}
satisfies

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\bf{x}}}U_{2}=0\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\Omega\,;\quad\partial_{n}U_{2}=0\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\partial\Omega\backslash\{{{\bf{x}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{x}}_{N}\}}\,,

(4.62a)

\displaystyle U_{2}\sim-\left(\frac{U_{0}C_{i}}{2}+\overline{U}_{10}\right)\frac{C_{i}}{|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i}|}\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad{\bf{x}}\to{\bf{x}}_{i}\in\partial\Omega\,,\quad i=1,\ldots,N\,.

(4.62b)

By using the divergence theorem, (
4.62
) is
solvable only when
\overline{U}_{10}
satisfies

\frac{\overline{U}_{10}}{U_{0}}=-\frac{{\bf{C}}^{T}{\bf{C}}}{2\overline{C}}\,,\quad\mbox{where}\quad{\bf{C}}^{T}{\bf{C}}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}C_{j}^{2}\,.

(4.63)

Then, the solution to (
4.62
) is represented in terms of
an unknown constant
\overline{U}_{2}
as

U_{2}=\overline{U}_{2}-2\pi\sum_{j=1}^{N}C_{j}\left(\frac{U_{0}C_{j}}{2}+\overline{U}_{10}\right)G_{s}({\bf{x}};{\bf{x}}_{j})\,.

(4.64)

Next, we match the
{\mathcal{O}}(1)
terms in (
4.57
). We
obtain that
V_{2i}
satisfies (
4.50
) with
k=2
together
with the far-field behavior

V_{2i}\sim\beta_{i}U_{0}+\overline{U}_{11}+\frac{U_{0}C_{i}}{2}\left(\log(y_{3}+|{\bf{y}}|)-\frac{y_{3}(y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2})}{|{\bf{y}}|^{3}}\right)\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty\,.

(4.65)

Since
V_{0i}=U_{0}(1-w_{i})
from (
4.51
), we decompose

V_{2i}
as

V_{2i}=U_{0}\left(\Phi_{2i}+\left(\beta_{i}+\frac{\overline{U}_{11}}{U_{0}}\right)(1-w_{i})\right)\,,

(4.66)

and obtain from (
4.50
) and (
4.65
) that
\Phi_{2i}

satisfies

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\bf{y}}}\Phi_{2i}

\displaystyle=-\left(2y_{3}w_{i,y_{3}y_{3}}+2w_{i,y_{3}}\right),\quad{\bf{y}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{3}\,,

(4.67a)

\displaystyle-\partial_{y_{3}}\Phi_{2i}+\kappa_{i}\Phi_{2i}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\in{\Gamma}_{i}\,,

(4.67b)

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{3}}\Phi_{2i}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\notin{\Gamma}_{i}\,,

(4.67c)

\displaystyle\Phi_{2i}

\displaystyle\sim\frac{C_{i}}{2}\left(\log(y_{3}+|{\bf{y}}|)-\frac{y_{3}(y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2})}{|{\bf{y}}|^{3}}\right)\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty\,.

(4.67d)

In Appendix
C
we analyze the solution to
(
4.67
) and we determine the monopole term

E_{i}=E_{i}(\kappa_{i})
in the refined far-field behavior, defined by the
limiting behavior

\Phi_{2i}-\frac{C_{i}}{2}\left(\log(y_{3}+|{\bf{y}}|)-\frac{y_{3}(y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2})}{|{\bf{y}}|^{3}}\right)\sim\frac{E_{i}}{|{\bf{y}}|}\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty\,.

(4.68)

For an arbitrary patch shape
{\Gamma}_{i}
,
E_{i}(\kappa_{i})
is given by
(
3.36
). Some properties of
E_{i}(\kappa_{i})
were
summarized in §
3.2
. In particular, when
{\Gamma}_{i}
is a
disk, the limiting asymptotics of
E_{i}(\kappa_{i})
for
\kappa_{i}\ll 1

and
\kappa_{i}\gg 1
are given in (
3.40
) of Lemma

2
. In addition, an accurate heuristic global
approximation for
E_{i}
was given in (
3.41
).

Finally, we determine
\overline{U}_{11}
from a solvability condition
for the problem for the outer correction
U_{3}
in
(
4.47
). To do so, we substitute (
4.68
)
into (
4.66
) and use
w_{i}\sim{C_{i}/|{\bf{y}}|}
as

|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty
. We conclude that
V_{2i}
satisfies the refined
far-field behavior

\begin{split}V_{2i}&\sim\beta_{i}U_{0}+\overline{U}_{11}+\frac{U_{0}C_{i}}{2}\left(\log(y_{3}+|{\bf{y}}|)-\frac{y_{3}(y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2})}{|{\bf{y}}|^{3}}\right)\\
&\qquad+\frac{U_{0}E_{i}}{|{\bf{y}}|}-\left(\beta_{i}U_{0}+\overline{U}_{11}\right)\frac{C_{i}}{|{\bf{y}}|}\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty\,.\end{split}

(4.69)

The second line in (
4.69
) is the first of two terms
that needs to be accounted for by
U_{3}
in the matching condition
(
4.57
). The second term is the dipole term in
(
4.57
), which arises from (
3.17d
). This term
is written in terms of outer variables using (
A.255
) of
Appendix
A
.

In this way, we conclude from (
4.48
), (
4.57
)
and (
4.69
) that
U_{3}
must satisfy

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\bf{x}}}U_{3}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\Omega\,;\quad\partial_{n}U_{3}=0\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\partial\Omega\backslash\{{{\bf{x}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{x}}_{N}\}}\,,

(4.70a)

\displaystyle U_{3}

\displaystyle\sim\frac{\left[U_{0}E_{i}-\left(\beta_{i}U_{0}+\overline{U}_{11}\right)C_{i}\right]}{|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i}|}

\displaystyle\qquad-U_{0}\frac{{\bf p}_{i}{\bf\cdot}{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}^{T}({\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i})}{|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i}|^{3}}\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad{\bf{x}}\to{\bf{x}}_{i}\in\partial\Omega\,,\quad i=1,\ldots,N\,,

(4.70b)

where the orthogonal matrix
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}
is defined in
(
A.255
) in terms of the basis vectors of the geodesic
coordinate system and
{\bf p}_{i}
is the dipole vector in
(
3.17d
). By using the divergence theorem,
(
4.70
) has a solution if and only if

\overline{U}_{11}
satisfies

\frac{\overline{U}_{11}}{U_{0}}=\frac{1}{\overline{C}}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{N}E_{j}-\sum_{j=1}^{N}\beta_{j}C_{j}\right)\,,

(4.71)

where we find that the contribution from the dipole term vanishes
identically by symmetry since
{\bf p}_{i}
has the form

{\bf p}_{i}=(p_{1i},p_{2i},0)^{T}
. Finally, by using (
4.58
) for

\beta_{i}
, we get

\frac{\overline{U}_{11}}{U_{0}}=\frac{2\pi}{\overline{C}}{\bf{C}}^{T}{\mathcal{G}}_{s}{\bf{C}}+\frac{\overline{E}}{\overline{C}}\,,\quad\mbox{where}\quad\overline{E}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}E_{j}\,.

(4.72)

We summarize our main result for the dimensionless MFRT
u({\bf{x}})
and
the volume-averaged MFRT
\overline{u}
in the small-patch limit in the
following proposition. We also provide the corresponding dimensional
result for (
2.3
), based on the scalings
(
2.8
) and (
2.9
).

Proposition 1
.

For
{\varepsilon}\to 0
, the asymptotic solution to (
2.10
) in the
unit sphere
\Omega
is given in the outer region

|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i}|\gg{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon})
for
i=1,\ldots,N
by

\begin{split}u({\bf{x}})&\sim\frac{U_{0}}{{\varepsilon}}\left[1+{\varepsilon}\log\left(\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\right)\frac{\overline{U}_{10}}{U_{0}}+{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{\overline{U}_{11}}{U_{0}}-2\pi\sum_{j=1}^{N}C_{j}G_{s}({\bf{x}};{\bf{x}}_{j})\right)\right.\\
&\qquad\left.+{\varepsilon}^{2}\log\left(\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\right)\left(\frac{\overline{U}_{2}}{U_{0}}-2\pi\sum_{j=1}^{N}C_{j}\left(\frac{C_{j}}{2}+\frac{\overline{U}_{10}}{U_{0}}\right)G_{s}({\bf{x}};{\bf{x}}_{j})\right)+{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon}^{2})\right]\,.\end{split}

(4.73)

The volume-averaged MFRT
\overline{u}
, defined by (
2.11
),
satisfies

\overline{u}\sim\frac{U_{0}}{{\varepsilon}}\left[1+{\varepsilon}\log\left(\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\right)\frac{\overline{U}_{10}}{U_{0}}+{\varepsilon}\frac{\overline{U}_{11}}{U_{0}}+{\mathcal{O}}\left({\varepsilon}^{2}\log{\varepsilon}\right)\right]\,.

(4.74)

In (
4.73
) and (
4.74
),
U_{0}
,

\overline{U}_{10}
and
\overline{U}_{11}
are determined in terms of

{\bf{C}}=(C_{1},\ldots,C_{N})^{T}
,
\overline{C}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}C_{j}
,

\overline{E}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}E_{j}
, and the Green’s matrix
{\mathcal{G}}_{s}
from (
4.59
) and (
3.43
) by

U_{0}=\frac{2}{3\overline{C}}\,,\qquad\frac{\overline{U}_{10}}{U_{0}}=-\frac{{\bf{C}}^{T}{\bf{C}}}{2\overline{C}}\,,\qquad\frac{\overline{U}_{11}}{U_{0}}=\frac{2\pi}{\overline{C}}{\bf{C}}^{T}{\mathcal{G}}_{s}{\bf{C}}+\frac{\overline{E}}{\overline{C}}\,.

(4.75)

For a single patch, where
N=1
, we have

U_{0}=\frac{2}{3C_{1}}\,,\qquad\frac{\overline{U}_{10}}{U_{0}}=-\frac{C_{1}}{2}\,,\qquad\frac{\overline{U}_{11}}{U_{0}}=-\frac{9}{10}C_{1}+\frac{E_{1}}{C_{1}}\,.

(4.76)

In terms of the dimensional variables, we use (
2.8
)
and (
2.9
) to conclude for a sphere of radius
R
and
for a collection of Robin patches with maximum diameter
L
that

\overline{U}\sim\frac{R^{2}}{D}\overline{u}\,.

(4.77)

Here in calculating
\overline{u}
in (
4.74
) we set

C_{i}=C_{i}\left({L{\mathcal{K}}_{i}/D}\right)
and
E_{i}=E_{i}\left({L{\mathcal{K}}_{i}/D}\right)
in
(
4.75
) and evaluate the Green’s matrix
{\mathcal{G}}_{s}

at
{\bf{x}}_{i}={{\bf X}_{i}/R}
.

Although in (
4.73
) the coefficient
\overline{U}_{2}

can only be determined at higher order, the spatial dependence of the

{\varepsilon}^{2}\log\left({{\varepsilon}/2}\right)
correction term is completely
specified up to this unknown constant. In (
4.73
)
and (
4.74
),
C_{i}=C_{i}(\kappa_{i})
is determined by
the solution to (
3.17
), while
E_{i}=E_{i}(\kappa_{i})
is given by
(
3.36
) for an arbitrary shaped patch and by
(
3.38
) when the Robin patches are disks. Their asymptotic
behaviors when
{\Gamma}_{i}
is a disk are given for small and large
reactivities in Lemmas
1
and
2
.

We emphasize several features of our main result for the MFRT:

(i) The coefficient
\overline{U}_{11}
in
(
4.74
) depends on the spatial configuration

\{{{\bf{x}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{x}}_{N}\}}
of the centers of the Robin patches
on the surface of the unit sphere via the Green’s matrix
{\mathcal{G}}_{s}
, defined in (
4.59
) and (
3.43
).
Therefore, the effect of the location of the patches is only
revealed at the third order in the asymptotic expansion.

(ii) To numerically calculate the asymptotic result
(
4.74
) for the volume-averaged MFRT, we need only
numerically compute
C_{i}(\kappa_{i})
from a PDE solution of
(
3.17
) and
E_{i}(\kappa_{i})
from the quadrature in
(
3.36
). However, by using the heuristic, but
accurate, approximations (
3.26
) and (
3.41
)
that closely predict respectively
C_{i}
and
E_{i}
for all

\kappa_{i}>0
, the coefficients in the asymptotic expansion of the
volume-averaged MFRT (
4.75
) can be explicitly estimated
when
{\Gamma}_{i}
is a disk.

(iii) We observe from Lemmas

1
and
2
that both

{\overline{U}_{10}/U_{0}}
and
{\overline{U}_{11}/U_{0}}
are

{\mathcal{O}}(\kappa_{i})
as
\kappa_{i}\to 0
for
i=1,\ldots,N
. As a
result, the expansion (
4.74
) in
{\varepsilon}
remains
uniformly valid in the limit
\kappa_{i}\to 0
for each Robin
patch.

(iv) Proposition
1
extends the previous result
from
[
30
]
that dealt with the special case of
N

perfectly reactive (
\kappa_{i}=\infty
) disk-shape patches.

For the special case of
N

identical
patches, we have

\overline{C}=NC_{1}
,
\overline{E}=NE_{1}
,
U_{0}={2/(3NC_{1})}
,

\overline{U}_{10}/U_{0}=-C_{1}/2
,
\overline{U}_{11}/U_{0}=2\pi C_{1}({\bf e}^{T}{\mathcal{G}}_{s}{\bf e})/N+E_{1}/C_{1}
, with
{\bf e}=(1,\ldots,1)^{T}
, so
that the asymptotic expansion (
4.74
) applied to the
dimensional volume-averaged MFRT in (
4.77
) yields

\overline{U}\sim\frac{|\Omega|}{2\pi DNR}\left(\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}C_{1}}+\frac{1}{2}\log\left({2/{\varepsilon}}\right)+\frac{2\pi}{N}({\bf e}^{T}{\mathcal{G}}_{s}{\bf e})+\frac{E_{1}}{C_{1}^{2}}+{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon}\log{\varepsilon})\right)\,,

(4.78)

where
|\Omega|={4\pi R^{3}/3}
. For instance, for
N
circular
perfectly reactive patches of radius
{\varepsilon}R
, we have
C_{1}={2/\pi}

and
E_{1}=(3-4\log 2)/\pi^{2}
from (
3.33a
) and
(
3.40a
), respectively, so that
(
4.78
) reduces to

\overline{U}\sim\frac{|\Omega|}{4DNR}\left(\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}}+\frac{1}{\pi}\log\left({1/{\varepsilon}}\right)+\frac{4}{N}({\bf e}^{T}{\mathcal{G}}_{s}{\bf e})+\frac{3}{2\pi}-\frac{\log{2}}{\pi}+{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon}\log{\varepsilon})\right)\,.

(4.79)

This expression corrects and extends, with its inclusion of the

{\mathcal{O}}(1)
term, the seminal result by Singer
et al.

[
114
]
that was obtained for a single circular patch
(note that the factor
1/\pi
in front of the logarithmic term was
missing in
[
114
]
).

4.1
Expansion for Moderate Reactivities

The crucial advantage of our asymptotic analysis is that the
expansions (
4.73
) and (
4.74
)
remain valid for any reactivities
\kappa_{i}
, even in the limit

\kappa_{i}\to\infty
of perfectly reactive patches. In contrast,
former approaches usually fixed
finite
reactivities
{\mathcal{K}}_{i}
and
then analyzed the limit
{\varepsilon}\to 0
such that
\kappa_{i}={\varepsilon}R{\mathcal{K}}_{i}/D\to 0
according to (
2.8
). In this limit, one
can use the asymptotic behaviors of
C_{i}(\kappa_{i})
and

E_{i}(\kappa_{i})
as
\kappa_{i}\to 0
to derive more explicit expansions.
Throughout this subsection, we aim to express the volume-averaged MFRT
in terms of finite reactivities
{\mathcal{K}}_{i}
and the small parameter
{\varepsilon}
.

We first rewrite the Taylor expansion (
3.22a
) for the
reactive capacitance in terms of dimensional parameters. Upon using

a_{i}=L_{i}/({\varepsilon}R)
from (
2.8
) together with
(
3.23
) for
c_{1i}
, we obtain

\begin{split}C_{i}&=\frac{1}{2\pi{\varepsilon}RD}\biggl(|{\partial\Omega}_{i}|{\mathcal{K}}_{i}-2\pi c_{2i}\frac{L_{i}^{3}{\mathcal{K}}_{i}^{2}}{D}+2\pi c_{3i}\frac{L_{i}^{4}{\mathcal{K}}_{i}^{3}}{D^{2}}+\ldots\biggr)\,.\end{split}

(4.80)

Here the dimensionless coefficients
c_{2i}
and
c_{3i}
are given in
(
3.23
) for arbitrary patch shapes, while their exact
values for circular patches are given in (
3.30
). We
emphasize that they depend only on the shape of the
i
-th patch, not
on its size. As a consequence, we get for
\overline{C}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}C_{j}

that

\overline{C}=\frac{|{\partial\Omega}|}{2\pi{\varepsilon}RD}\left(\overline{{\mathcal{K}}}^{(1)}-\overline{{\mathcal{K}}}^{(2)}+\overline{{\mathcal{K}}}^{(3)}+{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon}^{5})\right)\,,

(4.81)

where
|{\partial\Omega}|=4\pi R^{2}
is the surface area of the sphere and where we
introduced the weighted reactivities defined by

\displaystyle\overline{{\mathcal{K}}}^{(1)}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{|{\partial\Omega}|}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}|{\partial\Omega}_{i}|{\mathcal{K}}_{i}=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}K_{i}\equiv\overline{K}\,,\quad\mbox{with}\quad K_{i}\equiv{\mathcal{K}}_{i}\frac{|{\partial\Omega}_{i}|}{|{\partial\Omega}|}\,,

(4.82a)

\displaystyle\overline{{\mathcal{K}}}^{(n)}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{2\pi}{D^{n-1}|{\partial\Omega}|}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}c_{ni}L_{i}^{n+1}{\mathcal{K}}_{i}^{n}\,,\quad\mbox{for}\quad n=2,3,\ldots\,.

(4.82b)

Since
L_{i}\sim{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon})
and
|{\partial\Omega}_{i}|\sim{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon}^{2})
, we have

\overline{{\mathcal{K}}}^{(n)}\sim{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon}^{n+1})
for
n\geq 1
.

Substitution of the expansion (
4.81
) into
(
4.75
), while using the binomial approximation, yields

U_{0}=\frac{4\pi{\varepsilon}RD}{3|{\partial\Omega}|\overline{K}}\left(1+\frac{\overline{{\mathcal{K}}}^{(2)}}{\overline{K}}+\frac{[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}}^{(2)}]^{2}-\overline{{\mathcal{K}}}^{(3)}\overline{K}}{\overline{K}^{2}}+{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon}^{5})\right)\,.

(4.83)

We also derive from (
4.75
) that

\frac{\overline{U}_{10}}{U_{0}}=-\frac{1}{2\overline{C}}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}C_{i}^{2}=-\frac{|{\partial\Omega}|}{4\pi{\varepsilon}DR\overline{K}}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}K_{i}^{2}+{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon}^{2})\,.

(4.84)

To estimate
{\overline{U}_{11}/U_{0}}
in (
4.75
), we first use

E_{i}\sim C_{i}^{2}e_{i}
from (
3.37
) to obtain to leading order
that

\frac{\overline{E}}{\overline{C}}=\frac{|{\partial\Omega}|}{2\pi{\varepsilon}RD\overline{K}}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}e_{i}K_{i}^{2}+{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon}^{2})\,.

(4.85)

In this way, we obtain in terms of
{\bf K}=(K_{1},\ldots,K_{N})^{T}
that

\frac{\overline{U}_{11}}{U_{0}}=\frac{|{\partial\Omega}|}{2\pi{\varepsilon}RD\overline{K}}\left(2\pi({\bf K}^{T}{\mathcal{G}}_{s}{\bf K})+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}e_{i}K_{i}^{2}\right)+{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon}^{2})\,.

(4.86)

Substituting the expansions (
4.83
), (
4.84
),
and (
4.86
) into (
4.74
) and
(
4.77
), we finally obtain the following
four-term

expansion for the dimensional volume-averaged MFRT:

\begin{split}\overline{U}&\sim\frac{|\Omega|}{|{\partial\Omega}|}\biggl(\underbrace{\frac{1}{\overline{K}}}_{\propto{\varepsilon}^{-2}}+\underbrace{\frac{\overline{{\mathcal{K}}}^{(2)}}{\overline{K}^{2}}}_{\propto{\varepsilon}^{-1}}+\underbrace{\log(2/{\varepsilon})\frac{|{\partial\Omega}|({\bf K}^{T}{\bf K})}{4\pi DR\overline{K}^{2}}}_{\propto\log({\varepsilon})}+\underbrace{\frac{[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}}^{(2)}]^{2}-\overline{{\mathcal{K}}}^{(3)}\overline{K}}{\overline{K}^{3}}}_{\propto 1}\\
&\qquad\qquad+\underbrace{\frac{|{\partial\Omega}|}{2\pi RD\overline{K}^{2}}\biggl(2\pi({\bf K}^{T}{\mathcal{G}}_{s}{\bf K})+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}e_{i}K_{i}^{2}\biggr)}_{\propto 1}+\,o(1)\biggr)\,.\end{split}

(4.87)

This is the main result of this subsection. Several comments are in
order:

(i) The first (leading-order) term in (
4.87
) depends only on
the reactivities and surface areas of the patches. The second and the
third terms, providing the contributions
{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon}^{-1})
and

{\mathcal{O}}(\log{\varepsilon})
, also depend on the shape of the patches (via the
coefficients
c_{2i}
and
c_{3i}
). Finally, the last term in
(
4.87
) of order
{\mathcal{O}}(1)
incorporates the details on the
spatial arrangement of patches via the Green’s matrix
{\mathcal{G}}_{s}
.

(ii) For
N
identical patches of a common reactivity
{\mathcal{K}}
and surface
area
|{\partial\Omega}_{1}|
, (
4.87
) reduces, with
{\bf e}=(1,\ldots,1)^{T}
, to

\begin{split}\overline{U}&\sim\frac{|\Omega|}{N}\biggl(\frac{1}{{\mathcal{K}}|{\partial\Omega}_{1}|}+2\pi c_{21}\frac{L_{1}^{3}}{D|{\partial\Omega}_{1}|^{2}}+\frac{\log(2/{\varepsilon})}{4\pi DR}+\frac{1}{2\pi RD}\biggl(e_{1}+\frac{2\pi}{N}({\bf e}^{T}{\mathcal{G}}_{s}{\bf e})\biggr)\\
&\qquad\qquad+\frac{2\pi{\mathcal{K}}L_{1}^{6}}{D^{2}|{\partial\Omega}_{1}|^{3}}\left(2\pi c_{21}^{2}-c_{31}\frac{|{\partial\Omega}_{1}|}{L_{1}^{2}}\right)+o(1)\biggr)\,.\end{split}

(4.88)

Interestingly, the second and the third terms do not depend on the
reactivity
{\mathcal{K}}
. One might thus naively expect that these terms
remain valid even in the limit
{\mathcal{K}}\to\infty
. However, this is not
true, as revealed by comparison of these terms with the first two
terms of the expansion (
4.78
), which is valid for
any
{\mathcal{K}}
. One sees that the logarithmic terms are indeed the same.
However, the coefficients in front of the contribution
{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon}^{-1})

are in general different (e.g., for circular patches, the second term
in (
4.88
) is
8/(3\pi^{2}DR{\varepsilon})
, whereas the
first term in (
4.79
) is
1/(4DR{\varepsilon})
). This
distinction originates from the singular character of the limit
{\mathcal{K}}\to\infty
. In fact, the expansions (
4.87
) and
(
4.88
) are based on the asymptotic behavior of

C_{i}(\kappa_{i})
as
\kappa_{i}\to 0
, which is not applicable when

\kappa_{i}=\infty
.

(iii) For
N
identical circular patches of radius
{\varepsilon}R=L_{1}
, we
have
c_{2}={4/(3\pi)}
and
c_{3}\approx 0.3651
as given in
(
3.30
). Moreover, since
E_{1}\sim{C_{1}^{2}/8}
as

\kappa_{i}\to 0
from (
3.40b
), we identify from
(
3.37
) that
e_{1}={1/8}
. In this way,
(
4.88
) yields the explicit result

\begin{split}\overline{U}&\sim\frac{|\Omega|}{N\pi}\biggl(\frac{1}{{\mathcal{K}}R^{2}{\varepsilon}^{2}}+\frac{8}{3\pi RD{\varepsilon}}+\frac{1}{4DR}\log(2/{\varepsilon})+\biggl(\frac{64}{9\pi^{2}}-2c_{3}\biggr)\frac{{\mathcal{K}}}{D^{2}}\\
&\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{2RD}\biggl(\frac{1}{8}+\frac{2\pi}{N}({\bf e}^{T}{\mathcal{G}}_{s}{\bf e})\biggr)+o(1)\biggr)\,.\end{split}

(4.89)

First, we compare this expansion with an approximate expansion that
was derived in
[
61
]
for a single circular patch by
using a constant-flux approximation. Despite the approximate
character of the expansion from
[
61
]
, its first three
terms turn out to be identical with those in our exact expansion
(
4.89
).
Secondly, the expansion (
4.89
) also agrees with the
expansion (5.22) from
[
56
]
, which was obtained for a
single circular patch by a different method.

We conclude this subsection with two comments:

(i) To leading order, the volume-averaged MFRT behaves as

\overline{U}\sim|\Omega|/(|{\partial\Omega}|\overline{K})
, so that the
trapping effect of small patches is analogous to a parallel connection
of wires, whose resistances are inversely proportional to wire
cross-sectional areas
|\partial\Omega_{i}|
and charge career’s
mobilities (here
{\mathcal{K}}_{i}
) in electrostatics. Diffusion screening
between patches (i.e., their competition for diffusing particles)
appears only at higher orders.

(ii) The volume-averaged MFRT scales as
{\varepsilon}^{-2}
, i.e., as the
surface area of the patches, which is drastically different from the
case of perfectly reactive patches, for which
\overline{U}
scales as

{\varepsilon}^{-1}
. This crucial distinction between perfectly and partially
reactive patches was emphasized in
[
61
]
(see also

[
58
]
).

4.2
Numerical Comparison

We aim at validating the asymptotic results by comparison with a
numerical solution of the original PDE (
2.10
). Different
numerical methods are available for this purpose, including a
hierarchical, fast multipole method
[
76
]
, spectral expansions

[
48
]
, finite-element methods
[
31
]
, Monte
Carlo methods
[
100
,
122
]
. However, most of these methods
are either specifically designed for perfectly reactive patches (i.e.,
Dirichlet boundary condition), or limited to not-too-small partially
reactive patches. For our validation purposes, we resort to a basic
finite-element method (FEM) implemented in Matlab and focus on the
unit sphere with two patches centered at the north and south poles.
The symmetry of this setting allows one to reduce the original 3-D
problem to a planar one that can be solved with less numerical
efforts.

Figure
5
illustrates the volume-averaged MFRT

\overline{u}
(
4.74
) on two identical circular
patches of angle
\epsilon
on the unit sphere (since
{\varepsilon}=2\sin(\epsilon/2)
, one has
{\varepsilon}\approx\epsilon
for small
patches). While the asymptotic results were derived in the limit

{\varepsilon}\to 0
, the volume-averaged MFRT was computed numerically for a
broad range of
\epsilon
, up to
{\pi/2}
, when one patch covers a
hemisphere, whereas two patches cover the whole sphere. Remarkably,
Fig.
5
reveals that the asymptotic formula
(
4.74
) remains accurate up to
\epsilon\simeq 1
, i.e., far beyond its expected range of applicability. We
remark that the minor deviations at small
\epsilon
can be
attributed to the inaccuracy of the FEM solution.

Figure 5
:
Dimensionless volume-averaged MFRT
\overline{u}
from
(
4.74
) to two identical circular patches of angle

\epsilon
(with
a_{1}=a_{2}=1
), located at the north and south
poles of the unit sphere, with
\kappa_{1}=\kappa_{2}=\infty

(squares) or
\kappa_{1}=\kappa_{2}=1
(circles). The
curves present the asymptotic formula (
4.74
)
with
{\varepsilon}=2\sin(\epsilon/2)
, whereas symbols indicate the
FEM solution with the maximal meshsize
h_{\rm max}=0.0025
.

4.3
Effective Reactivity in the Homogenized Limit

We now derive a scaling law that characterizes the (dimensionless)
effective reactivity
k_{\rm eff}
corresponding to a large number of
identical circular patches, with a common radius
{\varepsilon}
and reactivity

\kappa
, that are uniformly distributed on the surface on the unit
sphere. A similar analysis has been done for the case of Dirichlet
patches in
[
31
]
.

For this homogenized limit we define
u_{\rm eff}=u_{\rm eff}(|{\bf{x}}|)
as the
radially symmetric solution to

\Delta_{{\bf{x}}}u_{\rm eff}=-1\,,\quad 0\leq|{\bf{x}}|<1\,;\qquad\partial_{n}u_{\rm eff}+k_{\rm eff}u_{\rm eff}=0\,,\quad|{\bf{x}}|=1\,.

(4.90)

The solution to (
4.90
) and the homogenized volume-averaged
MFRT, defined as
\overline{u}_{\rm eff}\equiv|\Omega|^{-1}\int_{\Omega}u_{\rm eff}\,d{\bf{x}}
, are

u_{\rm eff}=\frac{1}{6}\left(1-|{\bf{x}}|^{2}\right)+\frac{1}{3k_{\rm eff}}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\overline{u}_{\rm eff}=\frac{1}{15}+\frac{1}{3k_{\rm eff}}\,.

(4.91)

Since the reactive patches are all disks with radius
{\varepsilon}
and
reactivity
\kappa
, we set
C_{i}=C(\kappa)
and
E_{i}=E(\kappa)
in
(
4.74
) and (
4.75
) to obtain that

\overline{u}\sim\frac{2}{3N{\varepsilon}C}\left[1+\frac{{\varepsilon}C}{2}\log\left({2/{\varepsilon}}\right)+{\varepsilon}C\left(\frac{P}{N}+\frac{E}{C^{2}}\right)\right]\,,

(4.92)

where

P=P({\bf{x}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{x}}_{N})\equiv 2\pi{\bf e}^{T}{\mathcal{G}}_{s}{\bf e}

with
{\bf e}=(1,\ldots,1)^{T}
. Upon using the entries of the Green’s
matrix
{\mathcal{G}}_{s}
in (
4.59
), as can be
calculated from (
3.43
), we obtain that

P=-\frac{9N^{2}}{10}+N(N-1)\log{2}+2{\mathcal{H}}({\bf{x}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{x}}_{N})\,,

(4.93)

where the discrete energy
{\mathcal{H}}
on the unit sphere is
defined by
