{\mathcal{H}}({\bf{x}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{x}}_{N})=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{j=i+1}^{N}\left(\frac{1}{|{\bf{x}}_{i}-{\bf{x}}_{j}|}-\frac{1}{2}\log|{\bf{x}}_{i}-{\bf{x}}_{j}|-\frac{1}{2}\log\left(2+|{\bf{x}}_{i}-{\bf{x}}_{j}|\right)\right)\,.

(4.94)

For our homogenized limit, we require

\overline{u}=\overline{u}_{{\rm eff}}
. By using
(
4.91
), and in terms of
{\mathcal{H}}
, we obtain
after a little algebra that (
4.92
) determines
k_{\rm eff}

as

\frac{1}{k_{\rm eff}}\sim\frac{2}{N{\varepsilon}C}\left[1+\frac{{\varepsilon}C}{2}\log\left({2/{\varepsilon}}\right)+{\varepsilon}C\left(\frac{E}{C^{2}}-N+(N-1)\log{2}+\frac{2{\mathcal{H}}}{N}\right)\right]\,.

(4.95)

As derived formally in Appendix of
[
31
]
(see also §4 of
[
30
]
), for a large collection of uniformly
distributed patches with centers at
{\bf{x}}_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,N
, we have
for
N\to\infty
that

{\mathcal{H}}\sim\frac{N^{2}}{2}\left(1-\log{2}\right)+b_{1}N^{3/2}+b_{2}N\log{N}+b_{3}N+{\mathcal{O}}\left(N^{1/2}\right)\,,

(4.96a)

with coefficients

b_{1}=-\frac{1}{2}\,,\quad b_{2}=-\frac{1}{8}\,,\quad b_{3}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\log{2}-\frac{1}{4}\right)\,.

(4.96b)

To derive our scaling law for
k_{\rm eff}
we substitute (
4.96
)
into (
4.95
) and write the result in terms of the
surface fraction of patches, defined by
f\equiv{N\pi{\varepsilon}^{2}/(4\pi)}
.
For the dilute fraction limit
f\ll 1
, we obtain after some algebra
that (
4.95
) reduces to

k_{\rm eff}\sim\frac{2fC}{{\varepsilon}}\left[1+4b_{1}C\sqrt{f}+{\varepsilon}C\left(\frac{E}{C^{2}}-\frac{1}{4}-\frac{1}{4}\log f\right)\right]^{-1}\,,

(4.97)

with
b_{1}=-1/2
. We remark that, as shown in
[
87
]
for a
related problem, the estimate
b_{1}\approx-0.5523
should be slightly
better than using
b_{1}=-{1/2}
as it accounts for the small defects
from the uniformly distributed patch assumption. Such defects will
always occur when tiling on a sphere. Related homogenization results,
but valid only for
\kappa=\infty
, have been derived in

[
31
]
and in
[
87
]
for the interior and
exterior problems, respectively.

This main result characterizes
k_{\rm eff}
in terms of both
C
and
E
,
which depend on the common local reactivity
\kappa
of the patches.
In particular, for
\kappa\to\infty
, we set
a_{i}=1
in
(
3.33a
) and (
3.40a
) to obtain
C\sim{2/\pi}
and
{E/C^{2}}\sim(3-4\log 2)/(2\pi)
, so that
(
4.97
) becomes

k_{\rm eff}\sim\frac{4f}{\pi{\varepsilon}}\left[1+\frac{8b_{1}}{\pi}\sqrt{f}+\frac{{\varepsilon}}{\pi}\left(1-\log{4}-\frac{1}{2}\log{f}\right)\right]^{-1}\,,\quad\mbox{for}\quad\kappa\gg 1\,.

(4.98)

Alternatively, for
\kappa\to 0
, (
3.33b
) and
(
3.40b
) with
a_{i}=1
yield that
C\sim{\kappa/2}
and
E\sim{\kappa^{2}/32}
so that (
4.97
) becomes

k_{\rm eff}\sim\frac{f\kappa}{{\varepsilon}}\left[1+2b_{1}\kappa\sqrt{f}-\frac{{\varepsilon}\kappa}{16}\left(1+2\log{f}\right)\right]^{-1}\,,\quad\mbox{for}\quad\kappa\ll 1\,.

(4.99)

In order to obtain an explicit approximation for
k_{\rm eff}
valid for all

\kappa>0
, one can use in (
4.97
) the heuristic
approximations for
C
and
E
from §
3
. Upon
setting
a_{i}=1
, we can use
C(\kappa)\approx{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm app}(\kappa)

and
E(\kappa)\approx{\mathcal{E}}^{\rm app}(\kappa)
, where the
explicit functions
{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm app}(\kappa)
and
{\mathcal{E}}^{\rm app}(\kappa)
are given by (
3.31
) and
(
3.41
), respectively.

Finally, we reformulate the homogenized result for the
dimensional problem (
2.3
) in a sphere of radius
R
,
covered by circular patches of a common radius
L\ll R
and common
dimensional reactivity
{\mathcal{K}}_{i}={\mathcal{K}}
for
i=1,\ldots,N
. Upon recalling
(
2.8
) and
{\varepsilon}={L/R}
, we use
(
4.97
) to identify that

{\mathcal{K}}_{\rm eff}=\frac{D}{L}k_{\rm eff}\sim\frac{2DR}{L^{2}}fC\left[1+4b_{1}C\sqrt{f}+{\varepsilon}C\left(\frac{E}{C^{2}}-\frac{1}{4}-\frac{1}{4}\log f\right)\right]^{-1}\,,

(4.100)

where
C=C\left({L{\mathcal{K}}/D}\right)
and
E=E\left({L{\mathcal{K}}/D}\right)
, and

{\mathcal{K}}_{\rm eff}
has units of
{\mbox{length}/\mbox{time}}
. This result
for
{\mathcal{K}}_{\rm eff}
pertains to the low patch area fraction

f={N\left({L/R}\right)^{2}/4}\ll 1
.

4.4
Laplacian Eigenvalue Problem with Reactive Patches

In this subsection, we briefly consider the following eigenvalue
problem for the Robin Laplacian in the unit sphere
\Omega
:

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\bf{x}}}\phi+\lambda\phi

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\Omega\,;\qquad\int_{\Omega}\phi^{2}\,d{\bf{x}}=1\,,

(4.101a)

\displaystyle{\varepsilon}\partial_{n}\phi+\kappa_{i}\phi

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\partial\Omega^{{\varepsilon}}_{i}\,,\quad i=1,\ldots,N\,,

(4.101b)

\displaystyle\partial_{n}\phi

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\partial\Omega_{r}=\partial\Omega\backslash\partial\Omega_{a}\,.

(4.101c)

In §3 of
[
30
]
, a three-term expansion for the
principal (lowest) eigenvalue
\lambda_{0}
of (
4.101
) was
derived for a collection of well-separated perfectly reactive
(
\kappa_{i}=\infty
) locally circular patches
\Omega_{i}^{{\varepsilon}}
. We
now derive the corresponding result for partially reactive and
arbitrary-shaped patches. Instead of repeating an inner-outer
expansion analysis similar to that done for the MFRT and in §3 of

[
30
]
, we derive our result primarily from an
eigenfunction expansion solution of (
2.10
).

Labeling
\lambda_{j}=\lambda_{j}({\varepsilon})
and
\phi_{j}=\phi_{j}({\bf{x}};{\varepsilon})
for

j\geq 0
to be the eigenpairs of (
4.101
), for which

\lambda_{0}\to 0
as
{\varepsilon}\to 0
and
\lambda_{j}={\mathcal{O}}(1)
as

{\varepsilon}\to 0
, we represent the solution to (
2.10
) as

u=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\lambda_{j}^{-1}\phi_{j}\left(\int_{\Omega}\phi_{j}\,d{\bf{x}}\right)
. By calculating the
average
\overline{u}\equiv|\Omega|^{-1}\int_{\Omega}u\,d{\bf{x}}
, we
conclude that

\overline{u}=\frac{\left(\int_{\Omega}\phi_{0}\,d{\bf{x}}\right)^{2}}{|\Omega|\lambda_{0}}+\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{\left(\int_{\Omega}\phi_{j}\,d{\bf{x}}\right)^{2}}{|\Omega|\lambda_{j}}\,,

(4.102)

where a three-term expansion for
\overline{u}
was given in
(
4.74
) of Proposition
1
.

As in
[
30
]
, the principal eigenvalue
\lambda_{0}
and
the corresponding eigenfunction
\phi_{0}
in the outer region have the expansion

\begin{split}\lambda_{0}&={\varepsilon}\lambda_{00}+{\varepsilon}^{2}\log\left(\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\right)\lambda_{01}+{\varepsilon}^{2}\lambda_{02}+{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon}^{3}\log{\varepsilon})\,,\\
\phi_{0}&=\phi_{00}+{\varepsilon}\phi_{01}+{\varepsilon}^{2}\log\left(\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\right)\phi_{02}+{\varepsilon}^{2}\phi_{03}+\ldots\,.\end{split}

(4.103)

By substituting the expansion for
\phi_{0}
into the normalization
condition in (
4.101a
), collecting powers of
{\varepsilon}
, and
ignoring negligible
{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon}^{3})
contributions from the
inner regions near the patches, we obtain that

\phi_{00}={1/|\Omega|^{1/2}}
,
\int_{\Omega}\phi_{01}\,d{\bf{x}}=0
,

\int_{\Omega}\phi_{02}\,d{\bf{x}}=0
, and
\int_{\Omega}\phi_{03}\,d{\bf{x}}\neq 0
. As a result, we estimate that

\left(\int_{\Omega}\phi_{0}\,d{\bf{x}}\right)^{2}\sim\left(|\Omega|^{1/2}+{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon}^{2})\right)^{2}\sim\Omega+{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon}^{2})\,.

(4.104)

Next, by the orthogonality property

\int_{\Omega}\phi_{j}\phi_{0}\,d{\bf{x}}=0
for
j\geq 1
, we use
(
4.103
) to estimate

0=\int_{\Omega}\phi_{j}\phi_{0}\,d{\bf{x}}\sim\frac{1}{|\Omega|^{1/2}}\int_{\Omega}\phi_{j}\,d{\bf{x}}+{\varepsilon}\int_{\Omega}\phi_{j}\phi_{0j}\,d{\bf{x}}\,,

(4.105)

which yields that

\left(\int_{\Omega}\phi_{j}\,d{\bf{x}}\right)^{2}={\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon}^{2})
for

j\geq 1
. By using this estimate and (
4.104
)
in (
4.102
), we conclude that

\overline{u}\sim\lambda_{0}^{-1}\left[1+{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon}^{2})\right]+{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon}^{2})
, which yields

\lambda_{0}\sim\frac{1+{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon}^{2})}{\overline{u}-{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon}^{2})}\,.

(4.106)

By using the expansion (
4.74
) for
\overline{u}

in (
4.106
) it follows that we can neglect the

{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon}^{2})
terms in (
4.106
), so that

\lambda_{0}\sim\frac{{\varepsilon}}{U_{0}}\left(1+{\varepsilon}\log\left(\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\right)\frac{\overline{U}_{10}}{U_{0}}+{\varepsilon}\frac{\overline{U}_{11}}{U_{0}}+{\varepsilon}^{2}\log{\varepsilon}\right)^{-1}\,.

(4.107)

Then, by using
(1+y)^{-1}\sim 1-y
, we obtain the expansion
(
4.103
) for
\lambda_{0}
in which

\lambda_{01}={1/U_{0}}
,
\lambda_{02}=-{\overline{U}_{10}/U_{0}^{2}}
and

\lambda_{03}=-{\overline{U}_{11}/U_{0}^{2}}
. Finally, by using
(
4.75
), we obtain our main result that

\lambda_{0}\sim\frac{2\pi{\varepsilon}\overline{C}}{|\Omega|}+{\varepsilon}^{2}\log\left(\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\right)\frac{\pi{\bf{C}}^{T}{\bf{C}}}{|\Omega|}\\
-\frac{2\pi{\varepsilon}^{2}}{|\Omega|}\left(2\pi{\bf{C}}^{T}{\mathcal{G}}_{s}{\bf{C}}+\overline{E}\right)+{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon}^{3}\log{\varepsilon})\,,

(4.108)

where
|\Omega|={4\pi/3}
,
{\bf{C}}=(C_{1},\ldots,C_{N})^{T}
,

\overline{C}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}C_{i}
,
\overline{E}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}E_{i}
,
and
{\mathcal{G}}_{s}
is the surface Neumann Green’s matrix, which
depends on the patch locations.

For the special case of perfectly reactive locally circular patches
for which
C_{i}={2a_{i}/\pi}
and
E_{i}=E_{i}(\infty)
is given in
(
3.40a
), we readily observe that (
4.108
)
agrees with the result in Proposition 3.1 of
[
30
]
. For
circular patches, by using our heuristic approximations in
(
3.31
) and (
3.41
) for
C_{i}
and
E_{i}
,
(
4.108
) gives an explicit three-term approximation for

\lambda_{0}
over the full range
\kappa_{i}>0
of reactivities.

5
The Splitting Probability

We use the method of matched asymptotic expansions to approximate
solutions to (
2.12
) as
{\varepsilon}\to 0
. Since the MFRT and
splitting probability problems have a similar structure, our asymptotic
analysis for (
2.12
) relies heavily on that done in
§
4
.

5.1
Asymptotic Analysis

In the outer region, we expand

u\sim U_{0}+{\varepsilon}U_{1}+{\varepsilon}^{2}\log\left(\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\right)U_{2}+{\varepsilon}^{2}U_{3}+\cdots\,,

(5.109)

where
U_{0}
is a constant to be determined and where
U_{k}
for
k\geq 1

satisfies

\Delta_{{\bf{x}}}U_{k}=0\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\Omega\,;\quad\partial_{n}U_{k}=0\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\partial\Omega\backslash\{{{\bf{x}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{x}}_{N}\}}\,.

(5.110)

The singularity behavior for
U_{k}
as
{\bf{x}}\to{\bf{x}}_{i}
will be found by
matching.

To construct the inner solution we introduce local geodesic
coordinates near each
{\bf{x}}_{i}\in\partial\Omega
to obtain
(
3.16
) with

-\partial_{y_{3}}V+\kappa_{i}V=\delta_{i1}\kappa_{i}
on
y_{3}=0
and

(y_{1},y_{2})\in{\Gamma}_{i}
. In the inner region near the
i
-th Robin patch
we expand the inner solution as

u\sim V_{0i}+{\varepsilon}\log\left(\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\right)V_{1i}+{\varepsilon}V_{i2}+\ldots\,.

(5.111)

Owing to the different boundary condition on the target

\partial\Omega_{1}^{{\varepsilon}}
, we find that
V_{0i}
satisfies

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\bf{y}}}V_{0i}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf{y}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{3}\,,

(5.112a)

\displaystyle-\partial_{y_{3}}V_{0i}+\kappa_{i}V_{0i}

\displaystyle=\delta_{i1}\kappa_{i}\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\in{\Gamma}_{i}\,,

(5.112b)

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{3}}V_{0i}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\notin{\Gamma}_{i}\,.

(5.112c)

Moreover, for
k=1,2
, we have that
V_{ki}
satisfies

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\bf{y}}}V_{ki}

\displaystyle=\delta_{k2}\left(2y_{3}V_{0i,y_{3}y_{3}}+2V_{0i,y_{3}}\right)\,,\quad{\bf{y}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{3}\,,

(5.113a)

\displaystyle-\partial_{y_{3}}V_{ki}+\kappa_{i}V_{ki}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\in{\Gamma}_{i}\,,

(5.113b)

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{3}}V_{ki}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\notin{\Gamma}_{i}\,.

(5.113c)

In terms of
w_{i}=w_{i}({\bf{y}};\kappa_{i})
, which satisfies
(
3.17
), the solution to (
5.112
) is

V_{0i}=U_{0}+\left(\delta_{i1}-U_{0}\right)w_{i}\,,\quad\mbox{for}\quad i=1,\ldots,N\,.

(5.114)

By using the far-field behavior (
3.17d
) for
w_{i}
, we have

V_{0i}\sim U_{0}+\left(\delta_{i1}-U_{0}\right)\left(\frac{C_{i}}{|{\bf{y}}|}+\frac{{\bf p}_{i}{\bf\cdot}{\bf{y}}}{|{\bf{y}}|^{3}}+\cdots\right)\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty\,.

The matching condition as
{\bf{x}}\to{\bf{x}}_{i}
and
|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty
is that

\begin{split}U_{0}+{\varepsilon}U_{1}+&{\varepsilon}^{2}\log\left(\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\right)U_{2}+{\varepsilon}^{2}U_{3}+\ldots\\
&\sim U_{0}+\left(\delta_{i1}-U_{0}\right)\left(\frac{C_{i}}{|{\bf{y}}|}+\frac{{\bf p}_{i}{\bf\cdot}{\bf{y}}}{|{\bf{y}}|^{3}}\right)+{\varepsilon}\log\left(\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\right)V_{1i}+{\varepsilon}V_{2i}+\ldots\,.\end{split}

(5.115)

Upon using
|{\bf{y}}|\sim{\varepsilon}^{-1}|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i}|
, (
5.115
) gives
the singularity behavior for
U_{1}({\bf{x}})
as
{\bf{x}}\to{\bf{x}}_{i}
.

At order
{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon})
in the outer expansion
(
5.109
) we obtain that
U_{1}({\bf{x}})
satisfies

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\bf{x}}}U_{1}=0\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\Omega\,;\quad\partial_{n}U_{1}=0\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\partial\Omega\backslash\{{{\bf{x}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{x}}_{N}\}}\,,

(5.116a)

\displaystyle U_{1}\sim-\frac{\left(U_{0}-\delta_{i1}\right)C_{i}}{|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i}|}\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad{\bf{x}}\to{\bf{x}}_{i}\in\partial\Omega\,,\quad i=1,\ldots,N\,.

(5.116b)

The solvability condition for (
5.116
) determines
U_{0}
as

U_{0}=\frac{C_{1}}{\overline{C}}\,,\quad\mbox{where}\quad\overline{C}\equiv\sum_{j=1}^{N}C_{j}\,.

(5.117)

In terms of the surface Neumann Green’s function
G_{s}
, given in
(
3.43
), the solution to (
5.116
) is

U_{1}=\overline{U}_{1}-2\pi\sum_{j=1}^{N}\left(U_{0}-\delta_{j1}\right)C_{j}G_{s}({\bf{x}};{\bf{x}}_{j})\,,\quad\mbox{where}\quad\overline{U}_{1}\equiv|\Omega|^{-1}\int_{\Omega}U_{1}\,d{\bf{x}}\,.

(5.118)

As similar to that for the MFRT problem in §
4
, we
expand
\overline{U}_{1}
as

\overline{U}_{1}=\overline{U}_{10}\log\left(\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\right)+\overline{U}_{11}\,,

(5.119)

where
\overline{U}_{10}
and
\overline{U}_{11}
are constants
independent of
{\varepsilon}
, which are to be determined.

Next, we expand
U_{1}
in (
5.118
) as
{\bf{x}}\to{\bf{x}}_{i}
to obtain,
after some algebra, that

\begin{split}U_{1}&\sim-\left(U_{0}-\delta_{i1}\right)\frac{C_{i}}{{\varepsilon}|{\bf{y}}|}+\left(\overline{U}_{10}+\frac{\left(U_{0}-\delta_{i1}\right)C_{i}}{2}\right)\log\left(\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\right)+\gamma_{i}+\overline{U}_{11}\\
&\qquad+\frac{\left(U_{0}-\delta_{i1}\right)C_{i}}{2}\left(\log(y_{3}+|{\bf{y}}|)-\frac{y_{3}(y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2})}{|{\bf{y}}|^{3}}\right)\,,\end{split}

(5.120)

where
\gamma_{i}
is the
i
-th component of the vector
{\bm{\gamma}}

defined by

{\bm{\gamma}}\equiv 2\pi C_{1}{\bf{g}}-2\pi U_{0}{\mathcal{G}}_{s}{\bf{C}}\,,\quad\mbox{where}\quad{\bf{g}}\equiv\left(R_{s},G_{12},\ldots,G_{1N}\right)^{T}\,.

(5.121)

Here
R_{s}=-{9/(20\pi)}
,
G_{1j}=G_{s}({\bf{x}}_{1};{\bf{x}}_{j})
, and
{\mathcal{G}}_{s}

is the Green’s matrix of (
4.59
).

Upon substituting (
5.120
) into the matching condition
(
5.115
), we conclude that the dominant

{\mathcal{O}}\left({\varepsilon}\log\left({{\varepsilon}/2}\right)\right)
terms
determine the far-field behavior for the inner correction
V_{1i}
in
(
5.111
). In particular, we find that
V_{1i}
satisfies
(
5.113
) with
k=1
subject to

V_{1i}\sim\overline{U}_{10}+{\left(U_{0}-\delta_{i1}\right)C_{i}/2}
as
|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty
. In terms of
w_{i}
,
satisfying (
3.17
), we obtain that
V_{1i}
is given by

V_{1i}=\left(\overline{U}_{10}+\frac{\left(U_{0}-\delta_{i1}\right)C_{i}}{2}\right)\left(1-w_{i}\right)\,.

(5.122)

We substitute (
5.122
) into the matching condition
(
5.115
), where we use
w_{i}\sim{C_{i}/|{\bf{y}}|}
as

|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty
from (
3.17d
). This provides the required
singularity behavior of the outer correction
U_{2}
in
(
5.109
). In this way, we find that
U_{2}
satisfies

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\bf{x}}}U_{2}=0\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\Omega\,;\quad\partial_{n}U_{2}=0\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\partial\Omega\backslash\{{{\bf{x}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{x}}_{N}\}}\,,

(5.123a)

\displaystyle U_{2}\sim-\left(\overline{U}_{10}+\frac{\left(U_{0}-\delta_{i1}\right)C_{i}}{2}\right)\frac{C_{i}}{|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i}|}\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad{\bf{x}}\to{\bf{x}}_{i}\in\partial\Omega\,,\quad i=1,\ldots,N\,.

(5.123b)

From the divergence theorem we find that (
5.123
) is
solvable only when
\overline{U}_{10}
satisfies

2\overline{U}_{10}\sum_{j=1}^{N}C_{j}=-\sum_{j=1}^{N}C_{j}^{2}\left(U_{0}-\delta_{j1}\right)
. Upon using
(
5.117
) for
U_{0}
, we obtain in terms of

{\bf{C}}=(C_{1},\ldots,C_{N})^{T}
and
\overline{C}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}C_{j}

that

\frac{\overline{U}_{10}}{U_{0}}=-\frac{1}{2\overline{C}}\left({\bf{C}}^{T}{\bf{C}}-\overline{C}C_{1}\right)\,.

(5.124)

Then, the solution to (
5.123
) is written in terms of an
unknown constant
\overline{U}_{2}
as

U_{2}=\overline{U}_{2}-2\pi\sum_{j=1}^{N}\left(\overline{U}_{10}+\frac{\left(U_{0}-\delta_{j1}\right)C_{j}}{2}\right)C_{j}G_{s}({\bf{x}};{\bf{x}}_{j})\,.

(5.125)

Next, we continue our expansion to higher order by substituting
(
5.120
) into (
5.115
). We conclude
that
V_{2i}
satisfies (
5.113
) with
k=2
subject to the
far-field behavior

V_{2i}\sim\frac{\left(U_{0}-\delta_{i1}\right)C_{i}}{2}\left(\log(y_{3}+|{\bf{y}}|)-\frac{y_{3}(y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2})}{|{\bf{y}}|^{3}}\right)+\gamma_{i}+\overline{U}_{11}\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty\,.

(5.126)

Since
V_{0i}=U_{0}+\left(\delta_{i1}-U_{0}\right)w_{i}
from
(
5.114
), we decompose
V_{2i}
as

V_{2i}=\left(U_{0}-\delta_{i1}\right)\left[\Phi_{2i}+\frac{\left(\gamma_{i}+\overline{U}_{11}\right)}{U_{0}-\delta_{i1}}(1-w_{i})\right]\,.

(5.127)

We conclude from (
5.113
) with
k=2
and
(
5.126
) that
\Phi_{2i}
satisfies (
4.67
)
of §
4
. As a result, the refined far-field
behavior of
\Phi_{2i}
is given by (
4.68
) in terms of

E_{i}=E_{i}(\kappa_{i})
, which is determined by the far-field behavior of
(
3.34
) (see also Appendix
C
).

To determine
\overline{U}_{11}
we will impose a solvability
condition on the problem for the outer correction
U_{3}
in
(
5.109
). To obtain the singularity behavior for
U_{3}

as
{\bf{x}}\to{\bf{x}}_{i}
, we substitute (
4.68
) and
w_{i}\sim{C_{i}/|{\bf{y}}|}
as
|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty
into (
5.127
) to
conclude that
V_{2i}
satisfies the refined far-field behavior

\begin{split}V_{2i}&\sim\gamma_{i}+\overline{U}_{11}+\frac{\left(U_{0}-\delta_{i1}\right)C_{i}}{2}\left(\log(y_{3}+|{\bf{y}}|)-\frac{y_{3}(y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2})}{|{\bf{y}}|^{3}}\right)\\
&\qquad+\frac{E_{i}\left(U_{0}-\delta_{i1}\right)}{|{\bf{y}}|}-\frac{\left(\gamma_{i}+\overline{U}_{11}\right)C_{i}}{|{\bf{y}}|}\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty\,.\end{split}

(5.128)

The second line in (
5.128
) is one of the two terms
that provides the singularity behavior for
U_{3}
in the matching
condition (
5.115
). The other term is the dipole term
given in (
5.115
), which is written in terms of outer
variables using (
A.255
) of Appendix
A
.

In this way, upon substituting (
5.128
) into the
matching condition (
5.115
), we conclude that
U_{3}
in the
outer expansion (
5.109
) must satisfy

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\bf{x}}}U_{3}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\Omega\,;\quad\partial_{n}U_{3}=0\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\partial\Omega\backslash\{{{\bf{x}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{x}}_{N}\}}\,,

(5.129a)

\displaystyle U_{3}

\displaystyle\sim\frac{E_{i}\left(U_{0}-\delta_{i1}\right)}{|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i}|}-\frac{\left(\gamma_{i}+\overline{U}_{11}\right)C_{i}}{|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i}|}

\displaystyle\quad+\left(\delta_{i1}-U_{0}\right)\frac{{\bf p}_{i}{\bf\cdot}{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}^{T}({\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i})}{|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i}|^{3}}\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad{\bf{x}}\to{\bf{x}}_{i}\in\partial\Omega\,,\quad i=1,\ldots,N\,.

(5.129b)

From the divergence theorem, (
5.129
) has a solution if
and only if
\sum_{j=1}^{N}E_{j}\left(U_{0}-\delta_{j1}\right)=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\left(\gamma_{j}+\overline{U}_{11}\right)C_{j}
, where we
observe that the contribution from the dipole term again vanishes
identically. By using (
5.121
) for
\gamma_{i}
, we solve
for
\overline{U}_{11}
as

\overline{U}_{11}\overline{C}=U_{0}\sum_{j=1}^{N}E_{j}-E_{1}+2\pi U_{0}{\bf{C}}^{T}{\mathcal{G}}_{s}{\bf{C}}-2\pi C_{1}\left({\mathcal{G}}_{s}{\bf{C}}\right)_{1}\,,

which yields

\overline{U}_{11}=\frac{U_{0}\sum_{j=1}^{N}E_{j}}{\overline{C}}-\frac{E_{1}}{\overline{C}}+\frac{2\pi C_{1}}{\overline{C}}\left(\frac{{\bf{C}}^{T}{\mathcal{G}}_{s}{\bf{C}}}{\overline{C}}-\left({\mathcal{G}}_{s}{\bf{C}}\right)_{1}\right)\,.

(5.130)

We summarize our main result for the splitting probability
u({\bf{x}})
and
the volume-averaged splitting probability
\overline{u}=|\Omega|^{-1}\int_{\Omega}u({\bf{x}})\,d{\bf{x}}
in the small-patch
limit as follows:

Proposition 2
.

For
{\varepsilon}\to 0
, the asymptotic solution to (
2.12
) is
given in the outer region
|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i}|\gg{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon})
for

i=1,\ldots,N
by

\begin{split}u({\bf{x}})&\sim U_{0}\left[1+{\varepsilon}\log\left(\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\right)\frac{\overline{U}_{10}}{U_{0}}+{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{\overline{U}_{11}}{U_{0}}+2\pi\sum_{j=1}^{N}\frac{\left(\delta_{j1}-U_{0}\right)}{U_{0}}C_{j}G_{s}({\bf{x}};{\bf{x}}_{j})\right)\right.\\
&\left.+{\varepsilon}^{2}\log\left(\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\right)\left(\frac{\overline{U}_{2}}{U_{0}}+2\pi\sum_{j=1}^{N}C_{j}\left(\frac{C_{j}\left(\delta_{j1}-U_{0}\right)}{2U_{0}}-\frac{\overline{U}_{10}}{U_{0}}\right)G_{s}({\bf{x}};{\bf{x}}_{j})\right)+{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon}^{2})\right].\end{split}

(5.131)

The volume-averaged splitting probability
\overline{u}
satisfies

\overline{u}\sim U_{0}\left[1+{\varepsilon}\log\left(\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\right)\frac{\overline{U}_{10}}{U_{0}}+{\varepsilon}\frac{\overline{U}_{11}}{U_{0}}+{\mathcal{O}}\left({\varepsilon}^{2}\log{\varepsilon}\right)\right]\,.

(5.132)

In (
5.131
) and (
5.132
),
U_{0}
,

\overline{U}_{10}
and
\overline{U}_{11}
are determined in terms of

{\bf{C}}=(C_{1},\ldots,C_{N})^{T}
,
\overline{C}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}C_{j}
,

\overline{E}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}E_{j}
, and the Green’s matrix

{\mathcal{G}}_{s}
from (
4.59
) by

\begin{split}U_{0}&=\frac{C_{1}}{\overline{C}}\,,\qquad\frac{\overline{U}_{10}}{U_{0}}=-\frac{1}{2\overline{C}}\left({\bf{C}}^{T}{\bf{C}}-C_{1}\overline{C}\right)\,,\\
\frac{\overline{U}_{11}}{U_{0}}&=\left(\frac{\overline{E}}{\overline{C}}-\frac{E_{1}}{C_{1}}\right)+2\pi\left(\frac{{\bf{C}}^{T}{\mathcal{G}}_{s}{\bf{C}}}{\overline{C}}-\left({\mathcal{G}}_{s}{\bf{C}}\right)_{1}\right)\,.\end{split}

(5.133)

In (
5.131
),
\overline{U}_{2}
can only be found at
higher order,
C_{i}=C_{i}(\kappa_{i})
is obtained from (
3.17
),
while
E_{i}=E_{i}(\kappa_{i})
is given by (
3.36
) for
arbitrary-shaped patches and by (
3.38
) when the patches
are disks. Their asymptotic behaviors for small and large reactivities
are given in Lemmas
1
and
2
.
When the patches are disks, heuristic approximations for
C_{i}
and

E_{i}
valid for all
\kappa_{i}>0
are given in (
3.31
)
and (
3.41
).

To obtain the corresponding result in terms of dimensional variables,
we use (
2.8
) to conclude that we need only replace

C_{i}=C_{i}\left({L{\mathcal{K}}_{i}/D}\right)
and
E_{i}=E_{i}\left({L{\mathcal{K}}_{i}/D}\right)
in
(
5.132
) and (
5.133
), while
evaluating the Green’s matrix
{\mathcal{G}}_{s}
at
{\bf{x}}_{i}={{\bf X}_{i}/R}
.

We remark that in the limit
\kappa_{i}\to 0
for
i=1,\ldots,N
, it
follows from Lemma
1
and Lemma
2

that
C_{i}\sim{\kappa_{i}a_{i}^{2}/2}
and
E_{i}={\mathcal{O}}(\kappa_{i}^{2})
. In this limit, we obtain from
(
5.133
) that
U_{0}={\mathcal{O}}(1)
, while both

{\overline{U}_{10}/U_{0}}
and
{\overline{U}_{11}/U_{0}}
are

{\mathcal{O}}(\kappa_{i})
as
\kappa_{i}\to 0
. As a result, we conclude
that the asymptotic expansions in (
5.131
) and
(
5.132
) remain well-ordered in
{\varepsilon}
in the limit

\kappa_{i}\to 0
for
i=1,\ldots,N
. Moreover, since

U_{0}\approx\frac{\kappa_{1}a_{1}^{2}}{\kappa_{1}a_{1}^{2}+\cdots+\kappa_{N}a_{N}^{2}}\,,\quad\mbox{for}\quad\kappa_{i}\ll 1\,,\quad i=1,\ldots,N\,,

(5.134)

we observe that the leading-order splitting probability is determined
by the relative reactive surface
\kappa_{1}a_{1}^{2}
of the first patch
as compared to other patches. When all reactivities
{\mathcal{K}}_{i}
are finite
and fixed, one has
\kappa_{i}={\varepsilon}{\mathcal{K}}_{i}R/D\to 0
as
{\varepsilon}\to 0
, so
that

\overline{U}\sim U_{0}\approx\frac{|{\partial\Omega}_{1}|{\mathcal{K}}_{1}}{|{\partial\Omega}_{1}|{\mathcal{K}}_{1}+\cdots+|{\partial\Omega}_{N}|{\mathcal{K}}_{N}}\,,

(5.135)

to leading order in
{\varepsilon}
. This shows that to leading-order the
trapping capacity of the
i
-th patch is simply the product of its
reactivity and surface area. We emphasize that this approximate
relation is not valid if at least one reactivity is infinite (see a
numerical example in the next subsection).

5.2
Numerical Example

As an example, we consider two circular patches located at the north
and south poles of the unit sphere. Figure
6

illustrates how the volume-averaged splitting probability

\overline{u}
depends on the reactivity
\kappa_{1}=\kappa
of the
first patch, where the second patch is assumed to be perfectly
reactive (
\kappa_{2}=\infty
). We consider two scenarios: two
patches of the same radius (i.e.,
a_{1}=a_{2}=1
), and two patches of
different radii (
0.5=a_{2}<a_{1}=1
). In both cases, the
asymptotic result (
5.132
) is in very close
agreement with the FEM solution of the BVP (
2.12
) over a
broad interval of reactivities, ranging from
10^{-2}
to
10^{2}
.
When two patches are of the same radius, the splitting probability
approaches
1/2
in the limit
\kappa\to\infty
. Curiously, even for
a weak reactivity (e.g.,
\kappa=0.1
), the first patch has a
non-negligible chance of capturing the particle.

Figure 6
:

The volume-averaged splitting probability
\overline{u}
to the first
circular patch of reactivity
\kappa_{1}=\kappa
in the presence of
the second circular patch of infinite reactivity (
\kappa_{2}=\infty
). Two patches are located at the north and south poles of the
unit sphere. Two configurations are considered: (i) patches of
equal radii (
a_{1}=a_{2}=1
,
{\varepsilon}=0.2
) and (ii) patches of
different radii (
a_{1}=1
,
a_{2}=0.5
,
{\varepsilon}=0.4
). The curves
present the asymptotic formula (
5.132
), while the
symbols indicate a FEM solution with the maximal meshsize
h_{\rm max}=0.0025
.

6
The Steklov-Dirichlet-Neumann (SDN)
Eigenvalue Problem

Next, we analyze the SDN eigenvalue problem (
2.13
) in the
unit sphere
\Omega
, where we refer to
\partial\Omega_{1}^{{\varepsilon}}
as
the Steklov patch and to
\partial\Omega_{i}^{{\varepsilon}}
for
i=2,\ldots,N

as Dirichlet patches. As previously, each boundary patch

\partial\Omega_{i}^{{\varepsilon}}
having diameter
{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon})\ll 1
is
assumed to be simply-connected with a smooth boundary, but with an
otherwise arbitrary shape and satisfies

\partial\Omega_{i}^{{\varepsilon}}\to{\bf{x}}_{i}\in\partial\Omega
. For convenience,
in our analysis below we will normalize the Steklov eigenfunctions of
(
2.13
) by

\int_{\partial\Omega_{1}^{{\varepsilon}}}u^{2}\,d{\bf s}=1\,,

(6.136)

where the surface area element on the unit sphere in geodesic
coordinates is
d{\bf s}=\cos(\xi_{1})d\xi_{1}d\xi_{2}
(see Appendix

A
).

As in §
4
and §
5
, the dependence
of the inner solution near the Steklov patch
{\Gamma}_{1}
on the spectral
parameter
\sigma
at each order in
{\varepsilon}
will rely on properties of
the parameterized solution
w_{1}\equiv w_{1}({\bf{y}};-\sigma)
to the
canonical problem (
3.17
). Here the dependences of the
monopole term
C_{1}
and the dipole term
{\bf p}_{1}
on
\sigma
are
obtained by setting
\kappa_{1}=-\sigma
in (
3.17
). However,
the “negative reactivity”
\kappa_{1}<0
presents the crucial
difference with respect to the previously considered problems in §
4
and §
5
. In fact, from the
Steklov spectral representation (
3.19
), it follows that

C_{1}(-\sigma)
has simple poles at the eigenvalues
\mu_{k1}
, with

k\geq 0
, of the local Steklov eigenvalue problem (
D.294
),
which is defined near the patch
{\Gamma}_{1}
, with nontrivial spectral
weights
d_{k1}\neq 0
(see Appendix
D
for details; we
also recall Fig.
2
where the poles of
C_{1}
were shown for
a circular patch of unit radius). We label this resonant set as

{\mathcal{P}}_{1}\equiv\bigcup\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\left\{\mu_{k1}~|~\textrm{if}~d_{k1}\neq 0\right\}\,,

(6.137)

(i.e., this set includes only the indices
k
for which
d_{k1}\neq 0
). As an eigenvalue
\sigma=\sigma({\varepsilon})
of the SDN problem
(
2.13
) depends on
{\varepsilon}
, a small-
{\varepsilon}
expansion of

w_{1}({\bf{y}};-\sigma({\varepsilon}))
would naturally lead to
w_{1}({\bf{y}};-\sigma_{0})
,
where
\sigma_{0}=\lim\limits_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}\sigma({\varepsilon})
. However,
the solution
w_{1}(y;-\sigma_{0})
exists only when
\sigma_{0}\notin{\mathcal{P}}_{1}
. This preliminary consideration clarifies the need to
distinguish between two cases in our analysis below:

(I)
\sigma_{0}\notin{\mathcal{P}}_{1}
, in which case an eigenpair

\{\sigma,u\}
is called
non-resonant
;

(II)
\sigma_{0}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{1}
, in which case an eigenpair

\{\sigma,u\}
is called
near-resonant
.

In the next subsection, we will determine the asymptotic behavior of
the non-resonant SDN eigenvalues. In turn, in §
6.4
,
we will show that near-resonant eigenvalues do not exist for the SDN
problem (
2.13
) with a single Steklov patch. In other words,
the near-resonant case is not possible for this SDN problem. In
contrast, this case will re-appear in our analysis in §
7

for the SN problem (
2.14
) with multiple Steklov patches.

Before undertaking our asymptotic analysis, we outline an important
auxiliary result related to the Steklov patch.
When
\sigma\notin{\mathcal{P}}_{1}
, we observe upon substituting

\kappa_{1}=-\sigma
into (
3.17
) and differentiating it with
respect to
\sigma
that

w_{c1}=w_{c1}({\bf{y}};-\sigma)\equiv\partial_{\sigma}w_{1}({\bf{y}};-\sigma)

(6.138)

satisfies

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\bf{y}}}w_{c1}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf{y}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{3}\,,

(6.139a)

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{3}}w_{c1}+\sigma w_{c1}

\displaystyle=1-w_{1}\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\in{\Gamma}_{1}\,,

(6.139b)

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{3}}w_{c1}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\notin{\Gamma}_{1}\,,

(6.139c)

\displaystyle w_{c1}

\displaystyle\sim-\frac{C_{1}^{\prime}(-\sigma)}{|{\bf{y}}|}-{\frac{{\bf p}_{1}^{\prime}(-\sigma){\bf\cdot}{\bf{y}}}{|{\bf{y}}|^{3}}}+\cdots\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty\,,

(6.139d)

where we have defined

C_{1}^{\prime}(-\sigma)\equiv\left.\left(\frac{dC_{1}(\kappa)}{d\kappa}\right)\right|_{\kappa=-\sigma}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad{\bf p}_{1}^{\prime}(-\sigma)\equiv\left.\left(\frac{d{\bf p}_{1}(\kappa)}{d\kappa}\right)\right|_{\kappa=-\sigma}\,.

(6.139e)

The identification of this problem satisfied by
w_{c1}
is key for
solving the different inner problems at each order of the inner
expansion near the Steklov patch
{\Gamma}_{1}
. A general spectral
representation (
D.304
) for
w_{c1}
is established in
Appendix
D
.

6.1
Asymptotic Analysis

In the outer region, we expand the solution to (
2.13
) away
from all the patches as

u\sim U_{0}+{\varepsilon}U_{1}+{\varepsilon}^{2}\log\left(\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\right)U_{2}+{\varepsilon}^{2}U_{3}+\cdots\,.

(6.140)

We will initially seek a solution where
U_{0}\neq 0
is a
constant. This non-zero leading-order outer solution will be used
below to satisfy the normalization condition (
6.136
). In
Remark
2
below we will briefly discuss whether one can
find SDN eigenpairs for the case where
U_{0}=0
.

Upon substituting (
6.140
) into (
2.13
) and
(
6.136
) we obtain that
U_{j}
for
j=1,2,3
satisfies the
outer problems

\Delta_{{\bf{x}}}U_{j}=0\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\Omega\,;\qquad\partial_{n}U_{j}=0\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\partial\Omega\backslash\{{{\bf{x}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{x}}_{N}\}}\,.

(6.141)

Our analysis below provides singularity behaviors for each
U_{j}

as
{\bf{x}}\to{\bf{x}}_{i}
.

The novel feature of our analysis of (
2.13
) is that
each Steklov eigenvalue
\sigma=\sigma({\varepsilon})
must be expanded as

\sigma=\sigma({\varepsilon})=\sigma_{0}+{\varepsilon}\log\left(\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\right)\sigma_{1}+{\varepsilon}\sigma_{2}+\ldots\,.

(6.142)

The coefficients
\sigma_{j}
for
j=0,1,2
, which are
independent of
{\varepsilon}
, will be determined below by ensuring that the
outer problems are solvable at each order. We emphasize that by using
(
6.142
) in (
2.13b
) we will obtain distinct
boundary conditions on the Steklov patch at each order of the inner
expansion.

In the inner region near the
i
-th patch we introduce the local
geodesic coordinates (
3.15
) and expand each inner
solution as

u\sim V_{0i}+{\varepsilon}\log\left(\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\right)V_{1i}+{\varepsilon}V_{i2}+\ldots\,.

(6.143)

For each Dirichlet patch with
i=2,\ldots,N
, we readily
obtain that
V_{ji}
for
j=0,1,2
satisfies

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\bf{y}}}V_{ji}

\displaystyle=\delta_{j2}\left(2y_{3}V_{0i,y_{3}y_{3}}+2V_{0i,y_{3}}\right)\,,\quad{\bf{y}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{3}\,,

(6.144a)

\displaystyle V_{ji}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\in{\Gamma}_{i}\,,

(6.144b)

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{3}}V_{ji}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\notin{\Gamma}_{i}\,,

(6.144c)

where
\delta_{22}=1
,
\delta_{j2}=0
if
j=0,1
, and

{\Gamma}_{i}\asymp{\varepsilon}^{-1}\partial\Omega_{i}^{{\varepsilon}}
. In contrast, on the
re-scaled Steklov patch
{\Gamma}_{1}\asymp{\varepsilon}^{-1}\partial\Omega_{1}^{{\varepsilon}}
, we
obtain that
V_{j1}
for
j=0,1,2
satisfies

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\bf{y}}}V_{j1}

\displaystyle=\delta_{j2}\left(2y_{3}V_{01,y_{3}y_{3}}+2V_{01,y_{3}}\right)\,,\quad{\bf{y}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{3}\,,

(6.145a)

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{3}}V_{j1}+\sigma_{0}V_{j1}

\displaystyle=-(1-\delta_{j0})\sigma_{j}V_{01}\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\in{\Gamma}_{1}\,,

(6.145b)

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{3}}V_{j1}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\notin{\Gamma}_{1}\,.

(6.145c)

Since the leading-order matching condition between inner and outer
solutions is that
V_{0i}\sim U_{0}
as
|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty
, the leading-order
inner solution, obtained from (
6.144
) and (
6.145
), is

V_{0i}=U_{0}\left(1-w_{0i}\right)\,.

(6.146)

In (
6.146
),
w_{0i}({\bf{y}})
is defined in terms of the solution

w_{i}({\bf{y}};\kappa_{i})
to (
3.17
) by

w_{0i}({\bf{y}})\equiv\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}w_{1}\left({\bf{y}};-\sigma_{0}\right)\,,&i=1,\\
w_{i}({\bf{y}};\infty)\,,&i=2,\ldots,N.\end{array}\right.

(6.147)

Our assumption
\sigma_{0}\notin{\mathcal{P}}_{1}
implies that

C_{1}(-\sigma_{0})
and
w_{1}({\bf{y}};-\sigma_{0})
are well-defined.

Upon using (
3.17d
) to obtain the far-field behavior for

w_{0i}
, we match the inner and outer expansions to conclude that the
outer correction
U_{1}
in (
6.140
) satisfies

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\bf{x}}}U_{1}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\Omega\,;\qquad\partial_{n}U_{1}=0\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\partial\Omega\backslash\{{{\bf{x}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{x}}_{N}\}}\,,

(6.148a)

\displaystyle U_{1}

\displaystyle\sim-\frac{C_{1}\left(-\sigma_{0}\right)U_{0}}{|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{1}|}\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad{\bf{x}}\to{\bf{x}}_{1}\in\partial\Omega\,,

(6.148b)

\displaystyle U_{1}

\displaystyle\sim-\frac{C_{i}(\infty)U_{0}}{|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i}|}\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad{\bf{x}}\to{\bf{x}}_{i}\in\partial\Omega\,,\quad i=2,\ldots,N\,.

(6.148c)

The solvability condition for (
6.148
), together with the
assumption that
U_{0}\neq 0
, provides the following nonlinear
algebraic equation for the leading-order term
\sigma_{0}
in the
expansion (
6.142
) of a Steklov eigenvalue:

C_{1}\left(-\sigma_{0}\right)={\mathcal{N}}\equiv-\sum_{i=2}^{N}C_{i}(\infty)\,.

(6.149)

Since
C_{i}(\infty)>0
, we conclude that
{\mathcal{N}}<0
. The
spectral expansion (
3.19
) ensures that, for any

{\mathcal{N}}<0
, (
6.149
) has infinitely many solutions
that we denote as
\sigma_{0}^{(k)}
, with
k=0,1,\ldots
. Moreover,
since
C_{1}(-\sigma_{0})
decreases monotonically in
\sigma_{0}
between
its poles in
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}
, each solution
\sigma_{0}^{(k)}
is
simple and lies between two consecutive poles. In our analysis below,
we will determine the next-order corrections
\sigma_{1}^{(k)}
and

\sigma_{2}^{(k)}
to the dominant contribution
\sigma_{0}^{(k)}
. As
our asymptotic analysis is applicable to any
k
, we omit the
superscript
(k)
for brevity.

With
\sigma_{0}
determined by (
6.149
), the solution to
(
6.148
) is represented in terms of an unknown constant

\overline{U}_{1}
as

U_{1}({\bf{x}})=\overline{U}_{1}-2\pi U_{0}\sum_{j=1}^{N}C_{j}G_{s}({\bf{x}};{\bf{x}}_{j})\,,\quad\mbox{where}\quad C_{j}\equiv\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}C_{1}\left(-\sigma_{0}\right)\,,&j=1\\
C_{j}(\infty)\,,&j=2,\ldots,N\,.\end{array}\right.

(6.150)

Here
G_{s}
is the surface Neumann Green’s function for the sphere given
in (
3.43
).

To proceed to higher order, we expand
U_{1}({\bf{x}})
in (
6.150
)
as
{\bf{x}}\to{\bf{x}}_{i}
to obtain in terms of local geodesic coordinates that

\begin{split}U_{1}&\sim-\frac{C_{i}U_{0}}{|{\bf{y}}|}+\frac{U_{0}C_{i}}{2}\log\left(\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\right)+\frac{U_{0}C_{i}}{2}\left(\log(y_{3}+|{\bf{y}}|)-\frac{y_{3}(y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2})}{|{\bf{y}}|^{3}}\right)\\
&\qquad+U_{0}\beta_{i}+\overline{U}_{1}\,,\end{split}

(6.151)

where
\beta_{i}
is the
i
-th component of the vector
{\bm{\beta}}

defined by

{\bm{\beta}}\equiv-2\pi{\mathcal{G}}_{s}{\bf{C}}\,,\qquad\mbox{where}\qquad{\bf{C}}=(C_{1},\ldots,C_{N})^{T}\,.

(6.152)

Here
C_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,N
is defined in (
6.150
) and

{\mathcal{G}}_{s}
is the Green’s matrix in (
4.59
).

Upon matching the inner and outer expansions for the

{\varepsilon}\log\left({{\varepsilon}/2}\right)
terms, we conclude that the inner
correction
V_{1i}
in (
6.143
) must satisfy

V_{1i}\sim{U_{0}C_{i}/2}
as
|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty
. As a result, we seek a
solution to (
6.144
) and (
6.145
) for
k=1
in the
form

V_{1i}=\frac{U_{0}C_{i}}{2}\left(1-w_{1i}\right)\,,\quad\mbox{for}\quad i=1,\ldots,N\,.

(6.153)

By using the problem (
6.139
) satisfied by

w_{c1}({\bf{y}};-\sigma_{0})
to account for the inhomogeneous term in
(
6.145b
) on the Steklov patch, we use superposition to get
that

\displaystyle V_{1i}

\displaystyle=\frac{U_{0}C_{i}(\infty)}{2}\left(1-w_{i}({\bf{y}};\infty)\right)\,,\quad i=2,\ldots,N\,,

(6.154a)

\displaystyle V_{11}

\displaystyle=-U_{0}\sigma_{1}w_{c1}({\bf{y}};-\sigma_{0})+\frac{U_{0}C_{1}(-\sigma_{0})}{2}\left(1-w_{1}({\bf{y}};-\sigma_{0})\right)\,.

(6.154b)

Then, by using the far-field behaviors in (
3.17d
) and
(
6.139d
), we find for
|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty
that

\displaystyle V_{1i}

\displaystyle\sim\frac{U_{0}C_{i}(\infty)}{2}\left(1-\frac{C_{i}(\infty)}{|{\bf{y}}|}\right)+\cdots\,,\quad i=2,\ldots,N\,,

(6.155a)

\displaystyle V_{11}

\displaystyle\sim U_{0}\sigma_{1}\frac{C_{1}^{\prime}(-\sigma_{0})}{|{\bf{y}}|}+\frac{U_{0}C_{1}(-\sigma_{0})}{2}\left(1-\frac{C_{1}(-\sigma_{0})}{|{\bf{y}}|}\right)+\cdots\,,

(6.155b)

where the neglected higher-order terms are dipole contributions.

Upon matching the monopole terms in the far-field behavior
(
6.155
) to the outer correction
U_{2}
in
(
6.140
), we obtain from (
6.141
) that
U_{2}
satisfies

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\bf{x}}}U_{2}=0\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\Omega\,;\qquad\partial_{n}U_{2}=0\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\partial\Omega\backslash\{{{\bf{x}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{x}}_{N}\}}\,,

(6.156a)

\displaystyle U_{2}\sim-\frac{U_{0}}{2}\frac{C_{i}^{2}}{|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i}|}+U_{0}\sigma_{1}\delta_{i1}\frac{C_{1}^{\prime}(-\sigma_{0})}{|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i}|}\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad{\bf{x}}\to{\bf{x}}_{i}\in\partial\Omega\,,\quad i=1,\ldots,N\,,

(6.156b)

where
C_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,N
is defined in (
6.150
) and

\delta_{i1}=1
if
i=1
and
\delta_{i1}=0
for
i=2,\ldots,N
. The
solvability condition for (
6.156
) yields

U_{0}\sum_{i=1}^{N}C_{i}^{2}=2U_{0}\sigma_{1}C_{1}^{\prime}(-\sigma_{0})
. For

U_{0}\neq 0
, this expression determines the coefficient

\sigma_{1}
in (
6.142
) as

\sigma_{1}=\frac{1}{2C_{1}^{\prime}(-\sigma_{0})}\left(\left[C_{1}(-\sigma_{0})\right]^{2}+\sum_{i=2}^{N}\left[C_{i}(\infty)\right]^{2}\right)\,,

(6.157)

where
\sigma_{0}
is a root of (
6.149
).
Upon using (
6.149
) directly in (
6.157
),
we can write
\sigma_{1}
equivalently as

\sigma_{1}=\frac{1}{2C_{1}^{\prime}(-\sigma_{0})}\left(\left[\sum_{i=2}^{N}C_{i}(\infty)\right]^{2}+\sum_{i=2}^{N}\left[C_{i}(\infty)\right]^{2}\right)\,.

(6.158)

From (
3.21
) we recall that
C_{1}(\kappa)
increases
monotonically in
\kappa
between its poles so that

C_{1}^{\prime}(\kappa)
, evaluated at
\kappa=-\sigma_{0}
, is strictly
positive. As a result, the denominator in (
6.158
) never
vanishes, and
\sigma_{1}
is well-defined and strictly positive. Then,
the solution to (
6.156
) is represented in terms of an
unknown constant
\overline{U}_{2}
as

U_{2}({\bf{x}})=\overline{U}_{2}-2\pi U_{0}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\left(\frac{C_{j}^{2}}{2}-\sigma_{1}C_{1}^{\prime}(-\sigma_{0})\delta_{j1}\right)G_{s}({\bf{x}};{\bf{x}}_{j})\,.

(6.159)

Finally, we calculate the eigenvalue correction
\sigma_{2}
in
(
6.142
). To do so, we observe from the matching condition
between inner and outer solutions that the
{\mathcal{O}}(1)
terms in
(
6.151
) provide the following far-field behavior for the
inner correction
V_{2i}
in (
6.143
):

V_{2i}\sim\frac{U_{0}C_{i}}{2}\left(\log(y_{3}+|{\bf{y}}|)-\frac{y_{3}(y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2})}{|{\bf{y}}|^{3}}\right)+U_{0}\beta_{i}+\overline{U}_{1}\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty\,.

(6.160)

For the Dirichlet patches, and as similar to the
analysis for the MFRT and splitting probability problems, the solution
to (
6.144
) with
k=2
subject to (
6.160
) is

V_{2i}=U_{0}\Phi_{2i}+\left(U_{0}\beta_{i}+\overline{U}_{1}\right)\left(1-w_{i}({\bf{y}};\infty)\right)\,,\quad\mbox{for}\quad i=2,\ldots,N\,,

(6.161)

where
\Phi_{2i}
satisfies (
4.67
) with
\kappa_{i}=\infty

so that
\Phi_{2i}=0
on
{\Gamma}_{i}
. As a result, by using
(
4.68
) for the far-field behavior of
\Phi_{2i}
, we
conclude for
i=2,\ldots,N
that
V_{2i}
has the refined far-field
behavior

\begin{split}V_{2i}&\sim U_{0}\beta_{i}+\overline{U}_{1}+\frac{U_{0}C_{i}(\infty)}{2}\left(\log(y_{3}+|{\bf{y}}|)-\frac{y_{3}(y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2})}{|{\bf{y}}|^{3}}\right)\\
&\qquad+\left[E_{i}(\infty)-\left(\beta_{i}+\frac{\overline{U}_{1}}{U_{0}}\right)C_{i}(\infty)\right]\frac{U_{0}}{|{\bf{y}}|}\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty\,.\end{split}

(6.162)

For an arbitrarily-shaped patch,
E_{i}(\infty)
is obtained by setting

\kappa_{i}=\infty
in (
3.36
), while for a locally
circular patch it is given in (
3.38
) of Lemma

2
. The second line in (
6.162
)
is one of the two terms that needs to be accounted for by the outer
correction
U_{3}
in the matching condition. The other term is the
dipole contribution from (
3.17d
) and (
6.146
).

In contrast, for the Steklov patch, we use superposition to determine
that the solution to (
6.145
) with
k=2
subject to
(
6.160
) is

V_{21}=U_{0}\Phi_{21}+\left(U_{0}\beta_{1}+\overline{U}_{1}\right)\left(1-w_{1}({\bf{y}};-\sigma_{0})\right)-U_{0}\sigma_{2}w_{c1}({\bf{y}};-\sigma_{0})\,,

(6.163)

where
\Phi_{21}
satisfies (
4.67
) in which we set

\kappa_{1}=-\sigma_{0}
and
C_{1}=C_{1}(-\sigma_{0})
. As a
result, the refined far-field behavior of
V_{21}
is

\begin{split}V_{21}&\sim U_{0}\beta_{1}+\overline{U}_{1}+\frac{U_{0}C_{1}(-\sigma_{0})}{2}\left(\log(y_{3}+|{\bf{y}}|)-\frac{y_{3}(y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2})}{|{\bf{y}}|^{3}}\right)\\
&\qquad+\bigl[E_{1}(-\sigma_{0})-\left(\beta_{1}+\frac{\overline{U}_{1}}{U_{0}}\right)C_{1}(-\sigma_{0})+\sigma_{2}C_{1}^{\prime}(-\sigma_{0})\bigr]\frac{U_{0}}{|{\bf{y}}|}\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty\,.\end{split}

(6.164)

Here
E_{1}(-\sigma_{0})
is calculated by setting
\kappa_{i}=-\sigma_{0}
in (
3.36
).

The monopole terms in the far-field behaviors
(
6.164
) and (
6.162
) together
with the dipole terms from the leading order inner solutions
V_{0i}

in (
6.146
) provide the required singularity behavior for the
outer correction
U_{3}
in (
6.140
). In this way, we obtain
from (
6.141
) that
U_{3}
satisfies

\begin{split}\Delta_{{\bf{x}}}U_{3}&=0\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\Omega\,;\qquad\partial_{n}U_{3}=0\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\partial\Omega\backslash\{{{\bf{x}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{x}}_{N}\}}\,,\\
U_{3}&\sim\left[E_{i}-\left(\beta_{i}+\frac{\overline{U}_{1}}{U_{0}}\right)C_{i}+\sigma_{2}C_{1}^{\prime}(-\sigma_{0})\delta_{i1}\right]\frac{U_{0}}{|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i}|}\\
&\qquad-U_{0}\frac{{\bf p}_{i}{\bf\cdot}{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}^{T}({\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i})}{|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i}|^{3}}\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad{\bf{x}}\to{\bf{x}}_{i}\in\partial\Omega\,,\quad i=1,\ldots,N\,,\end{split}

(6.165)

where the orthogonal matrix
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}
is defined in
(
A.255
) in terms of the basis vectors of the geodesic
coordinate system. In (
6.165
) we have defined
E_{i}
and

{\bf p}_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,N
by

E_{i}\equiv\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}E_{1}(-\sigma_{0})\,,&i=1,\\
E_{i}(\infty)\,,&i=2,\ldots,N\,,\end{array}\right.\qquad{\bf p}_{i}\equiv\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}{\bf p}_{1}(-\sigma_{0})\,,&i=1,\\
{\bf p}_{i}(\infty)\,,&i=2,\ldots,N.\end{array}\right.

(6.166)

Here both
{\bf p}_{i}(\infty)
and
{\bf p}_{i}(-\sigma_{0})
are defined
by (
3.17d
).

In deriving the solvability condition for (
6.165
), we
observe that the dipole terms again do not contribute and we obtain

U_{0}\sum_{i=1}^{N}E_{i}-U_{0}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\beta_{i}C_{i}-\overline{U}_{1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}C_{i}+U_{0}\sigma_{2}C_{1}^{\prime}(-\sigma_{0})=0\,,

(6.167)

where
\sum_{i=1}^{N}C_{i}=0
from the leading-order result
(
6.149
) for
\sigma_{0}
. As a result, (
6.167
)
shows that
\sigma_{2}
is independent of the constant

\overline{U}_{1}
. This term is determined by a higher-order evaluation
of the normalization condition for the Steklov eigenfunction.

Then, by using (
6.152
) for
\beta_{i}
, we solve (
6.167
)
to determine
\sigma_{2}
as

\sigma_{2}=-\frac{1}{C_{1}^{\prime}(-\sigma_{0})}\left[2\pi{\bf{C}}^{T}{\mathcal{G}}_{s}{\bf{C}}+\sum_{i=1}^{N}E_{i}\right]\,,\quad\mbox{where}\quad{\bf{C}}\equiv(C_{1},\ldots,C_{N})^{T}\,,

(6.168)

where
{\mathcal{G}}_{s}
is the Green’s matrix in
(
4.59
). Through this Green’s matrix, it follows that

\sigma_{2}
depends on the spatial configuration of the Steklov and
Dirichlet patches. We summarize our result as follows:

Proposition 3
.

For
{\varepsilon}\to 0
, there are eigenvalues
\sigma=\sigma({\varepsilon})
of the
Steklov-Dirichlet-Neumann problem (
2.13
) that have the
three-term asymptotics

\displaystyle\sigma

\displaystyle=\sigma_{0}+{\varepsilon}\log\left(\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\right)\sigma_{1}+{\varepsilon}\sigma_{2}+{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon}^{2}\log{\varepsilon})\,,

(6.169a)

where
\sigma_{0}
,
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
are respectively
given by (
6.149
), (
6.158
) and
(
6.168
). The corresponding eigenfunctions, restricted to

{\Gamma}_{1}
, have the three-term asymptotics

u|_{{\Gamma}_{1}}=V_{01}+{\varepsilon}\log\left(\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\right)V_{11}+{\varepsilon}V_{21}+{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon}^{2}\log{\varepsilon})\,,

(6.169b)

where
V_{01}
,
V_{11}
and
V_{21}
are respectively given by
(
6.146
), (
6.154b
) and (
6.163
). Here

u|_{{\Gamma}_{1}}
is given up to normalization constants
U_{0}\neq 0

and
\overline{U}_{1}
for the eigenfunction, while its spatial behavior
is determined by the functions
w_{1}({\bf{y}};-\sigma_{0})
and

w_{c1}({\bf{y}};-\sigma_{0})
, which admit the spectral expansions
(
D.302
) and (
D.304
), respectively. For
a circular patch, these expansions allow for their efficient numerical
computation (see Appendix
D
).

We emphasize that with our assumption
U_{0}\neq 0
, the Steklov
eigenfunction is not solely concentrated on the Steklov patch. Instead
it has a long-range extension into the outer region as a result of the
presence of the other small Dirichlet patches. To determine
U_{0}
we
substitute
u\sim V_{01}=U_{0}\left(1-w_{1}({\bf{y}};-\sigma_{0})\right)
into
the normalization condition (
6.136
). Upon using
d{\bf s}=\cos(\xi_{1})d\xi_{1}d\xi_{2}={\varepsilon}^{2}\cos({\varepsilon}\xi_{1})dy_{1}dy_{2}\sim{\varepsilon}^{2}dy_{1}dy_{2}
, we calculate that

U_{0}\sim{\varepsilon}^{-1}\left[\int_{{\Gamma}_{1}}\left[1-w_{1}({\bf{y}};-\sigma_{0})\right]^{2}d{\bf{y}}\right]^{-1/2}\,.

(6.170)

Remark 1
.

Proposition
3
offers a straightforward numerical
procedure to construct three-term asymptotic expansions of the SDN
eigenpairs in the small patch limit when
U_{0}\neq 0
. Moreover, since
(
6.149
) has infinitely many solutions, one can
construct SDN eigenvalues on any desired, but large enough,
interval
(\sigma_{\rm min},\sigma_{\rm max})
. To characterize the
associated eigenfunctions on the Steklov patch
\Gamma_{1}
, we
use
V_{01}=U_{0}\left[1-w_{1}(y;-\sigma_{0})\right]
to leading
order, together with the divergence theorem applied to the problem
(
3.17
) for
w_{1}
, to obtain for
U_{0}\neq 0
that

C_{1}(-\sigma_{0})=-\frac{\sigma_{0}}{2\pi}\int_{\Gamma_{1}}\left(1-w_{1}\right)\,dy_{1}dy_{2}=-\frac{\sigma_{0}}{2\pi U_{0}}\int_{\Gamma_{1}}V_{01}\,dy_{1}dy_{2}\,.

(6.171)

Since we must have
C_{1}(-\sigma_{0})\neq 0
from (
6.149
), we
conclude that to leading order all non-resonant SDN
eigenfunctions in Proposition
3
are such that

\int_{\Gamma_{1}}V_{01}\,dy_{1}dy_{2}\neq 0
. For a circular patch

\Gamma_{1}
, this implies that these SDN eigenfunctions must be axially
symmetric on the patch.

Remark 2
.

If we remove our requirement that
U_{0}\neq 0
, so that the bulk
solution is now asymptotically small, we can construct an SDN
eigenpair where the Dirichlet patches now have only a very weak
influence on the SDN eigenvalue. In this situation, the SDN
eigenfunction is concentrated on the Steklov patch, and to
leading-order is unaffected by the presence of the Dirichlet patches,
as if they were absent. The asymptotic behavior for a single
circular Steklov patch was studied in
[
56
]
. For an
arbitrary patch, to construct such a solution, we let

\mu_{k1}^{N}>0
and
\Psi_{k1}^{N}
for
k\geq 1
be the eigenpairs
of the local Steklov problem (
D.323
) near

\Gamma_{1}
that satisfies, up to a normalization condition, the local
boundary value problem

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\bf{y}}}\Psi_{k1}^{N}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf{y}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{3}\,,

(6.172a)

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{3}}\Psi_{k1}^{N}+\mu_{k1}^{N}\Psi_{k1}^{N}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\in{\Gamma}_{1}\,,

(6.172b)

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{3}}\Psi_{k1}^{N}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\notin{\Gamma}_{1}\,,

(6.172c)

\displaystyle\Psi_{k1}^{N}({\bf{y}})

\displaystyle\sim{\mathcal{O}}(|{\bf{y}}|^{-2})\,,\quad\textrm{as}\quad|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty\,,

(6.172d)

where there is no monopole behavior in the far-field
(
6.172d
). Then, a leading-order SDN eigenpair for
(
2.13
) is obtained by taking
\sigma_{0}=\mu_{k1}^{N}
for some
index
k\geq 1
, and by choosing
V_{01}=\Psi_{k1}^{N}
as the
leading-order inner solution near the Steklov patch
\Gamma_{1}
, and

V_{0i}=0
as the leading-order inner solution near the Dirichlet
patches
\Gamma_{i}
for
i=2,\ldots,N
. Owing to the fast-decay
(
6.172d
), the outer (bulk) solution away from the patches
is
{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon}^{2})
smaller than that of the SDN eigenfunction
evaluated on the Steklov patch. For this leading-order construction,
we find by applying the divergence theorem to (
6.172
)
that
\int_{\Gamma_{1}}V_{01}\,dy_{1}dy_{2}=0
. As a result, when

\Gamma_{1}
is a circular patch, all of the non-axially symmetric
eigenfunctions on the patch will satisfy this condition. It is an
open problem to asymptotically construct a higher-order approximation
for these SDN eigenpairs.

6.2
Example of Identical Circular Dirichlet Patches

Our main result can be simplified considerably for the special case of

N-1
identical locally circular Dirichlet patches
{\partial\Omega}_{i}^{\varepsilon}
, each
of radius
{\varepsilon}a
(i.e.,
a_{i}=a
for
i=2,\ldots,N
). For this
situation, we have
C_{i}(\infty)=2a/\pi
for
i=2,\ldots,N
, so that
from (
6.149
)
\sigma_{0}
is a root of the nonlinear
algebraic equation

C_{1}(-\sigma_{0})=-\frac{2a(N-1)}{\pi}\,.

(6.173)

In addition, we reduce the expression (
6.158
) for

\sigma_{1}
to

\sigma_{1}=\frac{2a^{2}}{\pi^{2}C_{1}^{\prime}(-\sigma_{0})}N(N-1)\,.

(6.174)

Moreover, we can use (
6.173
) together with
(
3.40a
) for
E_{i}(\infty)
, so that to determine

\sigma_{2}
in (
6.168
) we need only set

{\bf{C}}=\frac{2a}{\pi}\left(1-N,1,\ldots,1\right)^{T}\,;\quad E_{i}=-\frac{2a^{2}}{\pi^{2}}\left(\log{a}+\log{4}-\frac{3}{2}\right)\,,\quad i=2,\ldots,N\,.

(6.175)

In this way, the numerical evaluation of the three-term expansion for

\sigma
in (
6.169a
) only involves first solving the
root-finding problem (
6.173
) for
\sigma_{0}
and then
calculating
E_{1}(-\sigma_{0})
and
C_{1}^{\prime}(-\sigma_{0})

numerically. To calculate
E_{1}(-\sigma_{0})
for a circular Steklov
patch, we use the decomposition (
E.353
) of Appendix

E
together with the numerical results shown in
Fig.
12
.

To qualitatively illustrate our theory, we consider a Steklov patch of
arbitrary shape and examine the limit
a\to 0
with a fixed
N
. In
this limit, the right-hand side of (
6.173
) vanishes, and

\sigma_{0}
is determined by solving
C_{1}(-\sigma_{0})=0
. These
solutions correspond to the eigenvalues
\mu_{k1}^{N}
of another
local
exterior Steklov problem defined by (
D.323
) of
Appendix
D.4
near the Steklov patch
{\Gamma}_{1}
, with
a Neumann-like boundary condition (
D.323d
) at infinity (see

[
56
]
for details; in particular, Table 1 from

[
56
]
reports
\mu_{ki}^{N}
for a circular Steklov patch).
Indeed, as the Dirichlet patches vanish, one recovers the conventional
Steklov problem with a single Steklov patch (see Remark

2
.)
In particular, the principal eigenvalue
\sigma^{(0)}
of the SDN
problem should approach
0
for a Steklov patch of arbitrary
shape. Substituting the asymptotic relation (
3.25
) into
the left-hand side of (
6.173
), we obtain to
leading-order in
a
and
{\varepsilon}
that

\sigma^{(0)}\approx\sigma^{(0)}_{0}\approx\frac{4a(N-1)}{|{\Gamma}_{1}|}\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad a\to 0\,.

(6.176)

6.3
Numerical Comparison

A numerical solution of the SDN spectral problem was obtained via a
finite-element method (FEM) described in

[
24
,
57
]
. For an accurate computation, one
needs to ensure that a tetrahedral mesh of the domain is sufficiently
refined near small patches, which requires a numerical diagonalization
of very large matrices. To avoid these technical issues, we restrict
our analysis to two circular patches (
N=2
), located at the north
and south poles. The axial symmetry of this setting reduces the
original three-dimensional setting to a planar one. Since our
analysis leading to Proposition
3
does not access the
asymptotic behavior of non-axially-symmetric eigenfunctions (see
Remarks
1
and
2
), we restrict the
numerical comparison exclusively to the eigenvalues that correspond to
axially symmetric eigenfunctions.

Table
1
illustrates the accuracy of the
three-term asymptotic formula (
6.169a
) by comparing its
predictions (last column) to FEM solutions with different maximal
meshsizes
h_{\rm max}
, with smaller meshsizes yielding more accurate
solutions. Even though the obtained numerical values of

\sigma^{(k)}
did not fully converge to the true eigenvalues, further
refinement of the mesh yielded matrices that are too large to be
treated on our laptop. Nevertheless, the numerical values reported in
the 6th column are very close to the predictions from our three-term
asymptotic formula given in the last column. This example serves as a
numerical validation of the asymptotic formula (
6.169a
).
Further analysis of the SDN problem and its applications will be
reported elsewhere.

h_{\rm max}

asympt.

\sigma^{(0)}

0.5902

0.5719

0.5636

0.5620

0.5561

\sigma^{(1)}

4.617

\sigma^{(2)}

8.887

\sigma^{(0)}
