Table 1
:

The first three SDN eigenvalues (that correspond to axially
symmetric eigenfunctions) for the unit sphere with two circular
patches of radius
{\varepsilon}
(with
a_{1}=a_{2}=1
), located at the north
and south poles. Columns 3-6 present the numerical results by FEM
with different maximal meshsizes
h_{\rm max}
. The last column
indicates the three-term asymptotic relation (
6.169a
),
which is seen to compare very favorably with the numerical result on
the most refined mesh.

6.4
Near-Resonant Case

Next, we inspect whether there may exist a near-resonant SDN eigepair
for the spectral problem (
2.14
) for which the limiting value

\sigma_{0}=\lim\limits_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}\sigma({\varepsilon})
belongs to

{\mathcal{P}}_{1}
(see (
6.137
)). More specifically, let
us assume that there exists an index
k^{\prime}\geq 0
such that

\sigma_{0}=\mu_{k^{\prime}1}
for some
simple
eigenvalue

\mu_{k^{\prime}1}
of the local Steklov eigenvalue problem
(
D.294
) of Appendix
D
for which

d_{k^{\prime}1}\neq 0
(see also Remark
3
below).
We normalize the corresponding local Steklov eigenfunction

\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}1}
to be the unique solution of

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\bf{y}}}\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}1}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf{y}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{3}\,,

(6.177a)

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{3}}\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}1}+\sigma_{0}\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}1}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\in{\Gamma}_{1}\,,

(6.177b)

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{3}}\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}1}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\notin{\Gamma}_{1}\,,

(6.177c)

\displaystyle\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}1}({\bf{y}})

\displaystyle\sim\frac{1}{|{\bf{y}}|}+{\mathcal{O}}\left(|{\bf{y}}|^{-2}\right)\quad\textrm{as}\quad|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty\,,

(6.177d)

where
\sigma_{0}=\mu_{k^{\prime}1}
and

{\Gamma}_{1}\asymp{\varepsilon}^{-1}\partial\Omega_{1}^{{\varepsilon}}
. Here the tilde symbol
highlights that this normalization is different from the conventional
one used in Appendix
D
.

With
\sigma_{0}=\mu_{k^{\prime}1}
we again expand the outer solution,
the eigenvalue
\sigma({\varepsilon})
, and the inner solutions as in
(
6.140
), (
6.142
) and (
6.143
),
respectively. However, in place of (
6.146
) and
(
6.147
), the leading-order inner solutions near the Steklov
patch and the Dirichlet patches are now

V_{01}=A_{1}\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}1}({\bf{y}})\,;\qquad V_{0i}=U_{0}\left(1-w_{i}({\bf{y}};\infty)\right)\,,\quad i=2,\ldots,N\,,

(6.178)

where
A_{1}
is a constant to be determined. Since
V_{01}\to 0
as

|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty
, we can only match the far-field behaviors of the
inner solutions to a leading-order constant outer solution
U_{0}
when

U_{0}=0
. As a result, since
U_{0}=0
, we must have
V_{0i}=0
for

i=2,\ldots,N
. In this way, in place of (
6.148
) the outer
correction
U_{1}
must now satisfy

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\bf{x}}}U_{1}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\Omega\,;\qquad\partial_{n}U_{1}=0\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\partial\Omega\backslash\{{{\bf{x}}_{1}\}}\,,

(6.179a)

\displaystyle U_{1}

\displaystyle\sim\frac{A_{1}}{|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{1}|}\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad{\bf{x}}\to{\bf{x}}_{1}\in\partial\Omega\,.

(6.179b)

The solvability condition for (
6.179
) yields that

A_{1}=0
. As a result,
V_{01}=0
, and we must have

U_{1}=\overline{U}_{1}
in
\Omega
, where
\overline{U}_{1}
is an unknown
constant.

Proceeding to higher order it is readily established that

\overline{U}_{1}=0
. We conclude that one cannot construct a

nontrivial
solution to (
2.13
) with limiting behavior

\sigma_{0}=\mu_{k^{\prime}1}
. As a consequence, there is no
near-resonant eigenpair of the SDN problem (
2.14
) with a
single Steklov patch.

Remark 3
.

From the beginning of §
6.4
, we restricted our
analysis to the near-resonant cases, for which
\sigma_{0}=\mu_{k^{\prime}1}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{1}
such that
\mu_{k^{\prime}1}
is simple.
As a consequence, our statement that there is no near-resonant
eigenpair of the SDN problem (
2.14
) is established only under
the assumption that all eigenvalues in the resonant set
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}
are simple. For a circular patch numerical evidence suggests
that this assumption does hold, but its rigorous validation presents
an interesting open problem. For an arbitrary patch, however,
the assumption on the simplicity of eigenvalues from
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}

may not hold. However, we expect that this assumption can be
relaxed, i.e., one can use any nontrivial element of the eigenspace
associated to
\mu_{k^{\prime}1}
in the analysis above. A proof of this
statement is beyond the scope of this paper.

7
The Steklov-Neumann (SN) Eigenvalue Problem

Finally, we address the SN eigenvalue problem (
2.14
) in the unit
sphere
\Omega
, with
N\geq 1
Steklov patches
{\partial\Omega}_{i}^{{\varepsilon}}
.
We impose the normalization condition

\int_{\partial\Omega_{a}}u^{2}\,d{\bf s}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{\partial\Omega_{i}^{{\varepsilon}}}u^{2}d{\bf s}=1\,.

(7.180)

This spectral problem has a discrete spectrum, with a countable set of
nonnegative eigenvalues
\{\sigma^{(m)}({\varepsilon})\}
that are enumerated
by an integer index
m=0,1,2,\ldots
that accumulates to infinity

[
84
]
. Since the principal eigenvalue,
which corresponds to a constant eigenfunction, is

\sigma^{(0)}({\varepsilon})=0
, independently of
{\varepsilon}
, we exclude it from
our analysis below. Owing to the orthogonality of the Steklov
eigenfunctions, or more simply by applying the divergence theorem to
(
2.14
), we must have for any other Steklov eigenvalue

\sigma^{(m)}({\varepsilon})>0
with
m=1,2,\ldots
that the
corresponding eigenfunction
u^{(m)}
satisfies

\int_{\partial\Omega_{a}}u^{(m)}\,d{\bf s}=\frac{{\varepsilon}}{\sigma^{(m)}({\varepsilon})}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{\partial\Omega_{i}^{{\varepsilon}}}\partial_{n}u^{(m)}\,d{\bf s}=0\,.

(7.181)

For convenience, in our analysis below we omit the superscript

(m)
to highlight that our asymptotic analysis is not specific to
a particular value of
m
.

In analogy with the SDN problem studied in §
6
, we
need to consider both non-resonant and near-resonant cases. While the
near-resonant case was not possible for the SDN problem with a single
Steklov patch, it will present one of the challenging features of the
SN analysis here.

We introduce the resonant set

{\mathcal{P}}\equiv\bigcup_{i=1}^{N}{\mathcal{P}}_{i}\,,\quad\mbox{where}\quad{\mathcal{P}}_{i}\equiv\bigcup_{k=0}^{\infty}\left\{\mu_{ki}~|~d_{ki}\neq 0\right\}\,,

(7.182)

where
\mu_{ki}
with
k=0,1,\ldots
are the eigenvalues of the
local
Steklov problem (
D.294
) near the
i
-th Steklov
patch
{\Gamma}_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,N
, with nontrivial spectral weights

d_{ki}\neq 0
(see of Appendix
D
for details). We
distinguish two situations according to the limiting value

\sigma_{0}=\lim\limits_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}\sigma({\varepsilon})
of a SN eigenvalue

\sigma=\sigma({\varepsilon})
:

(I) If
\sigma_{0}\notin{\mathcal{P}}
, the eigenpair
\{\sigma,u\}

is called non-resonant. In this case, both
C_{i}(-\sigma_{0})
and

w_{i}({\bf{y}};-\sigma_{0})
are well-defined for each
i=1,\ldots,N
. The
analysis for this non-resonant case, which is similar to that done in
§
6.1
for the SDN problem, will be performed in
§
7.1
.

(II) If
\sigma_{0}\in{\mathcal{P}}
, the eigenpair
\{\sigma,u\}
is
called near-resonant. In this case, some
w_{i}({\bf{y}};-\sigma_{0})
may be
undefined and thus must be replaced by suitable eigenfunctions of the
local Steklov problem (
D.294
), associated to
\sigma_{0}
.
Even though the asymptotic analysis of the near-resonant case is
feasible to undertake in more generality, we will restrict our
attention below to one relevant setting. More specifically, we assume
that
N\geq 2
and that there are exactly
M

identical
patches,
with
2\leq M\leq N
, which we relabel by

\partial\Omega_{1}^{{\varepsilon}}=\ldots=\partial\Omega_{M}^{{\varepsilon}}\equiv\partial\Omega_{c}^{{\varepsilon}}
. For these identical patches, with a common
patch shape
{\Gamma}_{c}\asymp{\varepsilon}^{-1}\partial\Omega_{c}^{{\varepsilon}}
, there is
a common spectrum
\mu_{kc}
with
k\geq 0
of the local Steklov
problem (
D.294
) of Appendix
D
. In §
7.3
we will show that the global SN problem
(
2.14
) admits
M-1

near-resonant
eigenvalues (counting
multiplicity) such that
\sigma_{0}\in{\mathcal{P}}
. The
corresponding eigenfunctions will be shown to concentrate on
the
M
near-resonant patches. We will also derive a three-term
asymptotic behavior for these global SN eigenvalues.

Remark 4
.

We remark that for this setting, it is essential that
M>1
. In fact,
if the near-resonant condition occurs on only one patch, i.e. if

\sigma_{0}=\mu_{ki}
for some simple eigenvalue
\mu_{ki}
of
(
D.294
) of a unique patch
i\in\{{1,\ldots,N\}}

with
d_{ki}\neq 0
, it is readily shown, as similar to that done in
§
6.4
, that the SN problem (
2.14
) only admits
the trivial solution (see also Remark
3
). Hence, for

M=1
, there is no SN eigenpair for (
2.14
) with such limiting
asymptotics
\sigma_{0}
. We emphasize that when there are two or more
identical patches the analysis for the non-resonant case will only
capture a subset of the SN eigenvalues for the global problem
(
2.14
). The remaining global SN eigenvalues will be in
near-resonance with eigenvalues of the local Steklov problem
(
D.294
) on the identical patches. The corresponding
eigenfunctions will concentrate on these identical patches. We remark
that more intricate near-resonant cases are possible for specific
non-generic situations such as when there are two patch indices
i_{1}

and
i_{2}
and two eigenvalue indices
k_{1}
and
k_{2}
for which

\mu_{k_{1}i_{1}}=\mu_{k_{2}i_{2}}
. Although the construction of a SN
eigenpair for which
\sigma_{0}=\mu_{k_{1}i_{1}}=\mu_{k_{2}i_{2}}
can be
done in a similar way as for the identical patch case undertaken in §
7.3
, we will not consider this special case.

7.1
Non-Resonant Case

Since the analysis for the SN problem (
2.14
) in the
non-resonant case is very similar to that done in §
6.1
for the SDN problem (
2.13
), we will
only briefly outline the analysis to determine

\sigma({\varepsilon})
. As for the SDN problem, we seek
to construct eigenpairs of (
2.14
) for which the leading-order
outer solution
U_{0}
is non-vanishing (i.e.
U_{0}\neq 0
).

In the outer region, we expand
u
as in (
6.140
) to obtain
(
6.141
) at each order of the expansion. In addition, each
nontrivial Steklov eigenvalue is expanded as in
(
6.142
). In the inner region near each Steklov patch, we
expand the inner solution in terms of geodesic coordinates as in
(
6.143
), to derive that
V_{ji}
for
j=0,1,2
, and for each

i=1,\ldots,N
, satisfies

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\bf{y}}}V_{ji}

\displaystyle=\delta_{j2}\left(2y_{3}V_{0i,y_{3}y_{3}}+2V_{0i,y_{3}}\right)\,,\quad{\bf{y}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{3}\,,

(7.183a)

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{3}}V_{ji}+\sigma_{0}V_{ji}

\displaystyle=-(1-\delta_{j0})\sigma_{j}V_{0i}\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\in{\Gamma}_{i}\,,

(7.183b)

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{3}}V_{ji}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\notin{\Gamma}_{i}\,.

(7.183c)

For each Steklov patch, we set
w_{i}=w_{i}({\bf{y}};-\sigma)
as the
solution of (
3.17
), where the dependence of
w_{i}
,
C_{i}
and

{\bf p}_{i}
on
\sigma
is obtained by setting
\kappa_{i}=-\sigma
in
(
3.17
). As similar to the analysis of the SDN problem in
§
6
, we define

w_{ci}=w_{ci}({\bf{y}};-\sigma)\equiv\partial_{\sigma}w_{i}({\bf{y}};-\sigma),

(7.184)

which satisfies, for each
i=1,\ldots,N
, the following inner problem:

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\bf{y}}}w_{ci}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf{y}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{3}\,,

(7.185a)

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{3}}w_{ci}+\sigma w_{ci}

\displaystyle=1-w_{i}\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\in{\Gamma}_{i}\,,

(7.185b)

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{3}}w_{ci}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\notin{\Gamma}_{i}\,,

(7.185c)

\displaystyle w_{ci}

\displaystyle\sim-\frac{C_{i}^{\prime}(-\sigma)}{|{\bf{y}}|}-{\frac{{\bf p}_{i}^{\prime}(-\sigma){\bf\cdot}{\bf{y}}}{|{\bf{y}}|^{3}}}+\cdots\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty\,.

(7.185d)

Since
\sigma_{0}\notin{\mathcal{P}}
, it follows that
C_{i}(-\sigma_{0})

and
w_{i}({\bf{y}};-\sigma_{0})
are well-defined. Then, in terms of the
constant leading-order outer solution
U_{0}
, which will be found below
by the normalization condition (
7.180
), the leading order
inner solution for each
i=1,\ldots,N
, as obtained by setting
j=0

in (
7.183
), is

V_{0i}=U_{0}\left(1-w_{i}({\bf{y}};-\sigma_{0})\right)\,.

(7.186)

Upon matching the far-field of
V_{0i}
to the outer solution, we find
that
U_{1}
satisfies

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\bf{x}}}U_{1}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\Omega\,;\quad\partial_{n}U_{1}=0\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\partial\Omega\backslash\{{{\bf{x}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{x}}_{N}\}}\,,

(7.187a)

\displaystyle U_{1}

\displaystyle\sim-\frac{C_{i}\left(-\sigma_{0}\right)U_{0}}{|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i}|}\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad{\bf{x}}\to{\bf{x}}_{i}\in\partial\Omega\,,\quad i=1,\ldots,N\,.

(7.187b)

The solvability condition for (
7.187
) is that

U_{0}\sum_{i=1}^{N}C_{i}(-\sigma_{0})=0\,.

(7.188)

For
U_{0}\neq 0
, we conclude that the leading-order Steklov
eigenvalue
\sigma_{0}
is a root of the following nonlinear algebraic
equation:

{\mathcal{N}}(\sigma_{0})=0\,,\quad\mbox{where}\quad{\mathcal{N}}(\sigma_{0})\equiv\sum_{i=1}^{N}C_{i}\left(-\sigma_{0}\right)\,.

(7.189)

The spectral expansion (
3.19
) ensures that

{\mathcal{N}}(\sigma_{0})
increases monotonically between its
consecutive poles so that (
7.189
) has infinitely many
solutions that we denote as
\sigma_{0}^{(k)}
. These solutions lie
between consecutive poles but finding their explicit locations is in
general more difficult than for the SDN problem with a single Steklov
patch. As earlier, we omit the superscript
(k)
for brevity.

With
\sigma_{0}
determined in this way, the solution to
(
7.187
) for
U_{1}
is written in terms of the
surface Neumann Green’s function
G_{s}
and an unknown constant

\overline{U}_{1}
as

U_{1}({\bf{x}})=\overline{U}_{1}-2\pi U_{0}\sum_{j=1}^{N}C_{j}(-\sigma_{0})G_{s}({\bf{x}};{\bf{x}}_{j})\,.

(7.190)

To proceed to higher order, we expand
U_{1}
as
{\bf{x}}\to{\bf{x}}_{i}
to derive
(
6.151
), where we label
C_{i}=C_{i}(-\sigma_{0})
for

i=1,\ldots,N
. Upon matching to the inner solution we conclude that

V_{1i}\sim{U_{0}C_{i}/2}
as
|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty
for
i=1,\ldots,N
. Upon
solving the problem (
7.183
) for
V_{1i}
with this limiting
behavior, we obtain that

V_{1i}=-U_{0}\sigma_{1}w_{ci}({\bf{y}};-\sigma_{0})+\frac{U_{0}C_{i}(-\sigma_{0})}{2}\left(1-w_{i}({\bf{y}};-\sigma_{0})\right)\,,\quad i=1,\ldots,N\,.

(7.191)

The far-field behavior for
V_{1i}
as
|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty
is

V_{1i}\sim U_{0}\sigma_{1}\frac{C_{i}^{\prime}(-\sigma_{0})}{|{\bf{y}}|}+\frac{U_{0}C_{i}(-\sigma_{0})}{2}\left(1-\frac{C_{i}(-\sigma_{0})}{|{\bf{y}}|}\right)+\cdots\,,

(7.192)

where the neglected higher-order far-field terms are dipole contributions.

The monopole terms in (
7.192
) provide the singularity
behavior for the outer correction
U_{2}
in (
6.140
). In this
way, we find that
U_{2}
satisfies (
6.156a
) subject to

U_{2}\sim-\frac{U_{0}}{2}\frac{\left[C_{i}(-\sigma_{0})\right]^{2}}{|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i}|}+U_{0}\sigma_{1}\frac{C_{i}^{\prime}(-\sigma_{0})}{|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i}|}\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad{\bf{x}}\to{\bf{x}}_{i}\in\partial\Omega\,,\quad i=1,\ldots,N\,.

(7.193)

The solvability condition for this problem for
U_{2}
is that

-\frac{U_{0}}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left[C_{i}(-\sigma_{0})\right]^{2}+U_{0}\sigma_{1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}C_{i}^{\prime}(-\sigma_{0})=0\,.

(7.194)

Under the condition that
U_{0}\neq 0
, (
7.194
) determines

\sigma_{1}
as

\sigma_{1}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left[C_{i}(-\sigma_{0})\right]^{2}}{\sum_{i=1}^{N}C_{i}^{\prime}(-\sigma_{0})}\,,

(7.195)

where
\sigma_{0}
is a root of (
7.189
). From
(
3.21
), it follows that
C_{i}
increases monotonically
between its poles so that
C_{i}^{\prime}(-\sigma_{0})\neq 0
. As a result
the denominator in (
6.157
) never vanishes, and
\sigma_{1}

is well-defined and strictly positive. With
\sigma_{1}

determined in this way, the solution to (
6.156a
) with
(
7.193
) is given in terms of an unknown constant

\overline{U}_{2}
by

U_{2}({\bf{x}})=\overline{U}_{2}-2\pi U_{0}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\left(\frac{\left[C_{j}(-\sigma_{0})\right]^{2}}{2}-\sigma_{1}C_{j}^{\prime}(-\sigma_{0})\right)G_{s}({\bf{x}};{\bf{x}}_{j})\,.

(7.196)

Finally, we determine
\sigma_{2}
. We readily obtain (
6.160
)
for the far-field behavior for the inner correction
V_{2i}
, which
satisfies (
7.183
) with
k=2
. In analogy with (
6.163
),
we determine
V_{2i}
for
i=1,\ldots,N
as

V_{2i}=U_{0}\Phi_{2i}+\left(U_{0}\beta_{i}+\overline{U}_{1}\right)\left(1-w_{i}({\bf{y}};-\sigma_{0})\right)-U_{0}\sigma_{2}w_{ci}({\bf{y}};-\sigma_{0})\,,

(7.197)

where
\Phi_{2i}
satisfies (
4.67
) in which we set

\kappa_{i}=-\sigma_{0}
and
C_{i}=C_{i}(-\sigma_{0})
. As a result, the
refined far-field behavior of
V_{2i}
for each
i=1,\ldots,N
is

\begin{split}V_{2i}&\sim U_{0}\beta_{i}+\overline{U}_{1}+\frac{U_{0}C_{i}(-\sigma_{0})}{2}\left(\log(y_{3}+|{\bf{y}}|)-\frac{y_{3}(y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2})}{|{\bf{y}}|^{3}}\right)\\
&\qquad+\left[E_{i}(-\sigma_{0})-\left(\beta_{i}+\frac{\overline{U}_{1}}{U_{0}}\right)C_{i}(-\sigma_{0})+\sigma_{2}C_{i}^{\prime}(-\sigma_{0})\right]\frac{U_{0}}{|{\bf{y}}|}\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty\,.\end{split}

(7.198)

Here
E_{i}(-\sigma_{0})
is obtained by setting
\kappa_{i}=-\sigma_{0}

in (
3.36
).

As similar to the analysis of the SDN problem in §
6.1
,
the monopole terms in the far-field behavior (
7.198
),
together with dipole term in the far-field of
V_{0i}
, provide the
singularity behavior for the outer correction
U_{3}
. In this way, we get
that
U_{3}
satisfies

\begin{split}\Delta_{{\bf{x}}}U_{3}&=0\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\Omega\,;\quad\partial_{n}U_{3}=0\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\partial\Omega\backslash\{{{\bf{x}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{x}}_{N}\}}\,,\\
U_{3}&\sim\left[E_{i}(-\sigma_{0})-\left(\beta_{i}+\frac{\overline{U}_{1}}{U_{0}}\right)C_{i}(-\sigma_{0})+\sigma_{2}C_{i}^{\prime}(-\sigma_{0})\right]\frac{U_{0}}{|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i}|}\\
&\qquad-U_{0}\frac{{\bf p}_{i}(-\sigma_{0}){\bf\cdot}{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}^{T}({\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i})}{|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i}|^{3}}\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad{\bf{x}}\to{\bf{x}}_{i}\in\partial\Omega\,,\quad i=1,\ldots,N\,,\end{split}

(7.199)

where the orthogonal matrix
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}
is defined in
(
A.255
) in terms of the basis vectors of the geodesic
coordinate system. The solvability condition for (
7.199
) is
that

U_{0}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}E_{i}(-\sigma_{0})-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\beta_{i}C_{i}(-\sigma_{0})+\sigma_{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}C_{i}^{\prime}(-\sigma_{0})\right)-\overline{U}_{1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}C_{i}(-\sigma_{0})=0\,,

(7.200)

which determines
\sigma_{2}
. Since
\sigma_{0}
satisfies

\sum_{i=1}^{N}C_{i}(-\sigma_{0})=0
when
U_{0}\neq 0
, as seen from
(
7.189
) and (
7.188
), we observe that
\sigma_{2}
is
independent of the unknown normalization constant
\overline{U}_{1}
. As
a result, upon recalling that

\beta_{i}=-2\pi\left({\mathcal{G}}_{s}{\bf{C}}\right)_{i}
from
(
6.152
), we conclude from (
7.200
) that, when

U_{0}\neq 0
,
\sigma_{2}
is given by

\sigma_{2}=-\frac{1}{\sum_{i=1}^{N}C_{i}^{\prime}(-\sigma_{0})}\left(2\pi{\bf{C}}^{T}{\mathcal{G}}_{s}{\bf{C}}+\sum_{i=1}^{N}E_{i}(-\sigma_{0})\right)\,,

(7.201)

where
{\mathcal{G}}_{s}
is the Green’s matrix and

{\bf{C}}=\left(C_{1}(-\sigma_{0}),\ldots,C_{N}(-\sigma_{0})\right)^{T}
.
We summarize our result as follows:

Proposition 4
.

For
{\varepsilon}\to 0
, consider the eigenvalues
\sigma=\sigma({\varepsilon})
of
the Steklov-Neumann problem (
2.14
), for which

\sigma({\varepsilon})\to\sigma_{0}\notin{\mathcal{P}}
, where the resonant set

{\mathcal{P}}
is defined by (
7.182
). These Steklov
eigenvalues and the associated eigenfunctions, restricted to

{\Gamma}_{i}
, have the three-term asymptotics

\displaystyle\sigma

\displaystyle=\sigma_{0}+{\varepsilon}\log\left(\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\right)\sigma_{1}+{\varepsilon}\sigma_{2}+{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon}^{2}\log{\varepsilon})\,,

(7.202a)

\displaystyle u|_{{\Gamma}_{i}}

\displaystyle=V_{0i}+{\varepsilon}\log\left(\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\right)V_{1i}+{\varepsilon}V_{2i}+{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon}^{2}\log{\varepsilon})\,,\quad i=1,\ldots,N\,,

(7.202b)

where
\sigma_{0}
,
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
are
respectively determined by (
7.189
), (
7.195
) and
(
7.201
). Moreover,
V_{0i}
,
V_{1i}
and
V_{2i}
for
each
i=1,\ldots,N
are respectively given by (
7.186
),
(
7.191
), and (
7.197
). Here
u|_{{\Gamma}_{i}}
is given
up to a normalization constants
U_{0}\neq 0
and
\overline{U}_{1}
for
the eigenfunction, whereas its spatial behavior is determined by the
functions
w_{i}({\bf{y}};-\sigma_{0})
and
w_{ci}({\bf{y}};-\sigma_{0})
, which admit
the spectral expansions (
D.302
) and
(
D.304
), respectively. For a circular patch, these
expansions can be readily calculated numerically, as shown in Appendix

D
.

By using the normalization condition (
7.180
), together with
the leading-order inner solutions (
7.186
), we can determine

U_{0}
as

U_{0}\sim{\varepsilon}^{-1}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{{\Gamma}_{i}}\left[1-w_{i}({\bf{y}};-\sigma_{0})\right]^{2}d{\bf{y}}\right]^{-1/2}\,.

(7.203)

Since
U_{0}\neq 0
, the class of SN eigenpairs given in Proposition

4
results from a global interaction of the Steklov
patches through the outer (bulk) solution.

Remark 5
.

In analogy with the SDN eigenvalue problem analysis (see Remarks

1
and
2
), the analysis leading to
Proposition
4
does not give access to all SN
eigenpairs. In particular, as for the SDN problem there are eigenpairs
for which the leading-order bulk solution
U_{0}
vanishes. For
circular Steklov patches, this situation will always occur for
eigenfunctions that are not axially symmetric on the patches. To
leading order, these SN eigenvalues have limiting behavior

\sigma_{0}=\mu_{ki}^{N}
, for some index
k\geq 1
and patch index

i\in\{{1,\ldots,N\}}
, where
\mu_{ki}^{N}>0
is an
eigenvalue of the local Steklov problem (
6.172
)
in which
\Gamma_{1}
is replaced by
\Gamma_{i}
. The corresponding
eigenfunction concentrates on the
i
-th patch and is only weakly
influenced by the other patches. In addition, other SN eigenvalues
corresponding to the near-resonant case will be recovered in §
7.3
.

7.2
Numerical Comparison

In §
4.2
, §
5.2
and §
6.3
, we used a finite-element method for validating the
asymptotic formulas. However, obtaining accurate numerical results by
this method for small patches requires using very fine meshes, which
typically results in prohibitively long computations. To achieve a
more accurate computation of the SN eigenvalues for a single patch or
for two antipodal patches on a sphere, in Appendix
F
we
outline an alternative method, based on
[
48
]
, which
exploits properties of axially symmetric harmonic functions. Using
this more refined numerical approach we now give two examples to
illustrate our main result (
7.202a
).

Example I:
For a single circular patch
{\partial\Omega}_{1}^{\varepsilon}
of radius

{\varepsilon}
(i.e.,
N=1
and
a_{1}=1
), the condition
(
7.189
) for
\sigma_{0}
reads as
C_{1}(-\sigma_{0})=0
.
The spectral expansion (
3.19
) of
C_{1}(\kappa_{1})

implies that it has infinitely many nontrivial zeros, which we denote
as
-\mu_{k1}^{N}
with
k=1,2,\ldots
(see Fig.
2
). As
shown in Lemma
8
of Appendix
D.4
, these roots

\mu_{k1}^{N}
are in fact eigenvalues of a local Steklov eigenvalue
problem (
D.323
) defined near the patch
{\Gamma}_{1}
, for
which the corresponding eigenfunctions satisfy a far-field
Neumann-like condition (
D.323d
). These zeros
determine the leading-order behavior of the associated SN eigenvalues
for the global SN problem (
2.14
) as
\sigma_{0}^{(k)}=\mu_{k1}^{N}
. This leading-order behavior was studied in

[
56
]
, and the numerical values of
\mu_{k1}^{N}
were
reported in Table I of
[
56
]
. In turn, the asymptotic
formula (
7.202a
) gives the next-order corrections as

\sigma_{1}=0
from (
7.195
) and

\sigma_{2}=-{E_{1}(-\sigma_{0})/C_{1}^{\prime}(-\sigma_{0})}
from
(
7.201
). In this way, from (
7.202a
), the
first four SN eigenvalues (that correspond to axially symmetric
eigenfunctions) are predicted to have the two-term asymptotic behavior

\displaystyle\sigma_{\rm asy}^{(1)}

\displaystyle\sim 4.121-0.573\,{\varepsilon}+{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon}^{2}\log{\varepsilon})\,,\quad\sigma_{\rm asy}^{(2)}\sim 7.342-0.552\,{\varepsilon}+{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon}^{2}\log{\varepsilon})\,,

(7.204a)

\displaystyle\sigma_{\rm asy}^{(3)}

\displaystyle\sim 10.517-0.542\,{\varepsilon}+{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon}^{2}\log{\varepsilon})\,,\quad\sigma_{\rm asy}^{(4)}\sim 13.677-0.535\,{\varepsilon}+{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon}^{2}\log{\varepsilon})\,.

(7.204b)

In Fig.
7
we plot the difference between the
numerically computed values
\sigma^{(k)}_{\rm num}
of the first two
SN eigenvalues and their asymptotic approximations in
(
7.204a
), as a function of
{\varepsilon}^{2}
. The observed linear
dependence on
{\varepsilon}^{2}
indicates that: (i) the first two terms of
(
7.204a
) are correct, and (ii) the next-order term is

{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon}^{2})
as the coefficient of the error term

{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon}^{2}\log{\varepsilon})
seems to vanish.

Figure 7
:

Asymptotic behavior of the first two SN eigenvalues (that
correspond to axially-symmetric eigenfunctions) for a single circular
patch of radius
{\varepsilon}
on the unit sphere. Each curve shows the
difference between the numerical value
\sigma^{(k)}_{\rm num}
,
computed using the methodology in Appendix
F
, and its
asymptotic value
\sigma^{(k)}_{\rm asy}
given in
(
7.204a
). This difference is shown as a function of

{\varepsilon}^{2}
to highlight the order of the error estimate in
(
7.204a
).

Example II:
Next, we consider the special case of
N

circular Steklov patches of distinct radii
a_{i}>0
, for

i=1,\ldots,N
, so that
a_{i}\neq a_{j}
for
i\neq j
. Then, using the
scaling law (
3.20
), we obtain from (
7.189
)
that
\sigma_{0}^{(k)}
are the roots of
{\mathcal{N}}(\sigma_{0})=0
, where

{\mathcal{N}}(\sigma_{0})=\sum_{i=1}^{N}a_{i}{\mathcal{C}}(-\sigma_{0}a_{i})\,.

(7.205)

Here
{\mathcal{C}}(\mu)
is readily computed via the spectral
expansion (
3.19
) for any rescaled patch
{\Gamma}_{i}/a_{i}
.
Owing to the monotonicity of
{\mathcal{N}}(\sigma_{0})
between
consecutive poles, which readily follows from the monotonicity of

{\mathcal{C}}(\mu)
established in (
3.21
), we conclude that
between any two consecutive poles of
{\mathcal{N}}(\sigma_{0})
the
function
{\mathcal{N}}(\sigma_{0})=0
must have a unique root. For each
such root, the asymptotic result (
7.202a
) can then be
used to determine a three-term asymptotic expansion for this
particular SN eigenvalue.

To illustrate this result, we numerically compute the eigenvalues of
the SN problem (
2.14
) for two circular patches of radii

a_{1}{\varepsilon}
and
a_{2}{\varepsilon}
(with
a_{2}=1
), located at the north and
south poles of the unit sphere (note that the trivial principal
eigenvalue
\sigma^{(0)}=0
will be excluded from our discussion; we
also focus on the eigenvalues that correspond to axially symmetric
eigenfunctions). Figure
8(a)
shows an excellent
agreement between the asymptotic result in (
7.202a
) and
the numerically computed eigenvalues as the radius
a_{1}
of the
smaller patch is varied on
(0,1)
. We observe that as
a_{1}\to 0
, we recover the eigenvalues, written in the form
\mu_{k2}^{N}
,
for the SN problem with a single Steklov patch
{\Gamma}_{2}
.
The observed behavior of the eigenvalues allows us to push the
analogy to a single Steklov patch even further. When
{\varepsilon}
is small,
one might expect that the two well-separated patches do not almost
“feel” each other. This (over-)simplified picture suggests that, to
leading order, the spectrum of the SN problem with two patches
would be the union of the spectra of the two SN problems with a single
patch, either
{\Gamma}_{1}
, or
{\Gamma}_{2}
. In Fig.
8(b)
,
four thin horizontal lines present the asymptotic values
\sigma_{\rm asy}^{(k)}
from (
7.204
) of the first four eigenvalues
for a single Steklov patch
{\Gamma}_{2}
(as if
{\Gamma}_{1}
was absent).
In turn, the thick solid and dashed lines present the asymptotic values

\sigma_{\rm asy}^{(k)}/a_{1}
from (
7.204
) of the first
two eigenvalues for a single Steklov patch
{\Gamma}_{1}
(as if

{\Gamma}_{2}
was absent). For comparison, symbols show the numerically
computed eigenvalues of the SN problem with two patches; these symbols
are identical with those shown in Fig.
8(a)
but just
colored differently. One observes an excellent agreement between
symbols and curves that
partly
validates the intuitive idea of
the patches not feeling each other. However, there are points (shown
by triangles) that are not captured by either of the asymptotic
relations for single patches. To outline their dependence on
a_{1}
,
we added the dashed curve
0.95/a_{1}
, in which the prefactor
0.95

was obtained from fitting, i.e., it does not correspond to any
limiting eigenvalue
\mu_{ki}^{N}
. The presence of such points
highlights that the interaction between two patches is still relevant
but it mainly affects the smallest (nontrivial) eigenvalue.

(a)

Comparison with asymptotics (
7.202a
)

(b)

Comparison with asymptotics (
7.204
)

Figure 8
:

(a)
The first five SN eigenvalues
\sigma^{(k)}
(that
correspond to axially symmetric eigenfunctions) for two circular
patches
{\Gamma}_{i}
of radii
a_{1}
and
a_{2}=1
, with
0<a_{1}<1
, located
at the north and south poles of the unit sphere, with
{\varepsilon}=0.2
.
Symbols illustrate the numerical values computed by the method in
Appendix
F
with truncation order
n_{\rm max}=1000
, whereas
thick lines indicate the asymptotic formula (
7.202a
).

(b)
Symbols show the same eigenvalues computed numerically but
colored differently, along with the shown curves: the thin horizontal
lines present
\sigma_{\rm asy}^{(k)}/a_{2}
with
k=1,2,3,4
from
(
7.204
); thick solid and dashed curves present

\sigma_{\rm asy}^{(k)}/a_{1}
with
k=1,2
from
(
7.204
); thick dotted line presents
0.95/a_{1}
.

Let us now consider the limit
a_{1}\to 1
, which corresponds to the
setting of two identical patches. Figure
8(a)
shows
that the asymptotic theory of §
7.1
does not account for
the closely spaced SN eigenvalues that are computed numerically, and
instead accurately captures only one of these two eigenvalues.
To qualitatively explain this discrepancy for two identical patches,
we observe that the poles of
{\mathcal{N}}(\sigma_{0})
, as
characterized by the resonant set
{\mathcal{P}}
in (
7.182
)
with
N=2
, are no longer all distinct. In addition, the leading-order
SN eigenvalue for two identical patches with
a_{1}=a_{2}=1
reduces from
(
7.205
) to simply finding the roots of
{\mathcal{C}}(-\sigma_{0})=0
. As a result, our asymptotic theory when applied to
two identical patches would predict that, for each
k=1,2,\ldots
,
there is a
unique
leading-order approximation

\sigma_{0}^{(k)}=\mu_{k1}^{N}(=\mu_{k2}^{N})
for the SN eigenvalue,
satisfying
C_{1}(-\mu_{k1}^{N})=0
. We emphasize that these are simply
the leading-order SN eigenvalues for a single patch. In particular,
the asymptotic result (
7.202a
) of §
7.1

would erroneously predict that the two smallest SN eigenvalues have
the
same
two-term asymptotics
\sigma_{\rm asy}^{(k)}\sim\mu_{k1}^{N}+{\varepsilon}E_{1}(-\mu_{k1}^{N})/C_{1}^{\prime}(-\mu_{k1}^{N})
.
This is precisely the asymptotic result given in (
7.204a
)
for a single Steklov patch.
These results are shown by the limiting values of the green and black
dashed curves at the right endpoint in Fig.
8(a)
.
However, our asymptotic theory fails to account for the additional
nearby SN eigenvalue at the right end of the red curve in
Fig.
8(a)
, as well as a further closely spaced SN
eigenvalue near the right end of the black dashed curve. Moreover,
the asymptotic analysis of §
7.1
does not predict the
lowest SN eigenvalue at the right end of the lower blue curve in
Fig.
8(a)
. Indeed, the first line of Table

2
reports the first five nontrivial SN eigenvalues for two
identical patches with
{\varepsilon}=0.2
that were calculated by the accurate
numerical methodology outlined in Appendix
F
, and which
we consider as benchmarks. These results are the SN eigenvalues at
the right ends of the curves in Fig.
8(a)
. For
comparison, the second line of Table
2
yields the first two
SN eigenvalues for two identical patches as predicted by the
asymptotic theory of §
7.1
as obtained by setting

{\varepsilon}=0.2
in (
7.204a
). As a result, we conclude that
when applied to the case of identical patches, the asymptotic theory
of §
7.1
only accounts for a subset of the true SN
eigenvalues. We now remedy this deficiency by refining our asymptotic
theory to treat the setting of multiple identical patches.
