Table 2
:

The first five SN eigenvalues (that correspond to axially symmetric
eigenfunctions) for two identical circular patches of radius
{\varepsilon}=0.2
located on the north and south poles of the unit sphere. The
first line presents the numerical results obtained by the accurate
method presented in Appendix
F
; as all digits of these
values are exact, they are considered as benchmarks. The second line
gives our three-term expansion (
7.202a
) for the
non-resonant case (it is identical to the single-patch asymptotics
(
7.204
)). The third line gives the three-term
expansion (
7.242
), that will be derived in §
7.3
for the near-resonant case (see below).

7.3
Near-Resonant Case

We now give a specific nontrivial illustration of the near-resonant
case that will always occur when there are
M
identical patches, with

2\leq M\leq N
. With a suitable relabeling of the patch indices, we
label the common patch shape as
\partial\Omega_{c}^{{\varepsilon}}=\partial\Omega_{i}^{{\varepsilon}}
(with
a_{i}=a_{c}
) for
i=1,\ldots,M
. On
these identical patches, there is a common spectrum, labeled by

\mu_{kc}
for
k\geq 0
, for the local Steklov problem
(
D.294
) of Appendix
D
.

We assume that

\sigma_{0}=\lim\limits_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}\sigma({\varepsilon})=\mu_{k^{\prime}c}
for
some simple eigenvalue
\mu_{k^{\prime}c}
of (
D.294
) for
which
d_{k^{\prime}c}\neq 0
. The corresponding local Steklov
eigenfunction from (
D.294
), labeled by

\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}c}
, is taken to be the unique solution to

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\bf{y}}}\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}c}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf{y}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{3}\,,

(7.206a)

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{3}}\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}c}+\sigma_{0}\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}c}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\in{\Gamma}_{c}\,,

(7.206b)

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{3}}\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}c}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\notin{\Gamma}_{c}\,,

(7.206c)

\displaystyle\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}c}({\bf{y}})

\displaystyle\sim\frac{1}{|{\bf{y}}|}+{\mathcal{O}}\left(|{\bf{y}}|^{-2}\right)\quad\textrm{as}\quad|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty\,,

(7.206d)

where
{\Gamma}_{c}\asymp{\varepsilon}^{-1}\partial\Omega_{c}^{\varepsilon}
. The tilde
highlights that we changed here the normalization of

\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}c}
by imposing (
7.206d
). Comparing
this decay with the asymptotic behavior (
D.311
) of an
equivalent eigenfunction
\Psi_{k^{\prime}c}
with the
conventional
L^{2}({\Gamma}_{c})
normalization, we deduce that

\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}c}=\frac{2\pi}{\mu_{k^{\prime}c}\,d_{k^{\prime}c}}\Psi_{k^{\prime}c}\,,

(7.207)

with
d_{k^{\prime}c}
being defined in (
D.301
).
We conclude that

\int\limits_{{\Gamma}_{c}}[\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}c}({\bf{y}})]^{2}d{\bf{y}}=\left(\frac{2\pi}{\mu_{k^{\prime}c}\,d_{k^{\prime}c}}\right)^{2}\,.

(7.208)

Moreover, in our analysis below, we assume that the remaining
N-M

patches are not in near-resonance in the sense that
\sigma_{0}\neq\mu_{ki}
for all
k\geq 0
and all
i=M+1,\ldots N
.

As similar to the analysis in §
7.1
, in the outer region
we expand
u
as in (
6.140
) to obtain (
6.141
) at each
order. We then expand the Steklov eigenvalue as in
(
6.142
), where
\sigma_{0}=\mu_{k^{\prime}c}
. In the
inner regions near each Steklov patch, we expand the inner solution as
in (
6.143
) to obtain the inner problems (
7.183
) at
each order.

In contrast to the analysis for the non-resonant case, we obtain in
place of (
7.186
) that the leading-order inner solutions are now

\begin{split}V_{0i}&=A_{i}\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}c}({\bf{y}})\,,\quad i=1,\ldots,M\,,\\
V_{0i}&=U_{0}\left(1-w_{i}({\bf{y}};-\sigma_{0})\right)\,,\quad i=M+1,\ldots,N\,,\end{split}

(7.209)

where
A_{1},\ldots,A_{M}
are constants to be determined. As

|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty
, we have
V_{0i}\to 0
for
i=1,\ldots,M
while

V_{0i}\to U_{0}
for
i=M+1,\ldots,N
. This implies that we can only
match to the leading-order constant outer solution
U_{0}
when

U_{0}=0
. As a result, the leading-order inner solutions near the
non-resonant patches vanish, i.e.
V_{0i}=0
for

i=M+1,\ldots,N
.

Next, by matching the far-field behavior of
V_{0i}
for

i=1,\ldots,M
to the outer correction
U_{1}
by using the far-field
(
7.206d
) for
\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}c}
, we obtain that

U_{1}
satisfies

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\bf{x}}}U_{1}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\Omega\,;\qquad\partial_{n}U_{1}=0\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\partial\Omega\backslash\{{{\bf{x}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{x}}_{M}\}}\,,

(7.210a)

\displaystyle U_{1}

\displaystyle\sim\frac{A_{i}}{|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{1}|}\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad{\bf{x}}\to{\bf{x}}_{i}\in\partial\Omega\,,\quad i=1,\ldots,M\,.

(7.210b)

The solvability condition for (
7.210
) yields that

\sum_{i=1}^{M}A_{i}=0\,.

(7.211)

In terms of an unknown constant
\overline{U}_{1}
, the
solution to (
7.210
) is

U_{1}=\overline{U}_{1}+2\pi\sum_{j=1}^{M}A_{j}G_{s}({\bf{x}};{\bf{x}}_{j})\,,\quad

(7.212)

where
G_{s}({\bf{x}};{\bf{x}}_{j})
is the surface Neumann Green’s function of
(
3.43
). By using the local behavior of
G_{s}
given in
(
3.45
) in terms of geodesic coordinates, we obtain as

{\bf{x}}\to{\bf{x}}_{i}
that for the resonant patches

\begin{split}U_{1}&\sim\frac{A_{i}}{{\varepsilon}|{\bf{y}}|}-\frac{A_{i}}{2}\log\left(\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\right)-\frac{A_{i}}{2}\left(\log(y_{3}+|{\bf{y}}|)-\frac{y_{3}(y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2})}{|{\bf{y}}|^{3}}\right)\\
&\qquad+\beta_{ci}+\overline{U}_{1}\,,\quad\mbox{for}\quad i=1,\ldots,M\,.\end{split}

(7.213a)

In (
7.213a
), we have defined
\beta_{ci}
as the

i
-th component of the vector
{\bm{\beta}}_{c}
defined by

{\bm{\beta}}_{c}\equiv 2\pi{\mathcal{G}}_{sc}{\bf{A}}\,,\qquad\mbox{where}\qquad{\bf{A}}=(A_{1},\ldots,A_{M})^{T}\,.

(7.213b)

Here
{\mathcal{G}}_{sc}
is the
M\times M
Green’s matrix representing
long-range interactions over the resonant patches, defined by

{\mathcal{G}}_{sc}\equiv\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}R_{s}&G_{12}&\cdots&G_{1M}\\
G_{21}&R_{s}&\cdots&G_{2M}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
G_{M1}&\cdots&G_{M,M-1}&R_{s}\end{array}\right)\,,\quad R_{s}\equiv-\frac{9}{20\pi}\,,\quad G_{ij}\equiv G_{s}({\bf{x}}_{i};{\bf{x}}_{j})\,.

(7.213c)

In contrast, for the non-resonant patches, we have as
{\bf{x}}\to{\bf{x}}_{i}
that

U_{1}\sim\overline{U}_{1}+2\pi\sum_{j=1}^{M}A_{j}G_{s}({\bf{x}}_{i};{\bf{x}}_{j})\,,\quad\mbox{for}\quad i=M+1,\ldots,N\,.

(7.214)

We observe upon comparing (
7.213a
) and
(
7.214
) that the
{\mathcal{O}}(\log{\varepsilon})
term only
occurs for the resonant patches. As a result, in the inner expansion
(
6.143
) we conclude that
V_{1i}=0
for the non-resonant
patches
i=M+1,\ldots,N
. Alternatively, for the resonant patches

i=1,\ldots,M
, we obtain from the matching condition between the
inner and outer solutions that
V_{1i}
satisfies

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\bf{y}}}V_{1i}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf{y}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{3}\,,

(7.215a)

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{3}}V_{1i}+\sigma_{0}V_{1i}

\displaystyle=-\sigma_{1}V_{0i}\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\in{\Gamma}_{c}\,,

(7.215b)

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{3}}V_{1i}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\notin{\Gamma}_{c}\,,

(7.215c)

\displaystyle V_{1i}

\displaystyle\sim-\frac{A_{i}}{2}+{\mathcal{O}}(|{\bf{y}}|^{-1})\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty\,.

(7.215d)

To derive the solvability condition for (
7.215
), which will
determine
\sigma_{1}
, we need the following lemma:

Lemma 4
.

Consider the inhomogeneous problem for
V({\bf{y}})
given by

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\bf{y}}}V

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf{y}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{3}\,,

(7.216a)

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{3}}V+\sigma_{0}V

\displaystyle={\mathcal{R}}(y_{1},y_{2})\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\in{\Gamma}_{c}\,,

(7.216b)

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{3}}V

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\notin{\Gamma}_{c}\,,

(7.216c)

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle\sim V_{\infty}+{\mathcal{O}}(|{\bf{y}}|^{-1})\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty\,,

(7.216d)

where
V_{\infty}
is a constant. A necessary and sufficient
condition for (
7.216
) to have a solution is that

\int_{{\Gamma}_{c}}\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}c}{\mathcal{R}}\,dy_{1}dy_{2}=2\pi V_{\infty}\,,

(7.217)

where
\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}c}
is the unique solution to
(
7.206
) with
\sigma_{0}=\mu_{k^{\prime}c}
. When
(
7.217
) holds, the solution
V
is unique up to adding
an arbitrary multiple of
\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}c}
.

Proof.

To prove the necessity of (
7.217
) we apply
Green’s second identity to
V
and
\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}c}
over
a large hemisphere of radius
R
in the upper half-space to
obtain

\begin{split}\int\limits_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{3}}\left(V\Delta_{{\bf{y}}}\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}c}-\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}c}\Delta_{{\bf{y}}}V\right)d{\bf{y}}&=\int\limits_{{\Gamma}_{c}}\left[\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}c}\left(\partial_{y_{3}}V+\sigma_{0}V\right)-V\left(\partial_{y_{3}}\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}c}+\sigma_{0}\tilde{\Psi_{k^{\prime}c}}\right)\right]d{\bf{y}}\\
&\qquad+2\pi\lim_{R\to\infty}R^{2}\left(V\frac{\partial\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}c}}{\partial|{\bf{y}}|}-\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}c}\frac{\partial V}{\partial|{\bf{y}}|}\right)\Big|_{|{\bf{y}}|=R}\,.\end{split}

(7.218)

Then, upon imposing the conditions (
7.216b
) and
(
7.206b
) on the patches, together with using the far-field
behaviors (
7.216d
) and (
7.206d
), we readily
obtain that (
7.218
) reduces to (
7.217
).
This proves the necessity of (
7.217
).

We now prove the sufficiency of (
7.217
). Since

\{\Psi_{kc}|_{{\Gamma}_{c}}\}
form a complete orthonormal basis of

L^{2}({\Gamma}_{c})
, any harmonic function
V
in
{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{3}
that decays
at infinity and satisfies the mixed Robin-Neumann conditions
(
7.216b
) and (
7.216c
) can be represented
in terms of the eigenfunctions
\Psi_{kc}
. In other words, a
general solution
V
to (
7.216
) can be written as

V({\bf{y}})=V_{\infty}+\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\nu_{k}\,\Psi_{kc}({\bf{y}})\,,

(7.219)

with suitable coefficients
\nu_{k}
. By construction, it
satisfies (
7.216a
), (
7.216c
), and
(
7.216d
). Substituting this representation into
(
7.216b
), multiplying it by
\Psi_{jc}
, integrating
over
{\bf{y}}\in{\Gamma}_{c}
and using the orthonormality of
\{\Psi_{kc}\}
, we
obtain that

\nu_{j}(\sigma_{0}-\mu_{jc})=\int\limits_{{\Gamma}_{c}}({\mathcal{R}}({\bf{y}})-\sigma_{0}V_{\infty})\Psi_{jc}({\bf{y}})\,d{\bf{y}}\,.

(7.220)

Since
\sigma_{0}=\mu_{k^{\prime}c}
, we observe that we must have

\int_{{\Gamma}_{c}}({\mathcal{R}}({\bf{y}})-\sigma_{0}V_{\infty})\Psi_{k^{\prime}c}({\bf{y}})\,d{\bf{y}}=0
, which is equivalent to
(
7.217
), as is readily seen by using the divergence
theorem. When this condition holds,
\nu_{k^{\prime}}
remains
undetermined (a free parameter). The other coefficients
\nu_{j}
for
any
j\neq k^{\prime}
are uniquely given by (
7.220
).

Finally, when (
7.217
) holds, the general solution
V

to (
7.216
) can be written as

V=V_{p}+B\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}c}
, where
B
is an arbitrary
constant and where
V_{p}
is the particular solution of
(
7.216
) satisfying

V_{p}\sim V_{\infty}+{\mathcal{O}}(|{\bf{y}}|^{-2})
as
|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty
. Since
\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}c}\sim{1/|{\bf{y}}|}
as

|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty
, it follows that
V\sim V_{\infty}+{B/|{\bf{y}}|}
as

|y|\to\infty
, where
B
is arbitrary.

To determine
\sigma_{1}
from (
7.215
) we simply apply the
solvability condition (
7.217
) of Lemma

4
where we set
V_{\infty}={-A_{i}/2}
, and

{\mathcal{R}}=-\sigma_{1}V_{0i}
with

V_{0i}=A_{i}\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}c}
. In this way, by using
(
7.208
), we readily determine in terms of

\mu_{k^{\prime}c}
and the weight
d_{k^{\prime}c}=\int_{\Gamma_{c}}\Psi_{k^{\prime}c}\,d{\bf{y}}
that

\sigma_{1}=\frac{\pi}{\int_{{\Gamma}_{c}}(\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}c})^{2}\,d{\bf{y}}}=\frac{\mu_{k^{\prime}c}^{2}d_{k^{\prime}c}^{2}}{4\pi}\,.

(7.221)

Without loss of generality, as shown in Lemma
4
we
are free to impose that

V_{1i}\sim-{A_{i}/2}+{\mathcal{O}}(|{\bf{y}}|^{-2})
as
|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty
,
which ensures that
V_{1i}
is unique. As a result, from the matching
condition we obtain that the outer correction
U_{2}
in
(
6.140
) satisfies (
6.141
), with no singularities at any

{\bf{x}}_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,N
. We conclude that
U_{2}=\overline{U}_{2}
,
where the constant
\overline{U}_{2}
can only be obtained at higher
order.

Next, we proceed to determine the SN eigenvalue correction
\sigma_{2}
.
For the non-resonant patches
i=M+1,\ldots,N
, we set
V_{0i}=0
in
(
7.183
) and use the local behavior (
7.214
)
to derive that the inner correction
V_{2i}
satisfies

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\bf{y}}}V_{2i}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf{y}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{3}\,,

(7.222a)

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{3}}V_{2i}+\sigma_{0}V_{2i}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\in{\Gamma}_{c}\,,

(7.222b)

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{3}}V_{2i}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\notin{\Gamma}_{c}\,,

(7.222c)

\displaystyle V_{2i}

\displaystyle\sim\overline{U}_{1}+2\pi\sum_{j=1}^{M}A_{j}G_{s}({\bf{x}}_{i};{\bf{x}}_{j})\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty\,.

(7.222d)

The solution to (
7.222
) for
i=M+1,\ldots,N
is

V_{2i}=\left(\gamma_{i}+\overline{U}_{1}\right)\left(1-w_{i}({\bf{y}};-\sigma_{0})\right)\,,\quad\mbox{where}\quad\gamma_{i}\equiv 2\pi\sum_{j=1}^{M}A_{j}G_{s}({\bf{x}}_{i};{\bf{x}}_{j})\,,

(7.223)

which has the far-field behavior

V_{2i}\sim\left(\gamma_{i}+\overline{U}_{1}\right)\left(1-\frac{C_{i}(-\sigma_{0})}{|{\bf{y}}|}\right)\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty\,,\quad i=M+1,\ldots,N\,.

(7.224)

In contrast, for the resonant patches
i=1,\ldots,M
, we obtain from
(
7.183
), together with the
{\mathcal{O}}(1)
terms in the
local behavior (
7.213a
), that the inner correction

V_{2i}
satisfies

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\bf{y}}}V_{2i}

\displaystyle=2y_{3}V_{0i,y_{3}y_{3}}+2V_{0i,y_{3}}\,,\quad{\bf{y}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{3}\,,

(7.225a)

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{3}}V_{2i}+\sigma_{0}V_{2i}

\displaystyle=-\sigma_{2}V_{0i}\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\in{\Gamma}_{c}\,,

(7.225b)

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{3}}V_{2i}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\notin{\Gamma}_{c}\,,

(7.225c)

\displaystyle V_{2i}

\displaystyle\sim\beta_{ci}+\overline{U}_{1}-\frac{A_{i}}{2}\left[\log\left(y_{3}+|{\bf{y}}|\right)-\frac{y_{3}(y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2})}{|{\bf{y}}|^{3}}\right]\,,\,\,\mbox{as}\quad|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty\,.

(7.225d)

To derive the solvability condition for (
7.225
), we
first need to decompose
V_{2i}
so as to account for the
inhomogeneous term in the PDE (
7.225a
) as well as the
term in the square bracket in (
7.225d
) in the far-field
behavior. More specifically, and as very similar to the analysis in Lemma

5
of Appendix
C
, we decompose
V_{2i}

as

V_{2i}=V_{2ip}+V_{2iH}\,,

(7.226)

where
V_{2ip}
is given explicitly in terms of
V_{0i}=A_{i}\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}c}
by

V_{2ip}=\frac{y_{3}^{2}}{2}V_{0i,y_{3}}+\frac{y_{3}}{2}V_{0i}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{y_{3}}V_{0i}(y_{1},y_{2},\eta)\,d\eta+A_{i}{\mathcal{F}}_{c}(y_{1},y_{2})\,,

(7.227)

where
{\mathcal{F}}_{c}
is the unique solution to

\displaystyle\Delta_{S}{\mathcal{F}}_{c}=\left(\frac{1}{2}\partial_{y_{3}}\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}c}|_{y_{3}=0}\right)I_{{\Gamma}_{c}}\,;\quad I_{{\Gamma}_{c}}\equiv\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1\,,&(y_{1},y_{2})\in{\Gamma}_{c}\\
0\,,&(y_{1},y_{2})\notin{\Gamma}_{c}\,,\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}_{c}\sim\left(\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{{\Gamma}_{c}}\partial_{y_{3}}\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}c}|_{y_{3}=0}\,d{\bf{y}}\right)\log\rho_{0}+o(1)\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad\rho_{0}\equiv(y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2})^{1/2}\to\infty\,,

(7.228c)

with
\Delta_{S}{\mathcal{F}}_{c}\equiv{\mathcal{F}}_{c,y_{1}y_{1}}+{\mathcal{F}}_{c,y_{2}y_{2}}
. By applying the divergence theorem
to (
7.206
) we calculate
\int_{{\Gamma}_{c}}\partial_{y_{3}}\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}c}|_{y_{3}=0}\,d{\bf{y}}=-2\pi
. In addition, upon using the relation (
7.206b
)
on
{\Gamma}_{c}
the solution to (
7.228
) is written in terms of
the free-space Green’s function in the plane as

{\mathcal{F}}_{c}({\bf{y}})=-\frac{\mu_{k^{\prime}c}}{4\pi}\int\limits_{{\Gamma}_{c}}\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}c}({\bf{y}}^{\prime})\log|{\bf{y}}-{\bf{y}}^{\prime}|\,d{\bf{y}}^{\prime}\,,

(7.229)

which satisfies
{\mathcal{F}}_{c}\sim-\tfrac{1}{2}\log\rho_{0}+o(1)
as

\rho_{0}\to\infty
.

Then, by repeating a similar calculation as in the proof of Lemma

5
, we conclude that
V_{2iH}
in (
7.226
)
for
i=1,\ldots,M
satisfies

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\bf{y}}}V_{2iH}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf{y}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{3}\,,

(7.230a)

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{3}}V_{2iH}+\sigma_{0}V_{2iH}

\displaystyle=-\sigma_{2}V_{0i}-\sigma_{0}A_{i}{\mathcal{F}}_{c}\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\in{\Gamma}_{c}\,,

(7.230b)

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{3}}V_{2iH}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\notin{\Gamma}_{c}\,,

(7.230c)

\displaystyle V_{2iH}

\displaystyle\sim\beta_{ci}+\overline{U}_{1}\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty\,.

(7.230d)

To determine
\sigma_{2}
from (
7.230
) we simply apply the
solvability condition (
7.217
) of Lemma

4
in which we set
V_{\infty}=\beta_{ci}+\overline{U}_{1}
and

{\mathcal{R}}=-\sigma_{2}V_{0i}-\sigma_{0}A_{i}{\mathcal{F}}_{c}
with

V_{0i}=A_{i}\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}c}
. This yields for
i=1,\ldots,M
that

-2\pi\left(\beta_{ci}+\overline{U}_{1}\right)=A_{i}\left[\sigma_{2}\int_{{\Gamma}_{c}}\left(\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}c}\right)^{2}\,d{\bf{y}}+\int_{{\Gamma}_{c}}\sigma_{0}\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}c}\,{\mathcal{F}}_{c}\,d{\bf{y}}\right]\,,

(7.231)

where we identify
\sigma_{0}\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}c}=-\partial_{y_{3}}\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}c}
on
{\Gamma}_{c}
and

\int_{{\Gamma}_{c}}(\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}c})^{2}\,d{\bf{y}}={\pi/\sigma_{1}}
from (
7.221
).

Finally, upon recalling (
7.213b
) for
\beta_{ci}
and the
constraint (
7.211
), we obtain a matrix eigenvalue
problem for
{\bf{A}}\equiv(A_{1},\ldots,A_{M})^{T}
and the eigenvalue
parameter
\alpha
given by

{\mathcal{G}}_{sc}{\bf{A}}+\frac{\overline{U}_{1}}{2\pi}{\bf e}_{M}=\alpha{\bf{A}}\,,\qquad{\bf e}_{M}^{T}{\bf{A}}=0\,,

(7.232a)

where
{\bf e}_{M}\equiv(1,\ldots,1)^{T}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{M}
. Here
\sigma_{2}
is
related to
\alpha
by

\sigma_{2}=-\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\pi}\left[4\pi^{2}\alpha+{\mathcal{J}}\right]\,,

(7.232b)

with
{\mathcal{J}}
given by

{\mathcal{J}}\equiv\int_{{\Gamma}_{c}}\sigma_{0}\,\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}c}{\mathcal{F}}_{c}\,d{\bf{y}}=-\frac{\mu_{k^{\prime}c}^{2}}{4\pi}\int\limits_{{\Gamma}_{c}}\int\limits_{{\Gamma}_{c}}\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}c}({\bf{y}})\,\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}c}({\bf{y}}^{\prime})\,\log|{\bf{y}}-{\bf{y}}^{\prime}|\,d{\bf{y}}\,d{\bf{y}}^{\prime}\,,

(7.232c)

where in the last equality, we used
\sigma_{0}=\mu_{k^{\prime}c}
and
(
7.229
). By using (
7.207
),
this relation can also be written in terms of the conventionally
normalized eigenfunction
\Psi_{k^{\prime}c}
as

{\mathcal{J}}=-\frac{\pi}{d_{k^{\prime}c}^{2}}\int\limits_{{\Gamma}_{c}}\int\limits_{{\Gamma}_{c}}\Psi_{k^{\prime}c}({\bf{y}})\,\Psi_{k^{\prime}c}({\bf{y}}^{\prime})\,\log|{\bf{y}}-{\bf{y}}^{\prime}|\,d{\bf{y}}\,d{\bf{y}}^{\prime}\,,

(7.232d)

where
d_{k^{\prime}c}=\int_{\Gamma_{c}}\Psi_{k^{\prime}c}\,d{\bf{y}}
. This
relation shows that
{\mathcal{J}}
, and thus the associated correction

\sigma_{2}
to the SN eigenvalue, are independent of the normalization
of
\Psi_{k^{\prime}c}
.

Since
V_{0i}=A_{i}\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}c}
for
i=1,\ldots,M
and
V_{0i}=0

for
i=M+1,\ldots,N
, the PDE normalization condition
(
7.180
) provides the following normalization condition
for the matrix eigenvalue problem (
7.232
):

{\bf{A}}^{T}{\bf{A}}=\sum_{j=1}^{M}A_{j}^{2}\sim\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}\int_{{\Gamma}_{c}}[\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}c}]^{2}\,d{\bf{y}}}\,.

(7.232e)

By taking the inner product of (
7.232a
) with
{\bf e}_{M}
, we
can isolate
\overline{U}_{1}
as

\overline{U}_{1}=-\frac{2\pi}{M}{\bf e}_{M}^{T}{\mathcal{G}}_{sc}{\bf{A}}\,,

(7.233)

where
{\bf{A}}
and
\alpha
is an eigenpair of the
M\times M
matrix
problem

\left({\bf I}-\frac{{\bf e}_{M}{\bf e}_{M}^{T}}{M}\right){\mathcal{G}}_{sc}{\bf{A}}=\alpha{\bf{A}}\,,\quad\mbox{with}\quad{\bf e}_{M}^{T}{\bf{A}}=0\,,

(7.234)

with normalization (
7.232e
). Here
{\bf I}
is the

M\times M
identity matrix.

We complete our analysis by deriving the problem for the outer
correction
U_{3}
in (
6.140
), which satisfies (
6.141
)
with singularity conditions at the patch locations. For the resonant
patches, as discussed in the proof of Lemma (
4
), we
can impose that
V_{2iH}\sim\beta_{ci}+\overline{U}_{1}+{B_{i}/|{\bf{y}}|}

as
|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty
, where
B_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,M
are unknown
constants. For the non-resonant patches, we have that
(
7.224
) provides the singularity behavior for
U_{3}
. In this
way, we obtain that
U_{3}
satisfies

\begin{split}\Delta_{{\bf{x}}}U_{3}&=0\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\Omega\,;\quad\partial_{n}U_{3}=0\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\partial\Omega\backslash\{{{\bf{x}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{x}}_{N}\}}\,,\\
U_{3}&\sim-\frac{\left(\gamma_{i}+\overline{U}_{1}\right)C_{i}(-\sigma_{0})}{|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i}|}\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad{\bf{x}}\to{\bf{x}}_{i}\,,\quad i=M+1,\ldots,N,\\
U_{3}&\sim\frac{B_{i}}{|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i}|}\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad{\bf{x}}\to{\bf{x}}_{i}\,,\quad i=1,\ldots,M\,.\end{split}

(7.235)

The solvability condition for (
7.235
) provides one
equation for
(B_{1},\ldots,B_{M})^{T}
:

\sum_{i=1}^{M}B_{i}=\sum_{i=M+1}^{N}\left(\gamma_{i}+\overline{U}_{1}\right)C_{i}(-\sigma_{0})\,,

(7.236)

where
\gamma_{i}
is defined in (
7.223
). A higher-order
analysis can in principle be undertaken to determine a matrix system
for
(B_{1},\ldots,B_{M})^{T}
.

We summarize our result in the following proposition.

Proposition 5
.

Suppose that there are exactly
M
identical patches, with
2\leq M\leq N
, with a common patch shape
\partial\Omega_{c}^{{\varepsilon}}=\partial\Omega_{i}^{{\varepsilon}}
for
i=1,\ldots,M
. Let

\{\mu_{kc}\}_{k\geq 0}
be the spectrum of the local Steklov problem
(
D.294
) of Appendix
D
on the local common patch

{\Gamma}_{c}\asymp{\varepsilon}^{-1}\partial\Omega_{c}^{{\varepsilon}}
. Then, for any

k\geq 0
such that
d_{kc}\neq 0
, the Steklov-Neumann (SN) problem
(
2.14
) has
M-1
eigenvalues
\sigma({\varepsilon})
(counting
multiplicity) for which
\sigma_{0}=\lim\limits_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}\sigma({\varepsilon})=\mu_{kc}
, where
\mu_{kc}
is assumed to be simple. A
three-term expansion for these eigenvalues is

\sigma=\sigma_{0}+{\varepsilon}\log\left(\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\right)\sigma_{1}+{\varepsilon}\sigma_{2}+{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon}^{2}\log{\varepsilon})\,,

(7.237)

where
\sigma_{1}
is given in (
7.221
) and
\sigma_{2}

is related via (
7.232b
) to
M-1
eigenpairs

\alpha
and
{\bf{A}}=(A_{1},\ldots,A_{M})^{T}
of the matrix eigenvalue
problem (
7.234
), with normalization condition
(
7.232e
). Moreover, the local behavior of the
eigenfunctions on the resonant and non-resonant patches are

\begin{split}u|_{{\Gamma}_{i}}&=A_{i}\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}c}+{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon})\,,\quad i=1,\ldots,M\,,\\
u|_{{\Gamma}_{i}}&\sim{\varepsilon}\left(\gamma_{i}+\overline{U}_{1}\right)\left(1-w_{i}({\bf{y}};-\sigma_{0})\right)\,,\quad i=M+1,\ldots,N\,,\end{split}

(7.238)

where
\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}c}
satisfies (
7.206
). In
(
7.238
),
\overline{U}_{1}
and
\gamma_{i}
are defined
in (
7.233
) and (
7.223
),
respectively. In the outer region, the SN eigenfunction is given by

u={\varepsilon}\left(\overline{U}_{1}+2\pi\sum_{j=1}^{M}A_{j}G_{s}({\bf{x}};{\bf{x}}_{j})\right)+{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon}^{2})\,.

(7.239)

We emphasize that in our analysis of this near-resonant case, the
eigenvalue corrections
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
are obtained upon
applying solvability conditions to the inner problems defined near the
resonant patches. For the non-resonant case studied in §
7.1
, these correction terms were found from solvability
conditions on the outer solution. Moreover, in contrast to our main
result in Proposition
4
for the non-resonant case, we
observe that the SN eigenfunctions for this near-resonant case are
concentrated primarily on the resonant patches and that the outer
solution in (
7.239
) is now
{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon})

smaller than that for the non-resonant case.

The results in Proposition
5
for
\sigma_{1}
and

\sigma_{2}
can be simplified for the special case where the common
patch shape
{\Gamma}_{c}
is a disk of radius one (
a_{c}=1
), for which

\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}c}
and

\partial_{y_{3}}\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}c}
, depend only on

\rho_{0}=(y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2})^{1/2}
when
y_{3}=0
and
(y_{1},y_{2})\in{\Gamma}_{c}
. In
this special case, where
{\mathcal{F}}_{c}={\mathcal{F}}_{c}(\rho_{0})
, we
write
{\mathcal{J}}
in (
7.232c
) as

{\mathcal{J}}=2\pi\sigma_{0}\int_{0}^{1}\rho_{0}\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}c}\,{\mathcal{F}}_{c}(\rho_{0})\,d\rho_{0}\,.

(7.240)

Since the problem (
7.228
) for
{\mathcal{F}}_{c}
is
radially symmetric, we find that its first integral is
\rho_{0}{\mathcal{F}}_{c\rho_{0}}=-\tfrac{\sigma_{0}}{2}\int_{0}^{\rho_{0}}\eta\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}c}(\eta)\,d\eta
for
0<\rho_{0}<1
. Upon integrating (
7.240
) by
parts, we use this first integral together with
{\mathcal{F}}_{c}(1)=0
and
\sigma_{0}=\mu_{k^{\prime}c}
, to conclude that

\displaystyle{\mathcal{J}}

\displaystyle=\pi\mu_{k^{\prime}c}^{2}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\left(\int_{0}^{\rho_{0}}\eta\tilde{\Psi}_{k^{\prime}c}(\eta)\,d\eta\right)^{2}\,d\rho_{0}\,,

(7.241a)

\displaystyle=\frac{4\pi^{3}}{d_{k^{\prime}c}^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\left(\int_{0}^{\rho_{0}}\eta\Psi_{k^{\prime}c}(\eta)\,d\eta\right)^{2}\,d\rho_{0}\,,

(7.241b)

where we used (
7.207
) in the second equality.

7.4
Numerical Comparison

We now consider two examples of our theory for the near-resonant case.

Example I:
We first apply our theory to Example II of
§
7.2
for two identical anti-podal circular
patches of radius
{\varepsilon}
centered at the north and south poles, for
which
M=N=2
and
a_{1}=a_{2}=1
. For such anti-podal patches
centered at
{\bf{x}}_{1}=(0,0,1)
and
{\bf{x}}_{2}=(0,0,-1)
, the
2\times 2

Green’s matrix
{\mathcal{G}}_{sc}
in (
7.213c
) is
circulant symmetric and so the only eigenpair of (
7.234
)
is
{\bf{A}}=A_{c}(1,-1)^{T}
and
\alpha=R_{s}-G_{s}({\bf{x}}_{1};{\bf{x}}_{2})
, where
A_{c}
is
a normalization constant. Upon using
R_{s}=-{9/(20\pi)}
and
(
3.43
) for
G_{s}({\bf{x}}_{1};{\bf{x}}_{2})
, we calculate that

\alpha={\left(\log{2}-1\right)/(4\pi)}
. By inserting the
superscript
(k)
to distinguish asymptotics for different
eigenvalues, we conclude from (
7.237
) and
(
7.232b
) that there are SN eigenvalues of
(
2.14
) for each
k\geq 0
given by

\sigma({\varepsilon})\sim\mu_{kc}+{\varepsilon}\log\left(\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\right)\sigma_{1}^{(k)}-\frac{{\varepsilon}\sigma_{1}^{(k)}}{\pi}\left[\pi(\log{2}-1)+{\mathcal{J}}^{(k)}\right]+\ldots\,,

(7.242)

where
\sigma_{1}^{(k)}
and
{\mathcal{J}}^{(k)}
are given by
(
7.221
) and (
7.241
), respectively. The
numerical values for some local Steklov eigenvalues
\mu_{kc}
and
their weights
d_{kc}
are given in Table
3
of
Appendix
D.2
. These values can be used to calculate

\sigma_{1}^{(k)}
from (
7.221
).

To illustrate the accuracy of the three-term expansion
(
7.242
), we employ the accurate numerical method from
Appendix
F
to compute the numerical values

\sigma_{\rm num}^{(j)}
, enumerated by the index
j=1,2,3,\ldots
,
that are considered as benchmarks (we recall that these SN eigenvalues
correspond to axially symmetric eigenfunctions). Figure

9
presents the difference between

\sigma_{\rm num}^{(j)}
with
j=1,3,5,
and the three-term
expansion (
7.242
) for near-resonant SN eigenvalues with
indices
k=1,2,3
. We also present the difference between

\sigma_{\rm num}^{(j)}
with
j=2,4
, and the asymptotic values
given by (
7.204
) for non-resonant eigenvalues for a
single patch with indices
k=1,2
. In both cases, the difference is
shown as a function of
{\varepsilon}^{2}
to outline the correct form of the
asymptotic relations. We can therefore conclude that these relations
are very accurate. We also observe that the asymptotic relation for
the first (nontrivial) eigenvalue
\sigma^{(1)}
is the least
accurate. Note also that the asymptotic relations for non-resonant
eigenvalues are more accurate than those for near-resonant ones.

Figure 9
:

Validation of the three-term expansions for the SN eigenvalues (that
correspond to axially symmetric eigenfunctions) in the case of two
identical circular patches of radius
{\varepsilon}
located at the north and
south poles of the unit sphere. The difference between the numerical
values
\sigma_{\rm num}^{(j)}
obtained by the method in
Appendix
F
with truncation order
n_{\rm max}=2000
, and the
asymptotic values
\sigma_{\rm asy}^{(k)}
given by
(
7.242
) for near-resonant eigenvalues with
k=1,2,3
and by (
7.204
) for non-resonant eigenvalues
with
k=1,2
.

Example II:
Next, we suppose that we have
N

identical circular patches of a common radius
{\varepsilon}
(i.e., all

a_{i}=1
), with centers located at the vertices of one of the five
largest platonic solids that can be inscribed within the unit
sphere. For this spatial configuration of patches, where
M=N
and

N\in\{{4,6,8,12,20\}}
, the Green’s matrix
{\mathcal{G}}_{sc}
is symmetric with a constant row sum, so that
{\mathcal{G}}_{sc}{\bf e}_{M}=\alpha_{M}{\bf e}_{M}
. Note that this matrix is in
general not circulant. It follows that, up to a normalization
condition, the
N-1
mutually orthogonal solutions to
(
7.234
) are

{\bf{A}}_{j}={\bf q}_{j}\,,\quad\mbox{where}\quad{\mathcal{G}}_{sc}{\bf q}_{j}=\alpha_{j}{\bf q}_{j}\,,\quad{\bf q}_{j}^{T}{\bf e}_{M}=0\quad\mbox{for}\quad j=2,\ldots,N\,.

(7.243)

In addition, we obtain from (
7.233
), that we must have

\overline{U}_{1}=0
in (
7.239
). We conclude from
(
7.237
) and (
7.232b
) that, for
each
k\geq 0
, there are
(N-1)
SN eigenvalues of (
2.14
),
enumerated by
j=1,\ldots,N-1
, given by

\sigma({\varepsilon})\sim\mu_{kc}+{\varepsilon}\log\left(\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\right)\sigma_{1}^{(k)}-\frac{{\varepsilon}\sigma_{1}^{(k)}}{\pi}\left(4\pi^{2}\alpha_{j}+{\mathcal{J}}^{(k)}\right)+\ldots\,,\quad j=2,\ldots,N\,.

(7.244)

We remark that
\sigma_{1}^{(k)}
and
{\mathcal{J}}^{(k)}
, as given by
(
7.221
) and (
7.241a
) respectively, are
independent of
N
and of the locations of the vertices. In
this way, determining a three-term expansion for the
(N-1)
SN
eigenvalues that are in near-resonance with an eigenvalue
\mu_{kc}

of the local Steklov problem reduces to simply finding all of the
eigenpairs of the Green’s matrix
{\mathcal{G}}_{sc}
that are
orthogonal to
{\bf e}_{M}
.

In addition, from our analysis in §
7.1
, we observe from
our main result in Proposition
4
that all non-resonant
eigenvalues are close to roots of
C(-\sigma_{0})=0
. These roots are
the eigenvalues
\mu_{k}^{N}
of the local Steklov eigenvalue problem
(
D.323
) of Appendix
D.4
satisfying the
far-field Neumann condition (
D.323d
). In this case, the
expansion (
7.202a
) is independent of the vertex locations
of the platonic solids and reduces to

\sigma({\varepsilon})\sim\mu_{k}^{N}+{\varepsilon}\frac{E(-\mu_{k}^{N})}{C^{\prime}(-\mu_{k}^{N})}+\cdots\,,

(7.245)

which is the same result derived in Example I of §
7.2
for a single Steklov patch.

8
Discussion

In a three-dimensional spherical domain, we have developed and
implemented a unified theoretical approach, based on the method of
matched asymptotic expansions combined with spectral theory, to
analyze the mean first-reaction time (MFRT), the splitting
probability, the Steklov-Dirichlet-Neumann (SDN) problem, and the
Steklov-Neumann (SN) problem for a collection of small partially
reactive patches of arbitrary shape located on the boundary of a
sphere. For the MFRT, our analysis extends that of
[
30
]

where only perfectly reactive and locally circular patches were
considered. In each case, our three-term asymptotic results in the
small-patch limit have been favorably compared with full numerical
results.

We now discuss a few open problems that are directly related to our
study. Firstly, it would be worthwhile to develop an accurate
numerical scheme, such as in
[
89
]
and
[
76
]
for
the exterior problem, to validate the homogenization result
(
4.99
) for the effective reactivity
k_{\rm eff}
for a
large collection of small, but equi-distributed, patches on the
boundary of a sphere. Secondly, our analysis has been focused only on
problems that are
interior
to the sphere. In the companion
article
[
63
]
, we derive the effective reactivity
rate for the
exterior
problem where there is a large number of
equi-distributed partially reactive surface patches of arbitrary
shape, but with small area, on the boundary of a sphere. This analysis
extends that of
[
87
]
where an effective Robin boundary
condition was derived for the case of perfectly reacting but locally
circular boundary patches. In turn, it would be worthwhile to analyze
the SDN and SN problems
exterior
to a sphere that has a
collection of small partially reactive surface patches. Thirdly, our
analysis of the SDN and SN problems has left open a few technical
issues. In particular, our analysis has provided only the
leading-order term for the Steklov eigenparameter for the special case
where the corresponding eigenfunction is not axially-symmetric on a
circular patch. In addition, for the SN problem, in §
7.3
we have only analyzed in detail the
near-resonant
case that will occur when a subset of the patches are
identical. We did not provide a similar analysis to investigate
near-resonance
behavior in other, less generic, situations, involving
non-identical patches, or when the local Steklov eigenvalue
problem near a patch has non-degenerate eigenvalues.

As we stated earlier, the leading-order terms of the derived
asymptotic expansions are independent of the actual shape of the
bounded domain and thus readily applicable to an arbitrary bounded 3-D
domain
\Omega
with a smooth boundary
\partial\Omega
that contains
a collection of
N
partially reactive surface patches.
An important, but completely open, direction is to provide a
higher-order asymptotic analysis of our four problems in this
more general situation. In this extension, the following surface
Neumann Green’s function with singularity
{\bf{x}}_{i}\in\partial\Omega

plays a key role:

\Delta_{{\bf{x}}}G_{s}=\frac{1}{|\Omega|}\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\Omega\,;\quad\partial_{n}G_{s}=\delta({\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i})\,,\quad{\bf{x}}\in\partial\Omega\,;\quad\int_{\Omega}G_{s}\,d{\bf{x}}=0\,.

(8.246a)

The local behavior near the singularity
{\bf{x}}_{i}\in\partial\Omega
is

[
110
]

G_{s}({\bf{x}};{\bf{x}}_{i})\sim\frac{1}{2\pi|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i}|}-\frac{H_{i}}{4\pi}\log\left[|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i}|-\hat{n}{\bf\cdot}({\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i})\right]+R_{i}+o(1)\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad{\bf{x}}\to{\bf{x}}_{i}\,,

(8.246b)

where
H_{i}
is the mean curvature to
\partial\Omega
at
{\bf{x}}={\bf{x}}_{i}

(with
H_{i}=1
for the unit sphere), and where the constant
R_{i}
is
the regular part. Two key challenges are that analytical solutions for

G_{s}
are not known for non-spherical domains and that an efficient numerical
scheme to compute
G_{s}
and
R_{i}
is difficult owing to the intricate
singularity behavior (
8.246b
) and the need to ensure the
global condition
\int_{\Omega}G_{s}\,d{\bf{x}}=0
.

However, by adopting an orthogonal curvilinear coordinate system near
each patch, inner problems very similar to that derived for the MFRT
in a sphere using geodesic normal coordinates will be obtained. From
a preliminary asymptotic analysis of the MFRT satisfying (
2.10
)
in an arbitrary 3-D domain with smooth boundary, we can readily derive
a two-term asymptotic expansion for
\overline{u}
in the form

\overline{u}\sim\frac{|\Omega|}{2\pi\overline{C}{\varepsilon}}\left(1-\left(\frac{1}{2\overline{C}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}H_{i}C_{i}^{2}\right){\varepsilon}\log{\varepsilon}+{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon})\right)\,,

(8.247)

where
\overline{C}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}C_{i}
and
C_{i}=C_{i}(\kappa_{i})
is the
local reactive capacitance of the
i
-th patch. It is an open problem
to extend this calculation for the volume-average MFRT to higher
order, and to undertake a similar approach to calculate the splitting
probability and to analyze the SDN and SN problems in an arbitrary 3-D
domain.

Appendix A
Geodesic Normal Coordinates to the Unit Sphere

\Omega

We define geodesic normal coordinates

{\bf{\xi}}=(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})^{T}\in\left({-\pi/2},{\pi/2}\right)\times\left(-\pi,\pi\right)\times[0,1]
in
\Omega\cup\partial\Omega
so
that
{\bf{\xi}}=0
corresponds to
{\bf{x}}_{i}\in\partial\Omega
, with
\xi_{3}>0

corresponding to the interior of
\Omega
. Geodesics on

\partial\Omega
are obtained by setting
\xi_{3}=0
and fixing either

\xi_{1}=0
or
\xi_{2}=0
. In terms of the spherical angles

\theta_{i}\in(0,\pi)
and
\varphi_{i}\in[0,2\pi)
(see
Fig.
1(b)
), and for
|{\bf{x}}_{i}|=1
, we define the orthonormal
vectors

{\bf{x}}_{i}\equiv\begin{bmatrix}\cos\varphi_{i}\,\sin\theta_{i}\\
\sin\varphi_{i}\,\sin\theta_{i}\\
\cos\theta_{i}\end{bmatrix}\,,\,\,\,{\bf{v}}_{2i}=\partial_{\theta}{\bf{x}}_{i}\equiv\begin{bmatrix}\cos\varphi_{i}\,\cos\theta_{i}\\
\sin\varphi_{i}\,\cos\theta_{i}\\
-\sin\theta_{i}\end{bmatrix}\,,\,\,\,{\bf{v}}_{3i}={\bf{x}}_{i}{\bf\times}\partial_{\theta}{\bf{x}}_{i}\equiv\begin{bmatrix}-\sin\varphi_{i}\\
\cos\varphi_{i}\\
0\end{bmatrix}\,.

(A.248)

The vectors
{\bf{v}}_{2i}
and
{\bf{v}}_{3i}
span the tangent plane to the
sphere at
{\bf{x}}={\bf{x}}_{i}
. We now define the geodesic normal coordinates

{\bf{\xi}}=(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})^{T}
by the global transformation

{\bf{x}}({\bf{\xi}})=\left(1-\xi_{3}\right)\left(\cos\xi_{1}\,\cos\xi_{2}\,{\bf{x}}_{i}+\cos\xi_{1}\,\sin\xi_{2}\,{\bf{v}}_{2i}+\sin\xi_{1}{\bf{v}}_{3i}\right)\,.

(A.249)

We observe that
\xi_{3}
measures the distance of
{\bf{x}}
to

\partial\Omega
. The curves obtained by setting

\xi_{3}=0
, and fixing either
\xi_{2}=0
or
\xi_{1}=0
are, respectively,

{\bf{x}}(\xi_{1},0,0)=\cos\xi_{1}\,{\bf{x}}_{i}+\sin\xi_{1}\,{\bf{v}}_{3i}
or

{\bf{x}}(0,\xi_{2},0)=\cos\xi_{2}\,{\bf{x}}_{i}+\sin\xi_{2}\,{\bf{v}}_{2i}
, which correspond
to intersections of
\partial\Omega
with planes spanned by

\{{{\bf{x}}_{i},{\bf{v}}_{3i}\}}
or
\{{{\bf{x}}_{i},{\bf{v}}_{2i}\}}
, respectively.

To transform the Laplacian from cartesian to geodesic
coordinates, we use (
A.249
) to calculate the scale factors

h_{\xi_{j}}\equiv|{\partial{\bf{x}}/\partial\xi_{j}}|
for
j=1,2,3
as

h_{\xi_{1}}=(1-\xi_{3})\,,\qquad h_{\xi_{2}}=(1-\xi_{3})\cos\xi_{1}\,,\qquad h_{\xi_{3}}=1\,.

(A.250)

For the transformation of a generic function

{\mathcal{V}}({\bf{\xi}})\equiv u\left({\bf{x}}({{\bf{\xi}}})\right)
, we calculate that

\begin{split}\Delta_{{\bf{x}}}u&=\frac{1}{h_{\xi_{1}}h_{\xi_{2}}h_{\xi_{3}}}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}\left(\frac{h_{\xi_{2}}h_{\xi_{3}}}{h_{\xi_{1}}}{\mathcal{V}}_{\xi_{1}}\right)+\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{2}}\left(\frac{h_{\xi_{1}}h_{\xi_{3}}}{h_{\xi_{2}}}{\mathcal{V}}_{\xi_{2}}\right)+\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{3}}\left(\frac{h_{\xi_{1}}h_{\xi_{2}}}{h_{\xi_{3}}}{\mathcal{V}}_{\xi_{3}}\right)\right]\,,\\
&=\frac{(1-\xi_{3})^{-2}}{\cos\xi_{1}}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{3}}\left((1-\xi_{3})^{2}\cos\xi_{1}{\mathcal{V}}_{\xi_{3}}\right)+\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}\left(\cos\xi_{1}{\mathcal{V}}_{\xi_{1}}\right)+\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{2}}\left(\frac{1}{\cos\xi_{1}}{\mathcal{V}}_{\xi_{2}}\right)\right]\,,\end{split}

which yields

\Delta_{{\bf{x}}}u={\mathcal{V}}_{\xi_{3}\xi_{3}}-\frac{2}{1-\xi_{3}}{\mathcal{V}}_{\xi_{3}}+\frac{1}{(1-\xi_{3})^{2}\cos^{2}\xi_{1}}{\mathcal{V}}_{\xi_{2}\xi_{2}}+\frac{1}{(1-\xi_{3})^{2}\cos\xi_{1}}\frac{\partial}{\partial_{\xi_{1}}}\left(\cos\xi_{1}{\mathcal{V}}_{\xi_{1}}\right)\,.

(A.251)

Next, by introducing the inner, or local variables,

{\bf{y}}=(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3})^{T}
, defined by

\xi_{1}={\varepsilon}y_{1}\,,\qquad\xi_{2}={\varepsilon}y_{2}\,,\qquad\xi_{3}={\varepsilon}y_{3}\,,

(A.252)

we use the Taylor approximations
(1-\xi_{3})^{-1}\sim 1+{\varepsilon}y_{3}
,

(1-\xi_{3})^{-2}\sim 1+2{\varepsilon}y_{3}
,
\cos^{2}\xi_{1}=1+{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon}^{2})
and

\sin\xi_{1}\sim{\varepsilon}y_{1}
. We readily obtain that (
A.251
) reduces
to (
3.16
).

To determine a two-term approximation for the Euclidian distance

|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i}|
near the patch, we use (
A.252
) in
(
A.249
). From a Taylor series approximation we obtain that

{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i}={\varepsilon}{\bf{b}}_{0}-{\varepsilon}^{2}{\bf{b}}_{1}+{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon}^{3})\,,\qquad|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i}|^{2}={\varepsilon}^{2}{\bf{b}}_{0}^{T}{\bf{b}}_{0}-2{\varepsilon}^{3}{\bf{b}}_{0}^{T}{\bf{b}}_{1}+{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon}^{4})\,,

(A.253a)

where
{\bf{b}}_{0}
and
{\bf{b}}_{1}
are defined by

{\bf{b}}_{0}=-y_{3}{\bf{x}}_{i}+y_{2}{\bf{v}}_{2i}+y_{1}{\bf{v}}_{3i}\,,\qquad{\bf{b}}_{1}=\frac{1}{2}\left(y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2}\right){\bf{x}}_{i}+y_{3}y_{2}{\bf{v}}_{2i}+y_{3}y_{1}{\bf{v}}_{3i}\,.

(A.253b)

By calculating

{\bf{b}}_{0}^{T}{\bf{b}}_{0}=y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2}+y_{3}^{2}\equiv\rho^{2}
and

{\bf{b}}_{0}^{T}{\bf{b}}_{1}={y_{3}\left(y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2}\right)/2}
, we get

|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i}|\sim{\varepsilon}\rho-\frac{{\varepsilon}^{2}y_{3}}{2\rho}\left(y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2}\right)+{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon}^{3})\,,\quad\frac{1}{|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i}|}\sim\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}\rho}\left(1+\frac{{\varepsilon}y_{3}}{2\rho^{2}}(y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2})+{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon}^{2})\right)\,.

(A.254)

In matrix form, and to leading order in
{\varepsilon}
, we can write
(
A.253
) in terms of
{\bf{y}}=(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3})^{T}
and
an orthogonal matrix
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}
as

{\bf{y}}\sim{\varepsilon}^{-1}{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}^{T}({\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i})\,,\quad\mbox{where}\quad{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}\equiv\begin{bmatrix}|&|&|\\
{\bf{v}}_{3i}&{\bf{v}}_{2i}&-{\bf{x}}_{i}\\
|&|&|\end{bmatrix}\quad\rightarrow\quad|{\bf{y}}|\sim{\varepsilon}^{-1}|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i}|\,.

(A.255)

Since
|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{x}}_{i}|={\varepsilon}\rho+{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon}^{3})
when
y_{3}=0
from
(
A.254
) it follows that for a locally circular Robin patch

\partial\Omega_{i}^{{\varepsilon}}
, we have in terms of local geodesic
coordinates that
\left(y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2}\right)^{1/2}\leq a+{\mathcal{O}}({\varepsilon}^{2})
. Moreover, since the scale factor is
h_{\xi_{3}}=1
, the
Robin boundary condition on a circular patch is well-approximated in
the local geodesic coordinates by

-\partial_{y_{3}}U+\kappa U=0\,,\qquad\mbox{for}\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,\,(y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2})^{1/2}\leq a\,.

Finally, by using the scale factors (
A.250
), the surface area
element on the unit sphere, as needed in
(
6.136
), is
d{\bf s}=h_{\xi_{1}}h_{\xi_{2}}|_{\xi_{3}=0}d\xi_{1}d\xi_{2}=\cos(\xi_{1})d\xi_{1}d\xi_{2}
.

Appendix B
Asymptotic Behavior of
C_{i}(\kappa_{i})
as
\kappa_{i}\ll 1
for the Disk

We derive (
3.33
) of Lemma
1
for a
disk-shaped patch of radius
a_{i}
. To do so, we first introduce

{\mathcal{W}}({\bf{y}})
by

w_{i}({\bf{y}})=1-C_{i}{\mathcal{W}}({\bf{y}})\,,

(B.256)

so that, upon dropping the subscript
i
, we obtain from (
3.17
) that

{\mathcal{W}}
satisfies

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\bf{y}}}{\mathcal{W}}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf{y}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{3}\,,

(B.257a)

\displaystyle-\partial_{y_{3}}{\mathcal{W}}+\kappa{\mathcal{W}}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\in{\Gamma}\,,

(B.257b)

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{3}}{\mathcal{W}}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\notin{\Gamma}\,,

(B.257c)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{W}}

\displaystyle\sim B(\kappa)-\frac{1}{|{\bf{y}}|}+\ldots\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty\,,

(B.257d)

where the neglected term in (
B.257d
) is a dipole and
where
B(\kappa)
is related to
C(\kappa)
by

C(\kappa)=\frac{1}{B(\kappa)}\,.

(B.258)

Here
{\Gamma}
is a disk of radius
a
. By applying the divergence
theorem over the hemisphere

\Omega_{R}=\{{{\bf{y}}=(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3})\,|\,|{\bf{y}}|\leq R\,,\,y_{3}\geq 0\}}
,
with boundary
\partial\Omega_{R}
, we get from (
B.257d
) that

\lim_{R\to\infty}\int_{\partial\Omega_{R}}\partial_{n}{\mathcal{W}}\,d{\bf s}=2\pi\,,

(B.259)

where
\partial_{n}
denotes the outward normal derivative to
\partial\Omega_{R}
.

For
\kappa\ll 1
, we expand the solution to (
B.257
) as

{\mathcal{W}}=\frac{b_{0}}{\kappa}+\left({\mathcal{W}}_{1}+b_{1}\right)+\kappa\left({\mathcal{W}}_{2}+b_{2}\right)+\kappa^{2}\left({\mathcal{W}}_{2}+b_{3}\right)+\ldots\,.

(B.260)

We substitute (
B.260
) into (
B.257
) and collect
powers of
\kappa
. At leading-order, we choose
b_{0}
so that

{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
satisfies

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\bf{y}}}{\mathcal{W}}_{1}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf{y}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{3}\,,

(B.261a)

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{3}}{\mathcal{W}}_{1}

\displaystyle=b_{0}\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\in{\Gamma}\,,

(B.261b)

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{3}}{\mathcal{W}}_{1}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\notin{\Gamma}\,,

(B.261c)

\displaystyle\lim_{R\to\infty}\int_{\partial\Omega_{R}}\partial_{n}{\mathcal{W}}_{1}\,d{\bf s}

\displaystyle=2\pi\,,\qquad{\mathcal{W}}_{1}\sim-\frac{1}{|{\bf{y}}|}+o(1)\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty\,.

(B.261d)

The
o(1)
condition in (
B.261d
) determines
{\mathcal{W}}_{1}

uniquely. By applying the divergence theorem to (
B.261
) over

\Omega_{R}
, we let
R\to\infty
to obtain
2\pi-b_{0}\pi a^{2}=0
, so
that

b_{0}=\frac{2}{a^{2}}\,.

(B.262)

At higher order in
\kappa
, for each
m=2,3,\ldots
, we will
choose
b_{m-1}
so that
{\mathcal{W}}_{m}
is the unique solution to

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\bf{y}}}{\mathcal{W}}_{m}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf{y}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{3}\,,

(B.263a)

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{3}}{\mathcal{W}}_{m}

\displaystyle={\mathcal{W}}_{m-1}+b_{m-1}\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\in{\Gamma}\,,

(B.263b)

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{3}}{\mathcal{W}}_{m}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\notin{\Gamma}\,,

(B.263c)

\displaystyle\lim_{R\to\infty}\int_{\partial\Omega_{R}}\partial_{n}{\mathcal{W}}_{m}\,d{\bf s}

\displaystyle=0\,,\qquad{\mathcal{W}}_{m}\sim o(1)\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty\,.

(B.263d)

By applying the divergence theorem to (
B.263
) we obtain that

\int_{{\Gamma}}\left({\mathcal{W}}_{m-1}+b_{m-1}\right)\,d{\bf s}=0
, which
determines
b_{m-1}
as

b_{m-1}=-\frac{1}{\pi a^{2}}\int_{{\Gamma}}{\mathcal{W}}_{m-1}(y_{1},y_{2},0)\,dy_{1}\,dy_{2}\,,\qquad m=2,3,\ldots\,.

(B.263e)

With
b_{0}
determined in (
B.262
), we use the method of images
to calculate
{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
as

{\mathcal{W}}_{1}({\bf{y}})=-\frac{1}{\pi a^{2}}\int_{{\Gamma}}\frac{d\xi_{1}d\xi_{2}}{\left[(\xi_{1}-y_{1})^{2}+(\xi_{2}-y_{2})^{2}+y_{3}^{2}\right]^{1/2}}\,.

(B.264)

In particular, for
y_{3}=0
, and with
\rho_{0}=(y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2})^{1/2}\leq a
, the
double integral in (
B.264
) can be evaluated as

{\mathcal{W}}_{1}(y_{1},y_{2},0)=-\frac{4}{\pi a}E\left({\rho_{0}/a}\right)\,,\quad 0\leq\rho_{0}\leq a\,,

(B.265)

where
E(z)\equiv\int_{0}^{\pi/2}\sqrt{1-z^{2}\sin^{2}\theta}\,d\theta
is the
complete elliptic integral of the second kind. By using
(
B.263e
) with
m=2
, and exploiting radial symmetry, we conclude
that

b_{1}=\frac{8}{\pi a^{3}}\int_{0}^{a}\rho_{0}E\left({\rho_{0}/a}\right)\,d\rho_{0}=\frac{8}{\pi a}\int_{0}^{1}zE(z)\,dz=\frac{16}{3\pi a}\,,

(B.266)

where we have used
\int_{0}^{1}zE(z)\,dz={2/3}
from (5.112) of

[
45
]
.

By using the method of images we calculate that

{\mathcal{W}}_{2}({\bf{y}})=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{{\Gamma}}\frac{4(\pi a)^{-1}E\left({|\xi|/a}\right)-b_{1}}{\left[(\xi_{1}-y_{1})^{2}+(\xi_{2}-y_{2})^{2}+y_{3}^{2}\right]^{1/2}}\,d\xi_{1}d\xi_{2}\,,

(B.267)

where
|\xi|=(\xi_{1}^{2}+\xi_{2}^{2})^{1/2}
. By evaluating (
B.267
) on

y_{3}=0
, we obtain on the patch
0\leq\rho_{0}\leq a
that

{\mathcal{W}}_{2}(y_{1},y_{2},0)=-\frac{2a}{\pi}b_{1}E\left(\frac{\rho_{0}}{a}\right)+\frac{2}{\pi^{2}a}\int_{{\Gamma}}\frac{E\left({|\xi|/a}\right)}{\left[(\xi_{1}-y_{1})^{2}+(\xi_{2}-y_{2})^{2}\right]^{1/2}}\,d\xi_{1}d\xi_{2}\,.

(B.268)

Upon substituting (
B.268
) into (
B.263e
) with

m=3
, we obtain that

b_{2}=\frac{4b_{1}}{\pi a}\int_{0}^{a}\rho_{0}E\left(\frac{\rho_{0}}{a}\right)\,d\rho_{0}-\frac{4}{\pi^{2}a^{3}}\int_{0}^{a}\rho_{0}{\mathcal{J}}(\rho_{0})\,d\rho_{0}\,,

(B.269)

where
{\mathcal{J}}(\rho_{0})
is defined by

{\mathcal{J}}(\rho_{0})\equiv\int_{{\Gamma}}\frac{E\left({|\xi|/a}\right)}{\left[(\xi_{1}-y_{1})^{2}+(\xi_{2}-y_{2})^{2}\right]^{1/2}}\,d\xi_{1}d\xi_{2}\,,\qquad\rho_{0}=\sqrt{y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2}}\,.

(B.270)

By using (
B.266
) for
b_{1}
and
\int_{0}^{1}zE(z)\,dz={2/3}
we
can calculate the first term in (
B.269
). Then, by writing the
second integral in (
B.270
) in polar coordinates we obtain that

b_{2}=\frac{128}{9\pi^{2}}-\frac{8}{\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\eta{\mathcal{H}}(\eta)\,d\eta\,,\quad\mbox{where}\quad{\mathcal{H}}(\eta)\equiv\int_{0}^{\pi}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{E(r)r\,dr\,d\theta}{\left[r^{2}+\eta^{2}-2r\eta\cos\theta\right]^{1/2}}\,.

(B.271)

Next, we use
\int_{0}^{\pi}\left[1+\beta^{2}-2\beta\cos\theta\right]^{-1/2}\,d\theta=2(1+\beta)^{-1}K\left({2\sqrt{\beta}/(1+\beta)}\right)
for

0\leq\beta<1
, where
K(z)
is the complete elliptic integral of the first
kind of modulus
z
(see (3.6.17) of
[
45
]
). We conclude from
(
B.271
) that

{\mathcal{H}}(\eta)=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{2rE(r)}{r+\eta}K\left(\frac{2\sqrt{\eta r}}{r+\eta}\right)\,dr\,.

(B.272)

We label
A_{0}\equiv\int_{0}^{1}\eta{\mathcal{H}}(\eta)\,d\eta
, in which
we use (
B.272
) for
{\mathcal{H}}(\eta)
. Upon switching the
order of integration and decomposing the resulting integral into two
parts we obtain

A_{0}=\int_{0}^{1}rE(r)\left[\int_{0}^{r}\frac{2\eta}{r+\eta}K\left(\frac{2\sqrt{r\eta}}{r+\eta}\right)\,d\eta+\int_{r}^{1}\frac{2\eta}{r+\eta}K\left(\frac{2\sqrt{r\eta}}{r+\eta}\right)\,d\eta\right]\,dr\,.

(B.273)

To ensure that the modulus of the elliptic functions are on
[0,1]
, we
introduce the new variables
s={\eta/r}
and
s={r/\eta}
in the first and
second integrals of (
B.273
), respectively. This yields that

A_{0}=\int_{0}^{1}2r^{2}E(r)\left[\int_{0}^{1}\frac{s}{1+s}K\left(\frac{2\sqrt{s}}{1+s}\right)\,ds+\int_{r}^{1}\frac{1}{s^{2}(1+s)}K\left(\frac{2\sqrt{s}}{1+s}\right)\,ds\right]\,dr\,.

(B.274)

Since
0\leq s\leq 1
, we use the Landen transformation

K\left({2\sqrt{s}/(1+s)}\right)=(1+s)K(s)
in (
B.274
) to
obtain that

A_{0}=\int_{0}^{1}2r^{2}E(r)\left[\int_{0}^{1}sK(s)\,ds+\int_{r}^{1}s^{-2}K(s)\,ds\right]\,dr\,.

(B.275)

By using the indefinite integrals

\int s^{-2}K(s)\,ds=-s^{-1}E(s)
and

\int sK(s)\,ds=E(s)-(1-s^{2})K(s)
from (6.12.05) and (6.10.01) of

[
45
]
together with
E(0)=K(0)={\pi/2}
, we obtain from
(
B.275
) that

A_{0}=\int_{0}^{1}2r^{2}E(r)\left[E(1)+\left(-E(1)+r^{-1}E(r)\right)\right]\,dr=\int_{0}^{1}2r\left[E(r)\right]^{2}\,dr\,.

(B.276)

In this way, since
A_{0}=\int_{0}^{1}\eta{\mathcal{H}}(\eta)\,d\eta
we
obtain from (
B.271
) and (
B.276
) that

b_{2}=\frac{128}{9\pi^{2}}-\frac{8}{\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}2r\left[E(r)\right]^{2}\,dr\,.

(B.277)

Finally, upon substituting
B(\kappa)\sim b_{0}\kappa^{-1}+b_{1}+b_{2}\kappa
into (
B.258
), we revert the expansion to
conclude that

C(\kappa)\sim\frac{\kappa}{b_{0}}-\frac{b_{1}\kappa^{2}}{b_{0}^{2}}+\frac{\kappa^{3}}{b_{0}^{3}}\left(b_{1}^{2}-b_{0}b_{2}\right)+\ldots\,.

(B.278)

Upon using (
B.262
), (
B.266
) and
(
B.277
) in (
B.278
), we obtain
(
3.33
) of Lemma
1
. Moreover, we
can also readily identify the first three terms in the Taylor expansion
(
3.22a
) as given in (
3.30
).

Appendix C
Inner Problem Beyond Tangent Plane Approximation

Omitting the subscript
i
for the
i
-th patch, we now analyze the
following inner problem that arises at a higher order beyond the
tangent-plane approximation:

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\bf{y}}}\Phi_{2}

\displaystyle=-2\left(y_{3}w_{y_{3}y_{3}}+w_{y_{3}}\right)\,,\quad{\bf{y}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{3}\,,

(C.279a)

\displaystyle-\partial_{y_{3}}\Phi_{2}+\kappa\Phi_{2}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\in{\Gamma}\,,

(C.279b)

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{3}}\Phi_{2}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\notin{\Gamma}\,,

(C.279c)

\displaystyle\Phi_{2}\sim\frac{C}{2}\log(y_{3}+\rho)-

\displaystyle\frac{Cy_{3}}{2\rho^{3}}(y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2})+\frac{E}{\rho}+\ldots\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad\rho\to\infty\,,

(C.279d)

where
\rho=(y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2}+y_{3}^{2})^{1/2}
and
{\Gamma}
is the Robin
patch, which is not necessarily circular. Here
w({\bf{y}})
is the
solution to the leading-order problem (
3.17
), where

C=C(\kappa)
is the coefficient of the monopole in the far-field
behavior (
3.17d
). Our goal is to determine the coefficient

E
of the monopole term in the far-field (
C.279d
). The
result is as follows:

Lemma 5
.

The solution to (
C.279
) can be decomposed as

\Phi_{2}=\Phi_{2p}+\Phi_{2h}\,,

(C.280)

where

\Phi_{2p}=-\frac{y_{3}^{2}}{2}w_{y_{3}}-\frac{y_{3}}{2}w+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{y_{3}}w(y_{1},y_{2},\eta)\,d\eta+{\mathcal{F}}(y_{1},y_{2};\kappa)\,,

(C.281)

and where
{\mathcal{F}}(y_{1},y_{2};\kappa)
, with
\Delta_{S}{\mathcal{F}}\equiv{\mathcal{F}}_{y_{1}y_{1}}+{\mathcal{F}}_{y_{2}y_{2}}
, is the unique solution to

\displaystyle\Delta_{S}{\mathcal{F}}=q(y_{1},y_{2};\kappa)I_{{\Gamma}}\,;\,\,\,q(y_{1},y_{2};\kappa)\equiv-\left(\frac{1}{2}w_{y_{3}}|_{y_{3}=0}\right)\,,\,\,\,I_{{\Gamma}}\equiv\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1\,,&(y_{1},y_{2})\in{\Gamma}\\
0\,,&(y_{1},y_{2})\notin{\Gamma}\,,\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}\sim\frac{C}{2}\log\rho_{0}+o(1)\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad\rho_{0}\equiv(y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2})^{1/2}\to\infty\,.

(C.282c)

The logarithmic growth as
\rho_{0}\to\infty
in
(
C.282c
) follows by applying the divergence theorem to
(
C.282
) and recalling (
3.18
) for
C
. The

o(1)
condition in the far-field (
C.282c
) specifies

{\mathcal{F}}
uniquely. In addition, the far-field behavior is

\Phi_{2p}\sim-\frac{Cy_{3}(y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2})}{2\rho^{3}}+\frac{C}{2}\log(y_{3}+\rho)+o\left({1/\rho}\right)\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad\rho\to\infty\,.

(C.283)

The remaining term
\Phi_{2h}
in (
C.280
) satisfies

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\bf{y}}}\Phi_{2h}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf{y}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{3}\,,

(C.284a)

\displaystyle-\partial_{y_{3}}\Phi_{2h}+\kappa\Phi_{2h}

\displaystyle=-\kappa{\mathcal{F}}\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\in{\Gamma}\,,

(C.284b)

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{3}}\Phi_{2h}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\notin{\Gamma}\,,

(C.284c)

\displaystyle\Phi_{2h}

\displaystyle\sim\frac{E}{\rho}\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad\rho\to\infty\,,

(C.284d)

where the monopole coefficient
E=E(\kappa)
is given explicitly by

E=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{{\Gamma}}q(y_{1},y_{2};\kappa)\,{\mathcal{F}}(y_{1},y_{2};\kappa)\,dy_{1}dy_{2}\,,

(C.285a)

where

{\mathcal{F}}(y_{1},y_{2};\kappa)=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{{\Gamma}}q(y_{1}^{\prime},y_{2}^{\prime},\kappa)\log\left(\left(y_{1}-y_{1}^{\prime}\right)^{2}+\left(y_{2}-y_{2}^{\prime}\right)^{2}\right)\,dy_{1}^{\prime}dy_{2}^{\prime}\,.

(C.285b)

In this way, the far-field behavior (
C.279d
) holds.
Finally, in the limit
\kappa\to 0
, the leading-order asymptotics for

E
is given by (
3.37
).

Proof.

To establish this result, we first show that
\Phi_{2p}
in
(
C.281
) accounts for the the inhomogeneous terms in
(
C.279a
). We readily calculate that

\Phi_{2py_{3}}=-\frac{y_{3}^{2}}{2}w_{y_{3}y_{3}}-\frac{3}{2}y_{3}w_{y_{3}}\,,\quad\Phi_{2py_{3}y_{3}}=-\frac{y_{3}^{2}}{2}w_{y_{3}y_{3}y_{3}}-\frac{5}{2}y_{3}w_{y_{3}y_{3}}-\frac{3}{2}w_{y_{3}}\,.

(C.286)

Moreover, we calculate

\Delta_{S}\Phi_{2p}\equiv\Phi_{2py_{1}y_{1}}+\Phi_{2py_{2}y_{2}}
, and by using

w_{y_{3}y_{3}}=-\Delta_{S}w
from (
3.17a
), we derive

\begin{split}\Delta_{S}\Phi_{2p}&=-\frac{y_{3}^{2}}{2}\partial_{y_{3}}\left[\Delta_{S}w\right]-\frac{y_{3}}{2}\Delta_{S}w+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{y_{3}}\Delta_{S}w\,d\eta+\Delta_{S}{\mathcal{F}}\\
&=\frac{y_{3}^{2}}{2}w_{y_{3}y_{3}y_{3}}+\frac{y_{3}}{2}w_{y_{3}y_{3}}-\frac{1}{2}w_{y_{3}}+\frac{1}{2}w_{y_{3}}|_{y_{3}=0}+\Delta_{S}{\mathcal{F}}\,.\end{split}

(C.287)

Upon adding (
C.286
) and (
C.287
) we conclude that

\Delta_{{\bf{y}}}\Phi_{2p}=\Phi_{2py_{3}y_{3}}+\Delta_{S}\Phi_{2p}=-2\left(y_{3}w_{y_{3}y_{3}}+w_{y_{3}}\right)+\left(\Delta_{S}{\mathcal{F}}+\frac{1}{2}w_{y_{3}}|_{y_{3}=0}\right)\,,

(C.288)

where the terms on the right-hand side in (
C.288
) are
the inhomogeneous terms for this PDE for
\Phi_{2p}
. It follows that

\Phi_{2p}
satisfies (
C.279a
) when
{\mathcal{F}}

satisfies (
C.282
). Consequently,
\Phi_{2h}
satisfies
the homogeneous problem (
C.284a
).

To establish the boundary conditions in (
C.284
)
we observe from (
C.286
) and
(
C.281
) that
\Phi_{2py_{3}}=0
and
\Phi_{2p}={\mathcal{F}}

on
y_{3}=0
. As a result, upon substituting (
C.280
) into
(
C.279b
)–(
C.279c
) we obtain
(
C.284b
)–(
C.284c
).

Next, we determine the asymptotic far-field behavior of
\Phi_{2p}

as defined in (
C.281
). We use
w\sim C\rho^{-1}
as

\rho\to\infty
with
\rho=(y_{3}^{2}+\rho_{0}^{2})^{1/2}
where

\rho_{0}\equiv(y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2})^{1/2}
. We readily calculate for
\rho\to\infty

that

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}y_{3}^{2}w_{y_{3}}-\frac{1}{2}y_{3}w\sim-\frac{C}{2}\frac{y_{3}\rho_{0}^{2}}{\rho^{3}}\,,

(C.289a)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{y_{3}}w(y_{1},y_{2},\eta)\,d\eta\sim\frac{C}{2}\int_{0}^{y_{3}}\frac{1}{\left(\eta^{2}+\rho_{0}^{2}\right)^{1/2}}\,d\eta\sim\frac{C}{2}\left[\log\left(y_{3}+\rho\right)-\log\rho_{0}\right]\,.

(C.289b)

In this way, it follows that
\Phi_{2p}
in (
C.281
) has the
divergent far-field behavior

\Phi_{2p}\sim\frac{C}{2}\log\left(y_{3}+\rho\right)-\frac{Cy_{3}}{2\rho^{3}}\left(y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2}\right)-\frac{C}{2}\log\rho_{0}+{\mathcal{F}}\,.

(C.290)

Therefore, since

{\mathcal{F}}\sim\left({C/2}\right)\log\rho_{0}+o(1)
as

\rho_{0}\to\infty
as specified in (
C.282c
) it follows
from (
C.290
) that

\Phi_{2p}\sim-{Cy_{3}(y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2})/(2\rho^{3})}+\left({C/2}\right)\log(y_{3}+\rho)+o\left({1/\rho}\right)
as

\rho\to\infty
. Finally, since (
C.284
) for

\Phi_{2h}
is a Neumann-Robin BVP with a spatially
inhomogeneous Robin condition on the patch, we have

\Phi_{2h}={\mathcal{O}}(\rho^{-1})
as
\rho\to\infty
. The
expression (
C.285
) for the monopole coefficient results
from using Green’s second identity to the problems (
3.17
) and
(
C.284
) for
w
and
\Phi_{2h}
, respectively, over a
large hemisphere and by calculating
{\mathcal{F}}
using the 2-D
free-space Green’s function. In this way, the far-field behavior
(
C.279d
) for
\Phi_{2}
holds.

Finally, we derive the limiting asymptotics (
3.37
) for

E
. For
\kappa\to 0
, we obtain from (
3.17
) that

w={\mathcal{O}}(\kappa)
so that

q=-\left({1/2}\right)\partial_{y_{3}}w\sim{\kappa/2}
on
{\Gamma}
. We
substitute (
C.285b
) into (
C.285a
) and use

q\sim{\kappa/2}
. Upon eliminating
\kappa
by using
C\sim{\kappa|{\Gamma}|/(2\pi)}
for
\kappa\ll 1
, as obtained from
(
3.22a
) and (
3.23
), we conclude for

\kappa\to 0
that

E\sim-\frac{\kappa^{2}}{8\pi^{2}}\int\limits_{{\Gamma}_{i}}\int\limits_{{\Gamma}_{i}}\log|{\bf{y}}-{\bf{y}}^{\prime}|\,d{\bf{y}}\,d{\bf{y}}^{\prime}=-\frac{C^{2}}{2|{\Gamma}|^{2}}\int\limits_{{\Gamma}_{i}}\int\limits_{{\Gamma}_{i}}\log|{\bf{y}}-{\bf{y}}^{\prime}|\,d{\bf{y}}\,d{\bf{y}}^{\prime}\,,

(C.291)

which establishes (
3.37
). This completes the proof of
Lemma
5
.

We remark that Lemma
5
applies to a patch
{\Gamma}
of
arbitrary shape. However, when
{\Gamma}
is a disk of radius
a
, the
expression for
E
in (
C.285
) can be reduced to quadrature
and we can determine its limiting asymptotics, as was summarized in Lemma

2
.

When
{\Gamma}
is a disk,
q
and
{\mathcal{F}}
in (
C.282
)
depend only on
\rho_{0}=(y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2})^{1/2}
, so that from
(
C.282
) we readily obtain that

{\mathcal{F}}={\mathcal{F}}(\rho_{0};\kappa)
satisfies

\left(\rho_{0}{\mathcal{F}}_{\rho_{0}}\right)_{\rho_{0}}=\rho_{0}q(\rho_{0};\kappa)
. We
integrate this ODE, impose that
{\mathcal{F}}(\rho_{0};\kappa)
has
no singularity at
\rho_{0}=0
, and we substitute the resulting
expression into (
C.285a
). Exploiting radial
symmetry, we determine
E
as

E=-2\int_{0}^{a}\rho_{0}q(\rho_{0};\kappa){\mathcal{F}}(\rho_{0};\kappa)\,d\rho_{0}\,;\qquad{\mathcal{F}}_{\rho_{0}}=\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\int_{0}^{\rho_{0}}\eta q(\eta;\kappa)\,d\eta\,,\quad 0\leq\rho_{0}\leq a\,,

(C.292)

with
{\mathcal{F}}=\left({C/2}\right)\log{a}
at
\rho_{0}=a
. Next, we
integrate (
C.292
) by parts to obtain

E=-2\left[{\mathcal{F}}(\rho_{0};\kappa)\left(\int_{0}^{\rho_{0}}\eta q(\eta;\kappa)\,d\eta\right)\Big|_{0}^{a}-\int_{0}^{a}{\mathcal{F}}_{\rho_{0}}\left(\int_{0}^{\rho_{0}}\eta q(\eta;\kappa)\,d\eta\right)\,d\rho_{0}\right]\,.

(C.293)

Then, upon using
{\mathcal{F}}=\left({C/2}\right)\log{a}
at
\rho_{0}=a
,

C=2\int_{0}^{a}\eta q(\eta;\kappa)\,d\eta
and
{\mathcal{F}}_{\rho_{0}}

from (
C.292
) we find that (
C.293
) reduces
to (
3.38
) in Lemma
2
.

For
\kappa=\infty
, we calculate (
3.38
) analytically.
By using (
3.28
) for
q=q(\rho_{0};\infty)
,

C=C(\infty)={2a/\pi}
and

\int_{0}^{\rho_{0}}{\eta/\sqrt{a^{2}-\eta^{2}}}\,d\eta=a-\sqrt{a^{2}-\rho_{0}^{2}}
, we obtain from (
3.38
) that

\displaystyle E(\infty)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\left[C(\infty)\right]^{2}}{2}\log{a}+\frac{2}{\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{a}\frac{1}{\rho_{0}}\left[a-\sqrt{a^{2}-\rho_{0}^{2}}\right]^{2}\,d\rho_{0}\,,

\displaystyle=-\frac{2a^{2}}{\pi^{2}}\log{a}+\frac{2a^{2}}{\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{1}{x}\left(2-x^{2}-2\sqrt{1-x^{2}}\right)\,dx\,,

\displaystyle=-\frac{2a^{2}}{\pi^{2}}\log{a}+\frac{2a^{2}}{\pi^{2}}\left(\frac{3}{2}-\log{4}\right)\,.

In this way, we recover the expression for
E(\infty)
given in
(
3.40a
) of Lemma
2
.

Finally, we calculate
E
when
\kappa\ll 1
and
{\Gamma}
is a
disk. Instead of using (
C.291
), we can proceed more
directly. When
\kappa\ll 1
, we find from (
3.17
) that

-\partial_{y_{3}}w\sim\kappa
on
y_{3}=0
,
(y_{1},y_{2})\in{\Gamma}
, so that
from (
C.282
),
q(\rho_{0};\kappa)\sim{\kappa/2}
on

0\leq\rho_{0}\leq a
. By evaluating the integrals in
(
3.38
), and using
C\sim{\kappa a^{2}/2}
for

\kappa\ll 1
, we obtain (
3.40b
) of Lemma

2
for
E
when
\kappa\ll 1
.

Appendix D
Computation and Analysis of Reactivity Capacitance

In this Appendix, we derive an exact representation for the reactivity
capacitance
C_{i}(\kappa_{i})
in the general case of an arbitrary patch.
We then analyze its properties for the case of a circular patch.

D.1
Arbitrary patch

The solution
w_{i}({\bf{y}};\kappa_{i})
of the mixed BVP (
3.17
) is
the key element for our asymptotic analysis. As this BVP is
formulated for the
i
-th patch
{\Gamma}_{i}
, independently of the other
patches, its solution can be searched separately for different patches
so that the index
i
will be kept fixed throughout this appendix. As
an explicit solution is not available even for circular patches, we
search for a suitable spectral representation of
w_{i}({\bf{y}};\kappa_{i})
.
For this purpose, we employ the
local
Steklov eigenvalue problem
defined in an upper half-space by

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\bf{y}}}\Psi_{ki}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf{y}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{3}\,,

(D.294a)

\displaystyle\partial_{n}\Psi_{ki}

\displaystyle=\mu_{ki}\Psi_{ki}\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\in{\Gamma}_{i}\,,

(D.294b)

\displaystyle\partial_{n}\Psi_{ki}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\notin{\Gamma}_{i}\,,

(D.294c)

\displaystyle\Psi_{ki}({\bf{y}})

\displaystyle={\mathcal{O}}\left({1/|{\bf{y}}|}\right)\quad\textrm{as}\quad|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty\,.

(D.294d)

This spectral problem can be reduced to the exterior Steklov problem
in the whole space
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
. The latter has a discrete spectrum

[
19
]
, whereas its eigenfunctions are necessarily either
symmetric, or antisymmetric with respect to the horizontal plane. The
symmetric ones satisfy the Neumann boundary condition
(
D.294c
). In the following, we focus only on these
symmetric eigenmodes and enumerate them by the index
k=0,1,2,\ldots
such that the associated eigenvalues form an increasing
sequence:

0<\mu_{0i}<\mu_{1i}\leq\cdots\nearrow+\infty\,,

(D.295)

with the principal eigenvalue
\mu_{0i}
being simple and strictly
positive. Importantly, the restrictions
\Psi_{ki}({\bf{y}})|_{{\Gamma}_{i}}

onto
{\Gamma}_{i}
form a complete orthonormal basis in
L^{2}({\Gamma}_{i})
:

\int_{{\Gamma}_{i}}\Psi_{ki}\Psi_{k^{\prime}i}\,d{\bf{y}}=\delta_{k,k^{\prime}}\,.

(D.296)

As discussed in
[
56
]
, the restriction of the Neumann
Green’s function onto
{\Gamma}_{i}
turns out to be the kernel of an
integral operator that determines the eigenpairs
\mu_{ki}
and

\Psi_{ki}({\bf{y}})
. In our setting, the restriction of the Neumann
Green’s function in the half-space onto a patch on the horizontal
plane is
1/(2\pi|{\bf{y}}-{\bf{y}}^{\prime}|)
and yields the following identity:

\frac{1}{2\pi|{\bf{y}}-{\bf{y}}^{\prime}|}=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\Psi_{ki}({\bf{y}})\Psi_{ki}({\bf{y}}^{\prime})}{\mu_{ki}}\,,\quad\mbox{for}\quad{\bf{y}},{\bf{y}}^{\prime}\in{\Gamma}_{i}\,.

(D.297)

This identity can also be recast as an eigenvalue problem

\int\limits_{{\Gamma}_{i}}\frac{1}{2\pi|{\bf{y}}-{\bf{y}}^{\prime}|}\Psi_{ki}({\bf{y}}^{\prime})\,d{\bf{y}}^{\prime}=\frac{1}{\mu_{ki}}\Psi_{ki}({\bf{y}})\,,\quad\mbox{for}\quad{\bf{y}}\in{\Gamma}\,,

(D.298)

whose eigenpairs are enumerated by
k=0,1,\ldots
.

The completeness of the basis of
\{\Psi_{ki}\}
in
L^{2}({\Gamma}_{i})

allows us to decompose the restriction of
w_{i}({\bf{y}};\kappa_{i})
to

{\Gamma}_{i}
and thus to search the solution of (
3.17
) in the
form:

w_{i}({\bf{y}};\kappa_{i})=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\eta_{ki}\Psi_{ki}({\bf{y}})\,.

(D.299)

The unknown coefficients
\eta_{ki}
can be found from imposing the
boundary condition (
3.17b
):

\sum\limits_{k^{\prime}=0}^{\infty}\eta_{k^{\prime}i}(\mu_{k^{\prime}i}+\kappa_{i})\Psi_{k^{\prime}i}({\bf{y}})=\kappa_{i}\,,\quad\mbox{for}\quad{\bf{y}}\in{\Gamma}_{i}\,.

(D.300)

Multiplying (
D.300
) by
\Psi_{ki}({\bf{y}})
and integrating the
resulting expression over
{\Gamma}_{i}
, we get

\eta_{ki}=\frac{\kappa_{i}\,d_{ki}}{\mu_{ki}+\kappa_{i}}\,,\quad\textrm{with}\quad d_{ki}=\int_{{\Gamma}_{i}}\Psi_{ki}\,d{\bf{y}}\,,

(D.301)

where we used the orthonormality condition (
D.296
). We
therefore deduced the following spectral representation:

w_{i}({\bf{y}};\kappa_{i})=\kappa_{i}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{d_{ki}}{\mu_{ki}+\kappa_{i}}\Psi_{ki}({\bf{y}}),\quad{\bf{y}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}_{+}.

(D.302)

As a consequence, the charge density becomes

q_{i}({\bf{y}};\kappa_{i})=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{n}w_{i}({\bf{y}};\kappa_{i})=\frac{\kappa_{i}}{2}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{d_{ki}\mu_{ki}}{\mu_{ki}+\mu}\Psi_{ki}({\bf{y}})\,,\quad\mbox{for}\quad{\bf{y}}\in{\Gamma}_{i}\,.

(D.303)

Moreover, setting
\kappa_{i}=-\sigma
and evaluating the derivative
with respect to
\sigma
gives

w_{ci}({\bf{y}};-\sigma)=\partial_{\sigma}w_{i}({\bf{y}};-\sigma)=-\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\mu_{ki}d_{ki}}{(\mu_{ki}-\sigma)^{2}}\Psi_{ki}({\bf{y}})\,.

(D.304)

These spectral expansions imply that if
\sigma=\mu_{ki}
for some
integer
k\geq 0
, then there is no solution
w_{i}({\bf{y}};-\sigma)
.

According to the definition (
3.18
), the reactive
capacitance can be found as

C_{i}(\kappa_{i})=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int\limits_{{\Gamma}_{i}}\partial_{n}w_{i}\,d{\bf{y}}=\frac{\kappa_{i}}{2\pi}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\mu_{ki}d_{ki}^{2}}{\mu_{ki}+\kappa_{i}}\,.

(D.305)

Equation (
D.305
) is one of the main results of this appendix.
In particular, its derivative, given by (
3.21
), is strictly
positive. As a result, the capacitance
C_{i}(\infty)
is an upper
bound for
C_{i}(\kappa_{i})
for
\kappa_{i}>0
. Another upper bound,
which is useful for small
\kappa_{i}
, reads

C_{i}(\kappa_{i})\leq\frac{\kappa_{i}}{2\pi}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}d_{ki}^{2}=\frac{\kappa_{i}\,|{\Gamma}_{i}|}{2\pi}\,,

(D.306)

where the second equality follows from (
D.310
), which is
shown below. We conclude that the eigenvalues
\mu_{ki}
and the
spectral weights
d_{ki}
, for which
d_{ki}\neq 0
, fully determine
the reactive capacitance and its properties. It is worth noting that
a dilation of the patch
{\Gamma}_{i}
by
a_{i}>0
implies

\mbox{If}~~{\Gamma}^{\prime}_{i}=a_{i}{\Gamma}_{i}\,,\quad\mbox{then}\quad\mu^{\prime}_{ki}=\mu_{ki}/a_{i},\quad d^{\prime}_{ki}=a_{i}\,d_{ki}\,,

(D.307)

where we used the
L^{2}({\Gamma}_{i})
normalization of eigenfunctions.

In the limit
\kappa_{i}\to 0
, the geometric series expansion of each
fraction in (
D.305
) yields the Taylor expansion

C_{i}(\kappa_{i})=-a_{i}\sum\limits_{n=1}^{\infty}c_{ni}\,(-\kappa_{i}a_{i})^{n}\,,\quad\mbox{with}\quad c_{ni}=\frac{1}{2\pi a_{i}^{n+1}}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{d_{ki}^{2}}{\mu_{ki}^{n-1}}\,.

(D.308)

The coefficient
c_{1i}
can be found by expanding the unity on the
complete basis of eigenfunctions
\{\Psi_{ki}\}
,

\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}d_{ki}\Psi_{ki}({\bf{y}})=1\,,\quad\mbox{for}\quad{\bf{y}}\in{\Gamma}_{i}\,,

(D.309)

while its integral over
{\bf{y}}\in{\Gamma}_{i}
yields

\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}d_{ki}^{2}=|{\Gamma}_{i}|\,,

(D.310)

where
|{\Gamma}_{i}|
denotes the area of
{\Gamma}_{i}
. This shows that
F_{ki}=d_{ki}^{2}/|{\Gamma}_{i}|
can be interpreted as the relative weight of the

k
-th eigenpair. Applying the divergence theorem to
(
D.294
), we observe that the coefficient
d_{ki}

determines the far-field behavior of
\Psi_{ki}({\bf{y}})
in the form

\Psi_{ki}({\bf{y}})\sim\frac{\mu_{ki}d_{ki}}{2\pi|{\bf{y}}|}+\ldots\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty\,.

(D.311)

According to (
D.310
), we get

c_{1i}=\frac{|{\Gamma}_{i}|}{2\pi a_{i}^{2}}\,.

(D.312)

It is convenient to derive a closed-form representation for the
coefficients
c_{2i}
and
c_{3i}
to facilitate their numerical
computation without solving the Steklov eigenvalue problem.
To this end, we integrate (
D.298
) over

{\bf{y}}\in{\Gamma}_{i}
to get

\int\limits_{{\Gamma}_{i}}\Psi_{ki}({\bf{y}})\,\omega_{i}({\bf{y}})\,d{\bf{y}}=\frac{d_{ki}}{\mu_{ki}}\,,\qquad\mbox{where}\qquad\omega_{i}({\bf{y}})\equiv\int\limits_{{\Gamma}_{i}}\frac{d{\bf{y}}^{\prime}}{2\pi|{\bf{y}}-{\bf{y}}^{\prime}|}\,.

(D.313)

Multiplying this relation by
d_{ki}/(2\pi)
and summing over
k
, we find

\displaystyle c_{2i}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi a_{i}^{3}}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{d_{ki}^{2}}{\mu_{ki}}=\frac{1}{2\pi a_{i}^{3}}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\int\limits_{{\Gamma}_{i}}\Psi_{ki}({\bf{y}})\,\omega_{i}({\bf{y}})\,d{\bf{y}}\int\limits_{{\Gamma}_{i}}\Psi_{ki}({\bf{y}}^{\prime})\,d{\bf{y}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi a_{i}^{3}}\int\limits_{{\Gamma}_{i}}\omega_{i}({\bf{y}})\,\left(\int\limits_{{\Gamma}_{i}}\underbrace{\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\Psi_{ki}({\bf{y}})\,\Psi_{ki}({\bf{y}}^{\prime})}_{=\delta({\bf{y}}-{\bf{y}}^{\prime})}\,d{\bf{y}}^{\prime}\right)\,d{\bf{y}}\ =\frac{1}{2\pi a_{i}^{3}}\int\limits_{{\Gamma}_{i}}\omega_{i}({\bf{y}})\,d{\bf{y}}\,,

(D.314)

where we used the completeness of the basis of eigenfunctions

\Psi_{ki}
in
L^{2}({\Gamma})
. In the same vein, we have

\displaystyle c_{3i}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi a_{i}^{4}}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{d_{ki}^{2}}{\mu_{ki}^{2}}=\frac{1}{2\pi a_{i}^{4}}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\int\limits_{{\Gamma}_{i}}\Psi_{ki}({\bf{y}})\,\omega_{i}({\bf{y}})\,d{\bf{y}}\,\int\limits_{{\Gamma}_{i}}\Psi_{ki}({\bf{y}}^{\prime})\,\omega_{i}({\bf{y}}^{\prime})\,d{\bf{y}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi a_{i}^{4}}\int\limits_{{\Gamma}_{i}}\omega_{i}({\bf{y}})\,\left(\int\limits_{{\Gamma}_{i}}\omega_{i}({\bf{y}}^{\prime})\underbrace{\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\Psi_{ki}({\bf{y}})\,\Psi_{ki}({\bf{y}}^{\prime})}_{=\delta({\bf{y}}-{\bf{y}}^{\prime})}\,d{\bf{y}}^{\prime}\right)\,d{\bf{y}}=\frac{1}{2\pi a_{i}^{4}}\int\limits_{{\Gamma}_{i}}\omega_{i}^{2}({\bf{y}})\,d{\bf{y}}\,.

(D.315)

These two representations allow one to compute the coefficients

c_{2i}
and
c_{3i}
numerically for any patch shape without solving
the exterior Steklov problem. In other words, we managed to represent
these coefficients in purely geometric terms.

A numerical computation of the function
\omega_{i}({\bf{y}})
requires
integration of the singular kernel
1/|{\bf{y}}-{\bf{y}}^{\prime}|
. To avoid
technical issues, it is convenient to recall that
\Delta_{2}|{\bf{y}}-{\bf{y}}^{\prime}|={1/|{\bf{y}}-{\bf{y}}^{\prime}|}
, where
\Delta_{2}
is the
two-dimensional Laplace operator. As a consequence, one has

\omega_{i}({\bf{y}})=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int\limits_{{\Gamma}_{i}}\Delta_{2}|{\bf{y}}-{\bf{y}}^{\prime}|\,d{\bf{y}}^{\prime}\,=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int\limits_{\partial{\Gamma}_{i}}\frac{({\bf{n}}_{{\bf{y}}^{\prime}}\cdot({\bf{y}}-{\bf{y}}^{\prime}))}{|{\bf{y}}-{\bf{y}}^{\prime}|}\,d{\bf{y}}^{\prime}\,,

(D.316)

where
{\bf{n}}_{{\bf{y}}^{\prime}}
is the unit normal vector to the boundary of
the patch, oriented outward from
{\Gamma}_{i}
. In this way, the
original integral over a planar region
{\Gamma}_{i}
is reduced to an
integral over its one-dimensional boundary that avoids singularities.
Using
\nabla_{{\bf{y}}}|{\bf{y}}-{\bf{y}}^{\prime}|=({\bf{y}}-{\bf{y}}^{\prime})/|{\bf{y}}-{\bf{y}}^{\prime}|
and the divergence theorem, we can
rewrite the integral in (
D.314
) as

c_{2i}=-\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}a_{i}^{3}}\int\limits_{\partial{\Gamma}_{i}}d{\bf{y}}\int\limits_{\partial{\Gamma}_{i}}|{\bf{y}}-{\bf{y}}^{\prime}|({\bf{n}}_{{\bf{y}}}\cdot{\bf{n}}_{{\bf{y}}^{\prime}})\,d{\bf{y}}^{\prime}\,.

(D.317)

D.2
Circular Patch

For a circular patch
{\Gamma}_{i}
, the exterior Steklov problem was
studied in
[
55
]
. In particular, an efficient numerical
procedure for constructing the eigenvalues and eigenfunctions was
developed by using oblate spheroidal coordinates. Moreover, the axial
symmetry of this setting implies that only axially symmetric
eigenfunctions do contribute to
w_{i}({\bf{y}};\kappa_{i})
in
(
D.302
) and related quantities (in fact, the
coefficients
d_{ki}
in (
D.301
) vanish for non-axially
symmetric eigenfunctions). As a consequence, we can focus exclusively
on axially symmetric eigenmodes that we still enumerate by the index

k=0,1,2,\ldots
.

k

0
