0.0394

0.0339

0.0297

Table 3
:

The first eight Steklov eigenvalues
\mu_{ki}
for the unit disk

{\Gamma}_{i}
(
a_{i}=1
) in the upper half-space that correspond to axially
symmetric eigenfunctions, for which the weights
d_{ki}
are nonzero.
These values were obtained via a numerical diagonalization of a
truncated matrix representing the Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator, with
the truncation order
100
(see details in
[
55
]
). Note
that the reduction of the truncation order to
50
does not affect the
shown digits, assuring the high quality of this computation. The last
two rows present the first eight eigenvalues
\mu_{ki}^{N}
,
corresponding to axially symmetric eigenfunctions, of the Steklov
problem (
D.323
), and the associated weights
F_{ki}^{N}=\pi[\Psi_{ki}^{N}(\infty)]^{2}
; see
[
68
,
56
]
for
their numerical computation.

Using the numerical procedure from
[
55
]
, we compute

\mu_{ki}
and
d_{ki}
by diagonalizing an appropriate truncated
matrix. The first eight eigenvalues
\mu_{ki}
and coefficients

d_{ki}
for the unit disk are shown in Table
3
.
One sees that the principal eigenmode provides the dominant
contribution of
98\%
, the next one gives
1.7\%
, whereas all the
remaining eigenmodes are almost negligible. In other words, the
infinite sum in (
3.22a
) can be truncated to only a few
terms to get very accurate results for
c_{ni}
. For instance,
keeping only the first two terms in the spectral expansion
(
3.22a
) determining
c_{ni}
and substituting the data
from Table
3
, we find

c_{ni}\approx\frac{0.4888}{(1.1578)^{n-1}}+\frac{0.0084}{(4.3168)^{n-1}}\,,\quad\mbox{for}\quad n\geq 2\,.

(D.318)

This explicit approximation gives
c_{2i}\approx 0.4241
and
c_{3i}\approx 0.3651
, which perfectly agree with the exact values from
(
3.30
). While the exact computation of
c_{ni}
rapidly
becomes very cumbersome (see Appendix
B
), the
approximation (
D.318
) is fully explicit.

We also note that an alternative exact computation of
c_{2i}
and

c_{3i}
to that done in Appendix
B
can be achieved
using the integral representations (
D.314
) and
(
D.315
). Setting
{\bf{y}}=(r,\phi)
and
{\bf{y}}^{\prime}=(1,\phi^{\prime})
in polar coordinates, we first evaluate the
integral in (
D.316
) as

\omega_{i}({\bf{y}})=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int\limits_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{r\cos(\phi-\phi^{\prime})-1}{\sqrt{1+r^{2}-2r\cos(\phi-\phi^{\prime})}}=\frac{2}{\pi}E(|{\bf{y}}|)\,,

(D.319)

where
E(z)
is the complete elliptic integral of the second kind.
As a consequence, we get immediately that

c_{2i}=\frac{2}{\pi}\int\limits_{0}^{1}r\,E(r)\,dr=\frac{4}{3\pi}\,,\qquad c_{3i}=\frac{4}{\pi^{2}}\int\limits_{0}^{1}r\,\left[E(r)\right]^{2}\,dr\approx 0.3651\,.

(D.320)

We also observe that
c_{2i}
could also be found directly from
(
D.317
):

c_{2i}=-\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\int\limits_{0}^{2\pi}\left(\int\limits_{0}^{2\pi}\sqrt{2-2\cos(\phi-\phi^{\prime})}\cos(\phi-\phi^{\prime})\,d\phi^{\prime}\right)\,d\phi=\frac{4}{3\pi}\,.

(D.321)

D.3
The Large-Reactivity Limit

While the fast decay of
d_{ki}^{2}
allows one to keep only few terms
in the analysis of the small-reactivity limit
\kappa_{i}\to 0
, all
eigenmodes become relevant in the opposite limit
\kappa_{i}\to\infty

of high reactivity. In the case of the unit disk (i.e.,
a_{i}=1
),
the solution of (
3.17
) can be related to the potential
\Phi

introduced and studied in
[
65
]
. In fact, the divergence
theorem applied to (
3.17
) yields
\kappa_{i}\int_{{\Gamma}_{i}}(1-w_{i}({\bf{y}};\kappa_{i}))\,d{\bf{y}}=2\pi C_{i}(\kappa_{i})
, so that

(1-w_{i})/C_{i}=2\pi\Phi
, where
\Phi
is the unique solution of
equations (12-14) from
[
65
]
. As a consequence, the
asymptotic relations (2,3,11) derived in
[
65
]
(with
D=1
) imply, in our notations, that

C_{i}(\kappa_{i})\approx\frac{2}{\pi}-\frac{2\left(\log\kappa_{i}+\log{2}+\gamma_{e}+1\right)}{\pi^{2}\kappa_{i}}\quad\mbox{as}\quad\kappa_{i}\to\infty\,,

(D.322)

where
\gamma_{e}\approx 0.5772\ldots
is the Euler constant.
Figure
10
illustrates an excellent agreement between
the numerically computed values of
C_{i}(\kappa_{i})
and the asymptotic
relation (
D.322
).

Figure 10
:

Asymptotic behavior of
C_{i}(\kappa_{i})
at large
\kappa_{i}
for the
unit disk
{\Gamma}_{i}
(
a_{i}=1
). The numerical results (symbols)
computed from (
D.305
) are well-predicted by the asymptotic
formula (
D.322
) when
\kappa_{i}
is
large.

D.4
Alternative Representation and Zeros of the Function
C_{i}(\kappa_{i})

As discussed in §
7
, the leading-order term in the
asymptotic expansion of the SN problem is determined by
(
7.189
), which requires finding zeros of the sum of

C_{i}(-\sigma_{0})
. We now discuss the relation between the zeros of

C_{i}(-\sigma_{0})
and the spectrum of an additional local Steklov
eigenvalue problem (see also

[
56
]
).

For this purpose, we consider the following exterior Steklov
problem:

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\bf{y}}}\Psi_{ki}^{N}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf{y}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{3}\,,

(D.323a)

\displaystyle\partial_{n}\Psi_{ki}^{N}

\displaystyle=\mu_{ki}^{N}\Psi_{ki}^{N}\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\in{\Gamma}_{i}\,,

(D.323b)

\displaystyle\partial_{n}\Psi_{ki}^{N}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\notin{\Gamma}_{i}\,,

(D.323c)

\displaystyle|{\bf{y}}|^{2}\,|\nabla\Psi_{ki}^{N}({\bf{y}})|

\displaystyle\to 0\,,\quad\textrm{as}\quad|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty\,.

(D.323d)

This spectral problem, which was formulated in
[
68
]
,
admits infinitely many nontrivial solutions
\{\mu_{ki}^{N},\Psi_{ki}^{N}\}
,
enumerated by
k=0,1,\ldots
, such that the eigenvalues form an
ordered sequence,

0=\mu_{0i}^{N}<\mu_{1i}^{N}\leq\mu_{2i}^{N}\leq\ldots\nearrow+\infty,

(D.324)

whereas the restrictions
\Psi_{ki}^{N}|_{{\Gamma}_{i}}
onto
{\Gamma}_{i}
form a
complete orthonormal basis of
L^{2}({\Gamma}_{i})
. The spectral problem
differs from the former problem (
D.294
) by the imposed
behavior of eigenfunctions at infinity. In fact, while
(
D.294d
) can be interpreted as a Dirichlet condition at
infinity, (
D.323d
) implements a vanishing flow
condition, which is a sort of Neumann condition at infinity. The distinctions
between these two cases were investigated in a much more general
setting in
[
2
]
. It is trivial to check that a constant
function
\Psi_{0i}^{N}={1/\sqrt{|{\Gamma}_{i}|}}
is a solution of
(
D.323
), with the trivial eigenvalue
\mu_{0i}^{N}=0
.
As the other eigenfunctions
\Psi_{ki}^{N}
must be orthogonal to

\Psi_{0i}^{N}
in
L^{2}({\Gamma}_{i})
, one has
\int\nolimits_{{\Gamma}_{i}}\Psi_{ki}^{N}\,d{\bf{y}}=0
for any
k>0
. Note that the condition
(
D.323d
) does not require vanishing of
\Psi_{ki}^{N}
at
infinity, i.e.,
\Psi_{ki}^{N}
may have a constant non-zero limit as

|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty
. For a circular patch, the first eight eigenvalues

\mu_{ki}^{N}
corresponding to axially symmetric eigenfunctions, are
given in the last row of Table

3
(see
[
56
]
for the details on their
numerical computation).

The exterior Steklov problems (
D.294
) and
(
D.323
) provide complementary spectral tools for our
asymptotic analysis. It is therefore instructive to discuss several
relations between the eigenpairs of these problems. For some indices

k
and
k^{\prime}
, let us multiply (
D.294a
) by

\Psi_{k^{\prime}i}^{N}
, multiply (
D.323a
) with
k^{\prime}

by
\Psi_{ki}
, subtract these equations, integrate over a large
hemisphere, apply the Green’s formula, and use the boundary conditions
and the asymptotic behavior at infinity to get

\left(\mu_{ki}-\mu_{k^{\prime}i}^{N}\right)\int\limits_{{\Gamma}_{i}}\Psi_{ki}({\bf{y}})\Psi_{k^{\prime}i}^{N}({\bf{y}})\,d{\bf{y}}=\mu_{ki}d_{ki}\Psi_{k^{\prime}i}^{N}(\infty)\,.

(D.325)

This identity allows us to characterize the spectra of the two
problems. In (
7.182
), we introduced the resonant set

{\mathcal{P}}_{i}
as the union of all eigenvalues
\mu_{ki}
for which

d_{ki}\neq 0
:

{\mathcal{P}}_{i}\equiv\bigcup_{k=0}^{\infty}\left\{\mu_{ki}~|~d_{ki}\neq 0\right\}\,.

(D.326)

The union of the remaining eigenvalues is then denoted by

{\mathcal{P}}_{i}^{0}\equiv\bigcup_{k=0}^{\infty}\left\{\mu_{ki}~|~d_{ki}=0\right\}\,.

(D.327)

We emphasize that the intersection of these two sets is not
necessarily empty, i.e., there may exist indices
k\neq k^{\prime}

such that
\mu_{ki}=\mu_{k^{\prime}i}
,
d_{ki}\neq 0
and

d_{k^{\prime}i}=0
. In analogy, one can define two sets for the
spectral problem (
D.323
) by

\displaystyle{\mathcal{P}}_{i}^{N}

\displaystyle\equiv\bigcup_{k=0}^{\infty}\left\{\mu_{ki}^{N}~|~\Psi^{N}_{ki}(\infty)\neq 0\right\}\,,

(D.328a)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{P}}_{i}^{0,N}

\displaystyle\equiv\bigcup_{k=0}^{\infty}\left\{\mu_{ki}^{N}~|~\Psi^{N}_{ki}(\infty)=0\right\}\,.

(D.328b)

We now prove the following statement.

Lemma 6
.

One has

{\mathcal{P}}_{i}^{0}={\mathcal{P}}_{i}^{0,N}\,,\qquad{\mathcal{P}}_{i}\cap{\mathcal{P}}_{i}^{N}=\emptyset\,.

(D.329)

Proof.

Let us first prove that
{\mathcal{P}}_{i}^{0}\subset{\mathcal{P}}_{i}^{0,N}
in the first relation. If
\mu_{ki}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{i}^{0}
, the right-hand side of (
D.325
) is zero for all

k^{\prime}=0,1,\ldots
. Since the eigenfunctions

\{\Psi_{k^{\prime}i}^{N}\}
form a complete basis of
L^{2}({\Gamma}_{i})
, an
eigenfunction
\Psi_{ki}
cannot be orthogonal to all eigenfunctions

\Psi_{k^{\prime}i}^{N}
, implying that there exists an index

k^{\prime}
such that
\mu_{ki}=\mu_{k^{\prime}i}^{N}
. As a
consequence, the associated eigenfunction
\Psi_{ki}
satisfies both
(
D.294
) and (
D.323
) so that it must decay
faster than
{\mathcal{O}}\left(1/|{\bf{y}}|\right)
, and thus

\Psi_{ki}(\infty)=0
. We conclude that
\mu_{ki}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{i}^{0,N}
. The opposite inclusion
{\mathcal{P}}_{i}^{0,N}\subset{\mathcal{P}}_{i}^{0}
in the first relation of (
D.329
) is proved
in the same way.

In turn, if
\mu_{ki}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{i}
and
\mu_{k^{\prime}i}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{i}^{N}
, the right-hand side of (
D.325
) is
nonzero, implying that
\mu_{ki}\neq\mu_{k^{\prime}i}
and that the
eigenfunctions
\Psi_{ki}
and
\Psi_{k^{\prime}i}^{N}
are not
orthogonal to each other. This proves the second relation in
(
D.329
).

Another practical consequence of the identity (
D.325
) is the
possibility to re-expand an eigenfunction from one basis on the
eigenfunctions from the other basis. More precisely, if
\mu_{ki}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{i}\backslash{\mathcal{P}}_{i}^{0}
, then

\Psi_{ki}({\bf{y}})=\sum\limits_{k^{\prime}=0}^{\infty}(\Psi_{ki},\Psi_{k^{\prime}i}^{N})_{L^{2}({\Gamma}_{i})}\Psi_{k^{\prime}i}^{N}({\bf{y}})=\mu_{ki}d_{ki}\sum\limits_{k^{\prime}=0}^{\infty}\frac{\Psi_{k^{\prime}i}^{N}(\infty)}{\mu_{ki}-\mu_{k^{\prime}i}^{N}}\Psi_{k^{\prime}i}^{N}({\bf{y}})\,,\quad{\bf{y}}\in{\Gamma}_{i}\,;

(D.330)

similarly, if
\mu_{k^{\prime}i}^{N}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{i}^{N}\backslash{\mathcal{P}}_{i}^{0}
, then

\Psi_{k^{\prime}i}^{N}({\bf{y}})=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}(\Psi_{k^{\prime}i}^{N},\Psi_{ki})_{L^{2}({\Gamma}_{i})}\Psi_{ki}({\bf{y}})=\Psi_{k^{\prime}i}^{N}(\infty)\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\mu_{ki}d_{ki}}{\mu_{ki}-\mu_{k^{\prime}i}^{N}}\Psi_{ki}({\bf{y}})\,,\quad{\bf{y}}\in{\Gamma}_{i}\,.

(D.331)

The complementary nature of the Steklov problems (
D.294
)
and (
D.323
) suggest that the reactive capacitance can
actually be expressed in terms of the eigenpairs
\{\mu_{ki}^{N},\Psi_{ki}^{N}\}
, as shown in the following lemma.

Lemma 7
.

For any
\kappa_{i}\notin{\mathcal{P}}_{i}^{N}
, one has

\frac{1}{C_{i}(\kappa_{i})}=\frac{1}{C_{i}(\infty)}+2\pi\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{[\Psi_{ki}^{N}(\infty)]^{2}}{\mu_{ki}^{N}+\kappa_{i}}\,.

(D.332)

Proof.

To prove (
D.332
), we consider an auxiliary function

\tilde{w}_{i}({\bf{y}};\kappa)=\frac{w_{i}({\bf{y}};\infty)}{C_{i}(\infty)}-\frac{w_{i}({\bf{y}};\kappa)}{C_{i}(\kappa)}\,,

(D.333)

which, by construction and via (
3.17
), satisfies

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\bf{y}}}\tilde{w}_{i}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf{y}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{3}\,,

(D.334a)

\displaystyle\partial_{n}\tilde{w}_{i}+\kappa\tilde{w}_{i}

\displaystyle=\kappa\biggl(\frac{1}{C_{i}(\infty)}-\frac{1}{C_{i}(\kappa)}\biggr)+\frac{\partial_{n}w_{i}({\bf{y}};\infty)}{C_{i}(\infty)}\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\in{\Gamma}_{i}\,,

(D.334b)

\displaystyle\partial_{n}\tilde{w}_{i}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\notin{\Gamma}_{i}\,,

(D.334c)

\displaystyle\tilde{w}_{i}({\bf{y}})

\displaystyle\sim o(1/|{\bf{y}}|)\,,\quad\textrm{as}\quad|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty\,.

(D.334d)

Since the decay of this function at infinity does not include the
monopole term
1/|{\bf{y}}|
, it can decomposed onto the Steklov
eigenfunctions
\{\Psi_{ki}^{N}\}
, with the coefficients obtained from
the boundary condition:

\tilde{w}_{i}({\bf{y}};\kappa)=\frac{1}{C_{i}(\infty)}-\frac{1}{C_{i}(\kappa)}+\frac{1}{C_{i}(\infty)}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\bigl(\partial_{n}w_{i}({\bf{y}};\infty),\Psi_{ki}^{N}\bigr)_{L^{2}({\Gamma}_{i})}}{\mu_{ki}^{N}+\kappa}\Psi_{ki}^{N}({\bf{y}})\,,

(D.335)

where we used
\Psi_{0i}^{N}({\bf{y}})=1/\sqrt{|\Gamma_{i}|}
for the first two
terms. Multiplying (
D.323a
) by
w_{i}({\bf{y}};\infty)
, multiplying
(
3.17a
) with
\kappa_{i}=\infty
by
\Psi_{ki}^{N}({\bf{y}})
,
subtracting these equations, integrating them over a large hemisphere,
applying the Green’s formula, the boundary conditions and the decay at
infinity, we get

\int\limits_{{\Gamma}_{i}}\Psi_{ki}^{N}({\bf{y}})\,(\partial_{n}w_{i}({\bf{y}};\infty))d{\bf{y}}=2\pi C_{i}(\infty)\Psi_{ki}^{N}(\infty)\,,

(D.336)

that determines the scalar product in (
D.335
).
Finally, in the limit
|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty
, the left-hand side of
(
D.335
) vanishes, yielding the spectral expansion
(
D.332
).

While the original representation (
3.19
) allowed us to
get the upper bound (
D.306
), the alternative expansion
(
D.332
) gives access to lower bounds. For instance, one has

\frac{1}{C_{i}(\kappa_{i})}\leq\frac{1}{C_{i}(\infty)}+\frac{2\pi}{\kappa_{i}|{\Gamma}_{i}|}+2\pi\sum\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{[\Psi_{ki}^{N}(\infty)]^{2}}{\mu_{ki}^{N}}\,.

(D.337)

The last sum can computed by comparing the Taylor series of

1/C_{i}(\kappa_{i})
as
\kappa_{i}\to 0
with (
3.22a
), from
which

\frac{1}{C_{i}(\infty)}+2\pi\sum\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{[\Psi_{ki}^{N}(\infty)]^{2}}{\mu_{ki}^{N}}=\frac{4\pi^{2}a_{i}^{3}c_{2i}}{|{\Gamma}_{i}|^{2}}\,,

(D.338)

where the coefficient
c_{2i}
is given by (
3.23
). We
conclude that

C_{i}(\kappa_{i})\geq\frac{\kappa_{i}|{\Gamma}_{i}|}{2\pi}\biggl(1+\frac{2\pi a_{i}^{3}c_{2i}}{|{\Gamma}_{i}|}\kappa_{i}\biggr)^{-1}\,.

(D.339)

We remark that if the last term in (
D.337
) is
neglected, we recover our sigmoidal approximation
(
3.26
). The smallness of this last term as compared to

1/C_{i}(\infty)
may explain the high accuracy of this approximation.

From the representation (
D.332
), it is clear that, when

\kappa_{i}
approaches
-\mu_{ki}^{N}
such that
\Psi_{ki}^{N}(\infty)\neq 0
, the right-hand side of (
D.332
) diverges, so that

C_{i}(-\kappa_{i})
vanishes. In other words, any element of
{\mathcal{P}}_{i}^{N}
is a zero of the function
C_{i}(-\kappa)
. In the following
lemma, we prove that all zeros of
C_{i}(-\kappa)
are in
{\mathcal{P}}_{i}^{N}
.

Lemma 8
.

Let
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
be the set of zeros of the function
C_{i}(-\mu)
,
and
\mathcal{P}_{i}^{N}
be the subset of eigenvalues
\mu_{ki}^{N}
such that

\Psi_{ki}^{N}(\infty)\neq 0
. Then
\mathcal{Z}_{0}=\mathcal{P}_{i}^{N}
.

Proof.

We first prove that
\mathcal{P}_{i}^{N}\subset\mathcal{Z}_{0}
.
This inclusion follows directly from (
D.332
) but we provide
an alternative argument here.

We recall from (
3.17
) that
C_{i}(-\mu)
is obtained from the
solution to

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\bf{y}}}w_{i}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf{y}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{3}\,,

(D.340a)

\displaystyle\partial_{n}w_{i}-\mu w_{i}

\displaystyle=-\mu\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\in{\Gamma}_{i}\,,

(D.340b)

\displaystyle\partial_{n}w_{i}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\notin{\Gamma}_{i}\,,

(D.340c)

\displaystyle w_{i}

\displaystyle\sim\frac{C_{i}(-\mu)}{|{\bf{y}}|}+{\mathcal{O}}(|{\bf{y}}|^{-2})\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty\,.

(D.340d)

By applying Green’s second identity to
w_{i}
and
\Psi_{ki}^{N}
,
which satisfies (
D.323
), over a large hemisphere of
radius
R
in the upper half-plane we pass to the limit
R\to\infty

to obtain

\begin{split}0=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{3}}\left(w_{i}\,\Delta_{{\bf{y}}}\Psi_{ki}^{N}-\Psi_{ki}^{N}\,\Delta_{{\bf{y}}}w_{i}\right)\,d{\bf{y}}&=\int_{{\Gamma}_{i}}\left(w_{i}\,\partial_{n}\Psi_{ki}^{N}-\Psi_{ki}^{N}\,\partial_{n}w_{i}\right)\,d{\bf{y}}\\
&\qquad+2\pi\lim_{R\to\infty}R^{2}\left(w_{i}\frac{\partial\Psi_{ki}^{N}}{\partial|{\bf{y}}|}-\Psi_{ki}^{N}\frac{\partial w_{i}}{\partial|{\bf{y}}|}\right)\Big|_{|{\bf{y}}|=R}\,.\end{split}

(D.341)

Then, upon using the Steklov conditions (
D.323b
) and
(
D.340b
) on
{\Gamma}_{i}
, together with the far-field
behaviors (
D.323d
) and (
D.340d
) for

\Psi_{ki}^{N}
and
w_{i}
, we find that (
D.341
) reduces to

\left(\mu_{ki}^{N}-\mu\right)\int_{{\Gamma}_{i}}w_{i}\Psi_{ki}^{N}\,d{\bf{y}}+\mu\int_{{\Gamma}_{i}}\Psi_{ki}^{N}\,dy+2\pi\Psi_{ki}^{N}(\infty)C_{i}(-\mu)=0\,.

(D.342)

Owing to the decay behavior (
D.323d
), we obtain from the
divergence theorem that for
k>0
, for which
\mu_{ki}^{N}>0
, we have

\int_{{\Gamma}_{i}}\Psi_{ki}^{N}\,d{\bf{y}}=\left(\mu_{ki}^{N}\right)^{-1}\int_{{\Gamma}_{i}}\partial_{n}\Psi_{ki}^{N}\,d{\bf{y}}=0
. As a result,
(
D.342
) simplifies to

\left(\mu_{ki}^{N}-\mu\right)\int_{{\Gamma}}w_{i}\Psi_{ki}^{N}\,d{\bf{y}}=-2\pi\Psi_{ki}^{N}(\infty)C_{i}(-\mu)\,.

(D.343)

We conclude that if an eigenfunction
\Psi_{ki}^{N}
does not vanish at
infinity, then the function
C_{i}(-\mu)
must vanish at
\mu=\mu_{ki}^{N}
. By its definition (
3.19
),
C_{i}(-\mu)
also
vanishes at
\mu=\mu_{0i}^{N}=0
. This proves that
\mathcal{P}_{i}^{N}\subset\mathcal{Z}_{0}
.

Let us now prove the opposite inclusion

\mathcal{Z}_{0}\subset\mathcal{P}_{i}^{N}
, i.e., there is no other zero of

C_{i}(-\mu)
than those determined by the eigenvalues
\mu_{ki}^{N}
.
Assume that there exists
\mu^{\prime}>0
such that

-\mu^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}
but

-\mu^{\prime}\notin\mathcal{P}_{i}^{N}
. Since the basis of
eigenfunctions
\Psi_{ki}^{N}
is complete in
L^{2}({\Gamma}_{i})
, there is a
unique solution to the inhomogeneous problem

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\bf{y}}}U

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\bf{y}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{3}\,,

(D.344a)

\displaystyle\partial_{n}U-\mu^{\prime}U

\displaystyle=f\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\in{\Gamma}_{i}\,,

(D.344b)

\displaystyle\partial_{n}U

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad y_{3}=0\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\notin{\Gamma}_{i}\,,

(D.344c)

\displaystyle|{\bf{y}}|^{2}\,|\nabla U({\bf{y}})|

\displaystyle\to 0\,,\quad\textrm{as}\quad|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty\,,

(D.344d)

where we set
f=(\Psi_{0i})|_{{\Gamma}}
. The divergence theorem implies

0=\int_{{\Gamma}_{i}}\partial_{n}U\,d{\bf{y}}=\mu^{\prime}\int_{{\Gamma}_{i}}U\,d{\bf{y}}+d_{0i}\,,

(D.345)

where
d_{0i}
is given by (
D.301
).
On one hand, upon multiplication of (
D.309
) by
U
and
integration over
{\Gamma}_{i}
, while using (
D.345
), we obtain

-\frac{d_{0i}}{\mu^{\prime}}=\int_{{\Gamma}}U\,d{\bf{y}}=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}d_{ki}\int_{{\Gamma}_{i}}U\Psi_{ki}\,d{\bf{y}}\,.

(D.346)

On the other hand, upon applying Green’s second identity to
U
and

\Psi_{ki}
over a large hemisphere in the upper half-plane and
passing to the limit we obtain

0=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{3}}\left(\Psi_{ki}\Delta_{{\bf{y}}}U-U\Delta_{{\bf{y}}}\Psi_{ki}\right)\,d{\bf{y}}=\mu_{ki}d_{ki}U(\infty)+(\mu^{\prime}-\mu_{ki})\int_{{\Gamma}_{i}}U\Psi_{ki}\,d{\bf{y}}+\delta_{0,k}\,,

(D.347)

due to the orthonormality of
\Psi_{ki}
to
\Psi_{0i}
. Upon solving
(
D.347
) for
\int_{{\Gamma}_{i}}U\Psi_{ki}\,d{\bf{y}}
, we obtain
from (
D.345
) and the Steklov eigenfunction expansion of

C_{i}(\mu)
in (
D.305
), that

-d_{0i}=\mu^{\prime}\biggl[\frac{d_{0i}}{\mu_{0i}-\mu^{\prime}}+U(\infty)\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\mu_{ki}d_{ki}^{2}}{\mu_{ki}-\mu^{\prime}}\biggr]=\frac{d_{0i}\mu^{\prime}}{\mu_{0i}-\mu^{\prime}}-2\pi U(\infty)C_{i}(-\mu^{\prime})\,.

(D.348)

Since
\mu^{\prime}
was assumed to be a zero of
C_{i}(-\mu)
, we
conclude that
-d_{0i}=d_{0i}\mu^{\prime}/(\mu_{0i}-\mu^{\prime})
.
Given that
d_{0i}\neq 0
, this yields that
\mu_{0i}=0
, which
contradicts the strict positivity of the principal eigenvalue

\mu_{0i}
. We conclude that the second inclusion
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\subset\mathcal{P}_{i}^{N}
must also hold. Therefore,
\mathcal{Z}_{0}=\mathcal{P}_{i}^{N}
.

D.5
Relation to Dirichlet-to-Neumann operators

The dual character of the Steklov problems (
D.294
) and
(
D.323
) can be further understood from the tight relation
between the associated Dirichlet-to-Neumann operators
{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
and

{\mathcal{D}}_{i}^{N}
. The operator
{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
associates to a function
f\in H^{1/2}({\Gamma}_{i})
on the patch
{\Gamma}_{i}
another function
g={\mathcal{D}}_{i}f=(\partial_{n}u)|_{{\Gamma}_{i}}\in H^{-1/2}({\Gamma}_{i})
on the same patch, where

u
is the unique solution of the BVP

\displaystyle\Delta u=0

\displaystyle\quad\textrm{in}~{\mathbb{R}}^{3}_{+}\,,\qquad u=f\quad\textrm{on}~y_{3}=0,~(y_{1},y_{2})\in{\Gamma}_{i}\,,

(D.349a)

\displaystyle\partial_{n}u=0

\displaystyle\quad\textrm{on}~y_{3}=0,~(y_{1},y_{2})\notin{\Gamma}_{i}\,,\qquad u\to 0\quad\textrm{as}~|{\bf{y}}|\to\infty\,.

(D.349b)

The operator
{\mathcal{D}}_{i}^{N}
acts similarly, i.e.,
g={\mathcal{D}}_{i}^{N}f=(\partial_{n}u^{N})|_{{\Gamma}_{i}}
, where
u^{N}
satisfies the similar BVP,
except for the asymptotic decay
|{\bf{y}}|^{2}|\nabla u^{N}|\to 0
at
infinity. The spectral properties of the operators
{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
and

{\mathcal{D}}_{i}^{N}
were investigated in a more general setting in

[
2
,
19
]
. It is easy to check that
\mu_{ki}
and

\Psi_{ki}|_{{\Gamma}_{i}}
are the eigenpairs of
{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
, whereas

\mu_{ki}^{N}
and
\Psi_{ki}^{N}|_{{\Gamma}_{i}}
are the eigenpairs of

{\mathcal{D}}_{i}^{N}
. Moreover, Theorem 5.9 from
[
2
]
states that
these two operators differ by a rank-one perturbation:

{\mathcal{D}}_{i}f={\mathcal{D}}_{i}^{N}f+\frac{1}{\beta_{i}}(f,\varphi_{i})_{L^{2}({\Gamma}_{i})}\varphi_{i}\,,\qquad\textrm{where}~\varphi_{i}={\mathcal{D}}_{i}1\quad\textrm{and}\quad\beta_{i}=(1,\varphi_{i})_{L^{2}({\Gamma}_{i})}

(D.350)

(even though this statement was proved for a slightly different
setting of exterior problems in the whole space, the arguments seem to
straightforwardly apply to our case, see further discussion in

[
19
]
).
Since (
D.349
) with
f=1
is identical to (
3.17
)
with
\kappa_{i}=\infty
, one gets

\varphi_{i}={\mathcal{D}}_{i}1=(\partial_{n}w_{i}({\bf{y}};\infty))|_{{\Gamma}_{i}}\,,\qquad\beta_{i}=2\pi C_{i}(\infty)\,,

(D.351)

where the second relation follows from the divergence theorem.

Many former relations can be rapidly recovered by using the operators

{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
and
{\mathcal{D}}_{i}^{N}
. To illustrate this point, we first note that,
according to (
D.297
),
1/(2\pi|{\bf{x}}-{\bf{y}}|)
is the kernel
of the inverse of
{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
so that the function
\omega_{i}
, defined in
(
3.24
), can be formally written as
\omega_{i}={\mathcal{D}}_{i}^{-1}1
(the operator
{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
is invertible because all its
eigenvalues are strictly positive). The projections of this relation
onto a constant function or onto itself yield immediately that

\displaystyle\int\limits_{{\Gamma}_{i}}\omega_{i}({\bf{y}})d{\bf{y}}

\displaystyle=(1,{\mathcal{D}}_{i}^{-1}1)_{L^{2}({\Gamma}_{i})}=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{d_{ki}^{2}}{\mu_{ki}}\,,

(D.352a)

\displaystyle\int\limits_{{\Gamma}_{i}}[\omega_{i}({\bf{y}})]^{2}d{\bf{y}}

\displaystyle=({\mathcal{D}}_{i}^{-1}1,{\mathcal{D}}_{i}^{-1}1)_{L^{2}({\Gamma}_{i})}=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{d_{ki}^{2}}{\mu_{ki}^{2}}\,,

(D.352b)

from which follows the representations (
3.23
) for the
coefficients
c_{2i}
and
c_{3i}
.

Appendix E
Computation and Analysis of the Monopole Term
E_{i}

We aim at computing numerically the coefficient
E_{i}
given by
(
3.38
) for the circular patch
{\Gamma}_{i}
of radius
a_{i}
.
Upon changing the integration variables, we represent it as

E_{i}(\kappa_{i})=-\frac{C_{i}^{2}(\kappa_{i})}{2}\log a_{i}+a_{i}^{2}{\mathcal{E}}_{i}(\kappa_{i}a_{i})\,,

(E.353)

where

{\mathcal{E}}_{i}(\mu)=2\int_{0}^{1}\frac{1}{r}\biggl(\int_{0}^{r}r^{\prime}a_{i}q_{i}(a_{i}r^{\prime};\mu/a_{i})\,dr^{\prime}\biggr)^{2}\,dr

(E.354)

now corresponds to the unit disk. By recalling the limiting
asymptotics in (
3.40
), we observe that

{\mathcal{E}}_{i}(\infty)=(3-4\log 2)/\pi^{2}
and that

{\mathcal{E}}_{i}(\mu)\approx\mu^{2}/32
as
\mu\to 0
.

In order to compute the integral in (
E.354
), one can employ
the spectral representation (
D.303
) of the density

q_{i}({\bf{y}};\kappa_{i})
. The presence of the factor
\mu_{ki}
in the
numerator of (
D.303
) deteriorates the numerical convergence of
the spectral expansion, thus requiring higher truncation
orders. Therefore, it is convenient to use the identity
(
D.309
) to represent this function as

q_{i}({\bf{y}};\kappa_{i})=\frac{\kappa_{i}}{2}\biggl(1-\kappa_{i}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{d_{ki}\,\Psi_{ki}({\bf{y}})}{\mu_{ki}+\kappa_{i}}\biggr)\,,\quad\mbox{for}\quad{\bf{y}}\in{\Gamma}_{i}\,,

(E.355)

which exhibits a faster convergence. Repeating this trick, we get an
even faster converging representation for
{\bf{y}}\in{\Gamma}_{i}
given by

q_{i}({\bf{y}};\kappa_{i})=\frac{\kappa_{i}}{2}\biggl(1-\kappa_{i}\biggl[\frac{2a_{i}}{\pi}E(|{\bf{y}}|/a_{i})-\kappa_{i}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{d_{ki}\,\Psi_{ki}({\bf{y}})}{\mu_{ki}(\mu_{ki}+\kappa_{i})}\biggr]\biggr)\,,\quad\mbox{for}\quad{\bf{y}}\in{\Gamma}_{i}\,,

(E.356)

where
E(z)
is the complete elliptic integral of the second kind.

Figure
11(a)
illustrates the behavior of the density
q_{i}

versus
r
for the unit disk (
a_{i}=1
). As expected, this density
approaches its limiting form
q_{i}({\bf{y}};\infty)
as
\kappa_{i}\to\infty
. Curiously, for a fixed
r
,
q_{i}
is not a monotone
increasing function of
\kappa_{i}
, as illustrated in
Fig.
11(b)
. Finally, we highlight that even the use of
large truncation orders does not fully resolve the issue of the
numerical accuracy at large
\kappa_{i}
, especially for small
r
.

(a)

q_{i}(r;\kappa_{i})
versus
r
for three
\kappa_{i}

(b)

q_{i}(r;\kappa_{i})
versus
\kappa_{i}
for three
r

Figure 11
:

(a):
The density
q_{i}(r;\kappa_{i})
for the unit disk
(
a_{i}=1
) as a function of
r
, for different values of
\kappa_{i}
,
as indicated in the legend. The three curves were obtained by
truncating the series in (
E.355
) at
k\leq 500
. The
black dashed curve presents the exact expression
(
3.28
).
(b):
The density
q_{i}(r;\kappa_{i})
as a
function of
\kappa_{i}
, for three fixed
r
. Here we used the
truncation up to
1000
terms; nevertheless, the obtained
q_{i}

at
r=0
exhibits erroneous behavior at large
\kappa_{i}
, since it does
not approach its limit
1/(\pi\sqrt{1-r^{2}})
, as indicated by
dashed horizontal line.

In Fig.
12
, we plot the numerically computed
E_{i}(\kappa_{i})={\mathcal{E}}_{i}(\kappa_{i})
versus
\kappa_{i}
for
\kappa_{i}<0
for the
unit disk (
a_{i}=1
). In computing
{\mathcal{E}}_{i}
from
(
E.354
), we numerically evaluated the integral in
(
E.354
) with the discretization step
\delta r=10^{-4}
,
where
q_{i}
was estimated from the series (
E.355
) truncated to
either
200
terms (crosses) or to
1000
terms (line). An excellent
agreement between these two results confirms the accuracy of our
numerical computation. Despite the vertical asymptotes (the poles of

C_{i}(\kappa_{i})
), the function
E_{i}(\kappa_{i})
remains always
positive.

In turn, Fig.
4
shows the dependence of the coefficient

E_{i}(\kappa_{i})
on
\kappa_{i}
for
\kappa_{i}>0
. In contrast to that
for
C_{i}(\kappa_{i})
, this dependence is not monotonous. We expect
that this is a result of the non-monotone approach of

q_{i}({\bf{y}};\kappa_{i})
to
q_{i}({\bf{y}};\infty)
, as discussed earlier. It is
also worth noting that the numerical computation for large
\kappa_{i}

requires large truncation orders; in fact, the truncation order
100

was not sufficient for
\kappa_{i}\geq 100
(two curves start to
deviate from each other). Even the large truncation order
500

becomes insufficient for
\kappa_{i}\geq 1000
(not shown). To
overcome this difficulty, we propose below a simple empirical formula
(
E.358
) for
E_{i}(\kappa_{i})
that closely predicts the
numerically computed value on the entire range
\kappa_{i}>0
. The
remarkable accuracy of this simple approximation was illustrated in
Fig.
4
.

Figure 12
:

The numerically computed
E_{i}(\kappa_{i})
for
\kappa_{i}<0
for a
circular patch
{\Gamma}_{i}
with
a_{i}=1
. Vertical dashed lines indicate
three poles
\{-\mu_{ki}\}
of
C_{i}(\kappa_{i})
, which are also the
poles of
q_{i}
and thus of
E_{i}
. Crosses correspond to the
truncation order
k_{\rm max}=200
, whereas line presents the result
for
k_{\rm max}=1000
.

Empirical Approximation of
E
for the Unit Disk

The non-monotonic behavior of
E_{i}(\kappa_{i})
makes its approximation
trickier. For this reason, we consider the ratio

E_{i}(\kappa_{i})/C_{i}^{2}(\kappa_{i})
, which turns out to be a monotone
decreasing function of
\kappa_{i}
that ranges from
1/8
at
\kappa_{i}=0
to
3/4-\log 2
as
\kappa_{i}\to\infty
. This behavior
suggests to approximate it for the unit disk as

\frac{E_{i}(\kappa)}{C_{i}^{2}(\kappa)}\approx\frac{3}{4}-\log{2}+\frac{1}{\frac{1}{\log{2}-5/8}+f(\kappa)}\,,

(E.357)

where
f(\kappa)
is a suitable increasing function of
\kappa
(such
that
f(0)=0
and
f(\infty)=\infty
). Choosing
f(\kappa)=3.04\,\kappa^{0.88}
, one can make this approximation accurate, but
it still requires the computation of
C_{i}(\kappa)
via its spectral
representation (
D.305
). Applying the empirical approximation

C^{\rm app}(\kappa)=2\kappa/(\pi\kappa+4)
from
(
3.31
) for
C_{i}(\kappa)
, we get a fully explicit
empirical approximation on
\kappa>0
for the unit disk:

E^{\rm app}(\kappa)=\frac{4\kappa^{2}}{(\pi\kappa+4)^{2}}\biggl(\frac{3}{4}-\log{2}+\frac{1}{\frac{1}{\log{2}-5/8}+5.17\,\kappa^{0.81}}\biggr)\,,

(E.358)

where we adjusted the function
f(\kappa)
to ensure higher accuracy.
The closeness of this approximation was shown in
Fig.
4
.

Appendix F
Improved Numerics for the SN Problem

In this appendix, we devise an accurate numerical method to
treat the SN problem (
2.14
) for the unit sphere with
either one circular patch, or two circular patches centered at the
north and south poles. To do so, we employ a general expansion of an
axially symmetric harmonic function
U
in spherical coordinates in
terms of Legendre polynomials
P_{n}(z)
, given by

V(r,\theta)=\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}c_{n}r^{n}P_{n}(\cos\theta)\,.

(F.359)

The unknown coefficients
c_{n}
are determined from the mixed boundary
condition:

\partial_{n}V=\mu I_{\Omega_{a}}V,

(F.360)

where
I_{\Omega_{a}}
is the indicator function of the patches

\Omega_{a}
. The substitution of the expansion
(
F.359
) yields

\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}c_{n}nP_{n}(\cos\theta)=\mu\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}c_{n}P_{n}(\cos\theta)I_{\Omega_{a}}(\theta)\,.

(F.361)

Multiplying this equation by
P_{m}(\cos\theta)\sin\theta
and
integrating over
\theta\in(0,\pi)
, we get a system of linear
equations

mc_{m}=\mu\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}K_{m,n}c_{n}\,,\qquad K_{m,n}=(m+1/2)\int\limits_{0}^{\pi}\sin\theta\,P_{m}(\cos\theta)P_{n}(\cos\theta)I_{\Omega_{a}}(\theta)d\theta\,.

(F.362)

As the left-hand side vanishes at
m=0
, it is convenient to isolate
the coefficient
c_{0}
from this relation at
m=0
as

c_{0}=-\frac{1}{K_{00}}\sum\limits_{n=1}^{\infty}K_{0,n}c_{n}\,.

(F.363)

We substitute it into the above system to get

mc_{m}=\mu\biggl(-\frac{K_{m,0}}{K_{0,0}}\sum\limits_{n=1}^{\infty}K_{0,n}c_{n}+\sum\limits_{n=1}^{\infty}K_{m,n}c_{n}\biggr)\quad\mbox{for}\quad m=1,2,\ldots\,.

(F.364)

This system of linear equations can be written in a matrix form as

{\bf M}{\bf C}=\frac{1}{\mu}{\bf C}\,,

(F.365)

where
{\bf C}
is the vector of coefficients
c_{1},c_{2},\ldots
, and

{\bf M}_{m,n}=\frac{1}{m}\biggl(K_{m,n}-\frac{K_{m,0}K_{0,n}}{K_{0,0}}\biggr)\quad\mbox{for}\quad m,n=1,2,\ldots\,.

(F.366)

A numerical diagonalization of the truncated matrix
{\bf M}
allows
one to determine the eigenvalues
\mu
of the SN problem.

For a single patch of angle
{\varepsilon}_{1}
at the north pole, an explicit
representation of the elements
K_{m,n}
was given in
Appendix D.3 of
[
48
]
as

K_{m,n}^{(1)}({\varepsilon}_{1})=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\min\{m,n\}}B_{mn}^{k}\frac{P_{m+n-2k-1}(\cos{\varepsilon}_{1})-P_{m+n-2k+1}(\cos{\varepsilon}_{1})}{2(m+n-2k)+1}\,,

(F.367)

where

B_{mn}^{k}=\frac{A_{k}A_{m-k}A_{n-k}}{A_{m+n-k}}\,\frac{2m+2n-4k+1}{2m+2n-2k+1}\,,\quad A_{k}=\frac{\Gamma(k+1/2)}{\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(k+1)}\,,\quad A_{0}=1\,.

(F.368)

For a single patch of angle
{\varepsilon}_{2}
at the south pole, one can use
the symmetry of Legendre polynomials,
P_{n}(1-x)=(-1)^{n}P_{n}(x)
, to
get

K_{m,n}^{(2)}({\varepsilon}_{2})=(-1)^{m+n}K_{m,n}^{(1)}({\varepsilon}_{2})\,.

(F.369)

When there are two patches, the matrix element
K_{m,n}
is the
sum of these two contributions.

We emphasize that
V(r,\theta)
in (
F.359
) is
constructed to be axially symmetric (i.e., independent of the angle

\phi
). In other words, this numerical procedure gives access
exclusively to axially symmetric eigenfunctions of the SN problem. In
a similar way, one can construct non-axially-symmetric eigenfunctions
by using a representation in the form
e^{im\phi}r^{n}P_{n}^{m}(\cos\theta)
with associated Legendre polynomials
P_{n}^{m}(z)
,
see similar constructions in
[
55
,
56
]
.

Numerical Results for One Patch

For validation purpose, we consider the case of a single patch
of radius
{\varepsilon}
at the north pole. We compute the first SN
eigenvalue by truncating the matrix
{\bf M}
to the size
n_{\rm max}\times n_{\rm max}
. Our numerical results for the first eigenvalue,
labeled by
\sigma_{\rm num}^{(1)}
, for different
{\varepsilon}
and two
different truncations
n_{\rm max}
are shown in
Table
4
. From this table we observe that increasing

n_{\rm max}
by a factor of two does not significantly change our numerical
estimate for the first SN eigenvalue, which confirms the high accuracy
of our numerical method. The favorable comparison between the first
two numerically computed SN eigenvalues and their asymptotic
predictions in (
7.204a
) is shown in
Fig.
7
.

.
